\part{Preface}

This booklet is the result of many thoughtful hours spent in careful collaboration by the English teaching team, created with a single guiding aim: to equip you with the tools, knowledge, and inspiration to approach your final English examination with clarity, confidence, and purpose.\\

Far more than a simple collection of notes, this guide seeks to be your companion in these final weeks, an intellectual compass to help you navigate the intricate terrain of literary analysis, argument construction, and creative exploration. Within these pages you will find a carefully curated array of resources: from exemplar essays and annotated responses, to thematic quote banks and character breakdowns; from breakdowns of past assessments to guidance drawn from VCAA feedback, every element has been selected with your success in mind.\\

This is not a booklet to be read in one sitting, nor is it intended to be passively consumed from cover to cover. Rather, it is a reference to be dipped into with purpose, a treasury of ideas and strategies to consult in moments of uncertainty or reflection. Like a well-thumbed dictionary or a trusted atlas, it offers direction precisely when you need it most.\\

The road to the English examination is often a challenging one, strewn with doubts, decisions, and the pressure of time. Yet it is also a journey rich in discovery, a final opportunity to let your voice be heard, your thinking be sharpened, and your understanding of the texts you’ve studied take flight. Whether you are refining your essay structure, revising key quotes, or contemplating the deeper implications of authorial intent, may this guide be a source of insight and encouragement.\\

And remember that your teachers are here to support you every step of the way. They know your strengths, understand your learning, and believe in your potential. Should you require further guidance or simply a reassuring word, do not hesitate to reach out.\\

On behalf of the English team, we extend to you our best wishes. May your hard work bear fruit, your confidence grow, and your skills shine clearly and proudly in the exam room.\\


 - The VCE English Team\\
 
Mr. Morlin, Ms. Levin, Ms. Hoernel and Ms. Zhiv.

\section{Practice Analytical Text Response Topics}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Practice Analytical Text Response Topics}

1. 'Maybe that’s why it’s good, it’s true” \underline{Sunset Boulevard} shows that the truth, however ordinary or painful, is what matters. Do you agree?\\

2. In Billy Wilder’s \underline{Sunset Boulevard} things are never what they seem. Discuss.\\

3. Wilder’s \underline{Sunset Boulevard} opposes exploitation in all its forms. Discuss.\\

4. \underline{Sunset Boulevard} explores different kinds of madness. Discuss.\\

5. 'There’s nothing tragic about being 50, not unless you try to be 25”. \underline{Sunset Boulevard} is about the need to face reality. Do you agree?\\

6. In \underline{Sunset Boulevard} fantasy prevents the truth from being confronted. To what extent do you agree?\\

7. 'Sagittarius! I like Sagittarians - you can trust them” \underline{Sunset Boulevard} warns against trusting others. Discuss.\\

8. 'I felt trapped like the cigarette in the contraption on her finger”. \underline{Sunset Boulevard} is concerned with entrapment. Do you agree?\\

9. \underline{Sunset Boulevard} explores the intermingling of public and private realms. Discuss.\\

10. Billy Wilder’s \underline{Sunset Boulevard} explores the intersection of the written and the real. Discuss.\\

11. \underline{Sunset Boulevard} touches on the true horrors of Hollywood. Do you agree?\\

12. The structure of \underline{Sunset Boulevard} is crucial to its message. Discuss.\\

13. \underline{Sunset Boulevard} is not just a sad tale of deception, greed, and jealousy. Discuss.\\

14. 'I am big, it's the pictures that got small' \underline{Sunset Boulevard} shows that mistaken beliefs are responsible for suffering. Do you agree?\\

15. \underline{Sunset Boulevard} argues that we have little control over our lives. To what extent do you agree?\\

16. 'But before you hear it all distorted and blown out of proportion, before those Hollywood columnists get their hands on it, maybe you'd like to hear the facts, the whole truth”. In \underline{Sunset Boulevard} truth is the real victim. Discuss.\\

17. \underline{Sunset Boulevard} reveals Hollywood as a world of predation and exploitation. Do you agree?\\

18. \underline{Sunset Boulevard} explores the consequences of people being in the wrong place or time. Discuss.\\

19. Billy Wilder’s \underline{Sunset Boulevard} is a contest between selfishness and self-sacrifice. Discuss.\\

20. Norma and Joe are very similar people. To what extend Do you agree?\\

21. How does Wilder create suspense for the audience in \underline{Sunset Boulevard}?\\

22. “I remember waving to you. I was dancing on a table.” \underline{Sunset Boulevard} shows the lengths people will go to in order to keep the past alive. Discuss.\\

23. “Look sweetie, be practical.” Joe’s pursuit of fame is a symptom of the selfish society he inhabits. To what extent do you agree?\\

24. How does Wilder’s choice of a noir thriller position viewers to feel uncomfortable in \underline{Sunset Boulevard}?\\

25. “That’s the trouble with you readers, you know all the plots.” ‘In the world of \underline{Sunset Boulevard}, it is impossible to experience anything true.’ Do you agree?\\

26. ‘In \underline{Sunset Boulevard}, the magic of the movies carries also the potential for disenchantment.’ Discuss.\\

27. ‘Joe’s death is the result of his failure to see the tragic nature of his relationship with Norma.’ Do you agree?\\

28. ‘\underline{Sunset Boulevard} represents a condemnation of Hollywood’s idea of itself as the dream factory.’ To what extent do you agree?\\

29. ‘\underline{Sunset Boulevard} exposes the less than benevolent treatment of creative talent in the pursuit of profit.’ Discuss.\\

30. Wilder’s film is a scathing critique of the Hollywood film industry. To what extent do you agree?\\

31. How does Wilder use Franz Waxman’s score to intensify the events of the film?\\

32. Joe Gillis was complicit in all the events of \underline{Sunset Boulevard} and deserves no sympathy. Discuss.\\

33. Norma Desmond is a tragic victim of a cruel industry. Discuss.\\

34. How does Wilder’s use of a voiceover contribute to the menacing nature of the film?\\

35. “I am big. It’s the pictures that got small.” \underline{Sunset Boulevard} is an examination of the dangers of pride. To what extent do you agree?\\

36. Norma Desmond’s delusions are fuelled and supported by men hoping to profit from her fame and fortune. Discuss.\\

37. In Wilder’s \underline{Sunset Boulevard}, every character is selfish and opportunistic. To what extent do you agree?\\

38. How does Wilder capture the power of delusion in his film \underline{Sunset Boulevard}?\\

39. “DeMille didn’t have the heart to tell you. None of us had the heart.” How does deception contribute to the tragic events of \underline{Sunset Boulevard}?\\



40. What film techniques has Billy Wilder used to further his themes and main messages?\\

41. How does Billy Wilder use film techniques to further his main messages in Sunset Boulevard?\\

42. What role does self-interested thinking play in Sunset Boulevard?\\

43. How does Billy Wilder use film techniques to further his message that isolation can be a dangerous thing?\\

44. What narrative devices does Wilder use to further his main messages?\\

45. 'One of Wilder's significant themes is that living in one's own created world can have its advantages.' To what extent do you agree?\\

46. 'Wilder uses his film to criticise a society that is in love with fame.' Discuss.\\

47. How does Wilder use his film to criticise modern social foibles?\\

48. 'The screenwriter and director of Sunset Boulevard, Billy Wilder, leaves his audience feeling disappointed, yet hopeful.' To what extent do you agree?\\

49. How does Wilder use his film text to explore the problem of excessive self-interest?\\

50. 'In the film Sunset Boulevard, the crucial conflict is between reality and ambition.' Discuss.\\

51. How does the structure of this film influence the reader's response to Joe Gillis?\\

52. 'In Norma Desmond, Billy Wilder has created a character who doesn't need anyone?' To what extent do you agree?\\

53. To what extent does Wilder use his film text to criticise modern society?\\

54. How does Wilder use his film to explore the problems caused by excessive pride?\\

55. 'From her Sunset Boulevard mansion, Norma Desmond imposes her view of the world upon others.' To what extent do you agree?\\

56. How does Wilder construct his film to further his significant themes?\\

57. What lessons does the director of Sunset Boulevard have about the way fame should be regarded?\\

58. 'In his film, Wilder points an accusing finger at the weaknesses of human behaviour.' Do you agree?\\

59. How does Wilder's directing style influence the audience's response?\\

60. 'Joe Gillis's view of the world is changed by his interaction with Norma Desmond.' Discuss.\\

61. How does Wilder represent Norma Desmond as a way to further his main messages?\\

62. Discuss the role of morality in Sunset Boulevard.\\

63. How does Wilder represent Joe Gillis as a way to further his theme of the value of autonomy?\\

64. 'The life choices of every character in Sunset Boulevard are flawed.' Discuss.\\

65. How does Wilder utilise setting to portray the world as an imperfect realm?\\

66. 'Despite appearances, in Sunset Boulevard nothing is easy.' Discuss.\\

67. Who is the main victim in Sunset Boulevard?\\

68. How does Wilder represent minor characters as a way to further his main messages?\\

69. 'A central message of Sunset Boulevard is to do with the stories we tell.' To what extent do you agree?\\

70. 'The most significant motif of Wilder's film is to do with the importance of connection.' Discuss.\\

71. How does Wilder represent minor characters as a way to further his theme of the dangers of deceit?\\

72. To what extent does Wilder suggest that there are benefits in believing in yourself?\\

73. How does Wilder attempt to convince his audience of the evils of self-adulation?\\

74. In Wilder's film, Sunset Boulevard, what is considered to be essential?\\

75. How does Wilder use symbolism to further his main messages?\\

76. 'Sunset Boulevard shows the importance of trying to see the world more clearly, even though this can come at great personal cost.' Discuss.\\

77. 'The greatest crime depicted in this film is to not pay enough attention.' Discuss.\\

14. ‘In this film, the distinctions between fantasy and reality are blurred.’\\ Discuss.\\

15. ‘Max’s actions are to blame for Norma’s dangerous detachment from reality.’\\ Do you agree?\\

17. ‘Norma’s obsession with her own celebrity status is detrimental to herself and others.’\\ Discuss.\\

18. ‘Relationships in Sunset Boulevard are not completely devoid of love.’\\ To what extent do you agree?\\\

20. ‘There is nothing more to Norma than her superficial appearance.’\\ To what extent do you agree?\\




\newpage
\section{Background and Context}
\subsection{Overview}
Understanding the historical and cinematic context of \underline{Sunset Boulevard} is essential to appreciating its form, themes, and tone. Billy Wilder’s 1950 film emerges from a period of immense transformation in Hollywood — from the decline of the silent era to the rise of the studio system and the eventual disillusionment of post-war America. The film reflects both the glamour and the decay of the entertainment industry, satirising its obsession with fame, morality, and illusion.\\

\subsection{The Silent Film Era}
From the early 1910s until the late 1920s, cinema existed largely without synchronised sound. The silent era represented the height of early film artistry, when narrative was conveyed through title cards, exaggerated gestures, and musical accompaniment. Although these films demonstrated remarkable creativity, the arrival of sound — the so-called “talkies” — revolutionised the industry.\\

Many silent stars found their careers abruptly curtailed as they struggled to adapt to dialogue-driven storytelling. This shift from visual expressiveness to vocal performance exposed the fragility of celebrity, a theme later echoed in Wilder’s portrayal of Norma Desmond. Her inability to transition from silent stardom to the spoken screen mirrors the real-life decline of many former icons.\\

\subsubsection{Analysis}
The silent era symbolises a world of illusion sustained by artifice and image — a notion central to \underline{Sunset Boulevard}. Wilder’s film draws nostalgia and melancholy from this lost cinematic world, exposing the cost of technological progress and the impermanence of fame.\\

\subsection{The Golden Age of Hollywood}
From the 1930s through to the early 1960s, Hollywood entered what became known as its “Golden Age”. During this time, the major studios — MGM, Paramount, Warner Bros., and others — dominated the global film industry. By the mid-1940s, these studios produced hundreds of films annually, shaping popular imagination through idealised visions of love, success, and moral order.\\

Films of this period were characterised by clear narratives, linear plots, and archetypal heroes and heroines. Their moral tone was often conservative, reinforcing social hierarchies and traditional gender roles. However, by the late 1950s, the system began to weaken under the pressures of television, shifting audiences, and artistic fatigue.\\

However, the flim industry in the United States was going through a rough patch:
\begin{itemize}
\item Television was emerging as the dominant entertainment medium in the U.S.
\item Weekly cinema attendance was declining.
\item The Paramount anti-trust consent decree was implemented, requiring separate ownership for production companies and theatre chains.
\item There was a population shift to the suburbs, impacting downtown movie theatres.
\item There were a set of rules for moral guidelines that filmmakers had to follow under The Hays Code. 
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Analysis}
Wilder’s critique of Hollywood in \underline{Sunset Boulevard} coincides with the decline of this era. The film’s depiction of faded glamour and moral corruption exposes the darker underbelly of an industry once devoted to producing dreams. Through satire, Wilder dismantles the myth of Hollywood’s innocence.\\

\subsection{The Production Code}
Between 1930 and 1968, the film industry was governed by the Production Code Administration (PCA), a body that imposed strict moral guidelines on cinematic content. Initially voluntary, these regulations became mandatory by the mid-1930s and dictated that all films must uphold public decency and moral virtue.\\

Depictions of nudity, profanity, excessive violence, or sexual behaviour were prohibited. Filmmakers were compelled to ensure that wrongdoing was punished and virtue rewarded. Wilder and his collaborator Charles Brackett often tested these boundaries, embedding subversive commentary within seemingly conventional narratives.\\

In \underline{Sunset Boulevard}, the tragic fates of Joe Gillis and Norma Desmond adhere to the code’s demand for moral consequence — the transgressors must pay for their sins. Yet Wilder’s irony turns this moralism on its head, inviting sympathy for both victim and perpetrator.\\

\subsubsection{Analysis}
The Production Code’s influence gives the film a paradoxical tone — simultaneously moralistic and cynical. While the narrative satisfies censorship standards through punishment, it also exposes the hypocrisy of Hollywood’s moral pretensions. Wilder uses the very language of the Code to critique the system that enforced it.\\

\subsection{Film Noir}
\underline{Sunset Boulevard} belongs to the film noir tradition — a style originating in 1940s post-war America and named by French critics to describe the “dark films” emerging from Hollywood. Noir was marked by chiaroscuro lighting, urban settings, complex psychology, and an atmosphere of mistrust and fatalism.\\

The genre’s visual style relied on unbalanced compositions, low-key lighting, and stark black-and-white contrasts, evoking a world of corruption and moral ambiguity. In tone, noir films explored alienation and moral decay, often narrated by disillusioned protagonists trapped by circumstance.\\

\subsubsection{Analysis}
Wilder fuses noir aesthetics with Hollywood satire, using shadow and perspective to visualise moral collapse. Joe’s cynical narration, the claustrophobic mansion, and the interplay of light and darkness all reinforce themes of delusion, decay, and entrapment. The film thus becomes both a noir thriller and a self-reflexive critique of the industry that produced it.\\

\subsection{America in the 1950s}
Post-war America entered a period of unparalleled prosperity and conservatism. The 1950s were shaped by the Cold War, the rise of consumer culture, and rigid gender expectations. Prosperity bred conformity, and political paranoia — intensified by McCarthyism and the House Un-American Activities Committee — fostered fear of dissent.\\

Socially, women were expected to embody domestic virtue while men resumed positions of economic dominance. Yet beneath this façade of order lay widespread disillusionment and anxiety about identity and purpose. \underline{Sunset Boulevard} channels these tensions through its portrayal of isolation, ambition, and moral corruption within an ostensibly glamorous world.\\

\subsubsection{Analysis}
The film captures the contradictions of 1950s America: wealth and decay, progress and repression, fame and emptiness. Norma’s madness and Joe’s moral exhaustion personify the cultural malaise of a nation seduced by success yet haunted by its own discontents. Wilder’s critique of Hollywood thus becomes a mirror for post-war America’s soul.\\
\newpage
\section{Values in \underline{Sunset Boulevard}}

\subsection{Social Values}
\subsubsection{Traditional Gender Roles}
The era upheld traditional gender roles, with men as breadwinners and women primarily responsible for homemaking and child-raising. The suburban housewife became an iconic image of the time. Norma’s character highlights the fading role of ageing actresses in Hollywood, mirroring the limited opportunities for women beyond traditional roles as wives and mothers.\\

\subsubsection{Consumerism}
The 1950s were characterised by a post-war economic boom, and there was a strong emphasis on consumerism. Owning a home, car, and various consumer goods became aspirational goals. The opulent mansion on Sunset Boulevard and Norma’s extravagant lifestyle represent a distorted version of the consumerist dream, where wealth and possessions mask deeper issues.\\

\subsection{Cultural Values}
\subsubsection{Hollywood's Golden Age}
Hollywood’s Golden Age is a term used to describe a period in the history of the American film industry that roughly spans from the late 1920s to the early 1960s. It was a time of unprecedented creativity, innovation, and commercial success in Hollywood.
The Studio System – a way of making movies where big companies, like Warner Bros. and Paramount, had total control over everything. They decided which actors to use, what movies to make, and even how the movies were shown in theatres.\\

\subsubsection{Technological Advancements}
This era witnessed significant advancements in filmmaking technology, including the transition from silent films to ‘talkies’, the development of colour cinematography, and the use of widescreen formats.\\

\subsubsection{The Star System}
The method of creating, promoting and exploiting stars in Hollywood films. Movie studios would select promising young actors and glamourise them through various promotion tactics.\\

\section{Thematic Quote Bank}

\subsection{Fame and Obsession}
\begin{itemize}
  \item Norma Desmond: 'I am big. It's the pictures that got small.'
  \item Norma Desmond: 'They still write me fan letters every day. Why do they beg me for my photographs? Because they want to see me, me, me! Norma Desmond.'
  \item Gillis' Voice: 'She was still sleepwalking along the giddy heights of a lost career — plain crazy when it came to that one subject: her celluloid self, the great Norma Desmond.'
  \item Norma Desmond: 'Salome — what a woman! What a part! The Princess in love with a Holy man. She dances the Dance of the Seven Veils. He rejects her, so she demands his head on a golden tray, kissing his cold, dead lips.'
  \item Gillis' Voice: 'I could sense her eyes on me from behind those dark glasses, defying me not to like what I read, or maybe begging me in her own proud way to like it. It meant so much to her.'
  \item Norma Desmond: 'I wrote that with my heart.'
  \item Norma Desmond: 'I hate that word. It is a return. A return to the millions of people who have never forgiven me for deserting the screen.'\\
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Isolation and Decay}
\begin{itemize}
  \item Gillis' Voice: 'A neglected house gets an unhappy look. This one had it in spades. It was like that old woman in Great Expectations — Miss Havisham in her rotting wedding dress.'
  \item Gillis' Voice: 'There was a tennis court, or rather the ghost of a tennis court, with faded markings and a sagging net.'
  \item Gillis' Voice: 'There was something else going on below: the last rites for that hairy old chimp, performed with the utmost seriousness — as if she were laying to rest an only child.'
  \item Gillis' Voice: 'The whole place seemed to have been stricken with a kind of creeping paralysis, out of beat with the rest of the world, crumbling apart in slow motion.'
  \item Gillis' Voice: 'And of course she had a pool. Who didn't then? It was empty now... or was it?'
  \item Gillis' Voice: 'That night I'd had a mixed-up dream. In it was an organ grinder. I couldn't see his face, but the organ was all draped in black, and a chimp was dancing for pennies.'
  \item Gillis' Voice: 'She'd sit very close to me, and she'd smell of tuberoses, which is not my favorite perfume, not by a long shot.'\\
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Reality vs. Illusion}
\begin{itemize}
  \item Norma Desmond: 'I hate that word. It is a return. A return to the millions of people who have never forgiven me for deserting the screen.'
  \item Norma Desmond: 'We didn't need dialogue. We had faces. There just aren't any faces like that any more.'
  \item Gillis' Voice: 'You don't yell at a sleepwalker — he may fall and break his neck. That's it — she was still sleepwalking along the giddy heights of a lost career.'
  \item Gillis' Voice: 'She sure could say a lot of things with those pale eyes of hers. They’d been her trademark. They’d made her the Number One Vamp of another era.'
  \item Norma Desmond: 'Still wonderful, isn't it? And no dialogue. We had faces.'
  \item Norma Desmond: 'The plain fact was that she was afraid of that world outside. Afraid it would remind her that time had passed.'
  \item Gillis' Voice: 'She sat coiled up like a watch spring, her cigarette clamped in a curious holder... begging me in her own proud way to like it.'\\
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Power Dynamics}
\begin{itemize}
  \item Norma Desmond: 'You proud boy, why didn't you tell me you were having difficulties. It's all taken care of. It's all paid for.'
  \item Max: 'I made your bed this afternoon.' (Indicating that Norma has already decided Joe will stay)
  \item Norma Desmond: 'Suppose you make up your mind. Do you want this job or don't you?'
  \item Gillis: 'I let him unpack my things. I wanted the dough and I wanted to get out of there as quickly as possible.'
  \item Gillis: 'I’m used to paying my own bills.'
  \item Norma Desmond: 'Now, now, don't let's be small about such matters. We won't keep books.'
  \item Gillis' Voice: 'So I let him unpack my things. I wanted the dough, and I wanted to get out of there as quickly as possible.'\\
\end{itemize}

\subsection{The Dark Side of Hollywood}
\begin{itemize}
  \item Gillis: 'Well, I needed about two hundred and ninety dollars real quick, or I'd lose my car... I was way ahead of the finance company.'
  \item Norma Desmond: 'Look at them in the front offices — the masterminds! They took the idols and smashed them.'
  \item Betty Schaefer: 'It's from hunger... Just a rehash of something that wasn't very good to begin with.'
  \item Gillis: 'Just sold an original for a hundred grand. The Life of the Warner Brothers. Starring the Ritz Brothers.' (sarcastically to Skolsky, showing how phony Hollywood is)
  \item Sheldrake: 'Who wants to see a Civil War picture?' (regarding Gone with the Wind)
  \item Gillis' Voice: 'Actors and stock girls and waiters. Kind of a combination office, Kaffee-Klatsch and waiting room. Waiting, waiting for the gravy train.' (about Schwab’s Drugstore)
  \item Gillis' Voice: 'The whole place seemed to have been stricken with a kind of creeping paralysis, out of beat with the rest of the world, crumbling apart in slow motion.'\\
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Gender Roles}
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{JOE} A woman almost twice my age ...
\item \textbf{JOE} A very simple set-up: An older woman who is well-to-do. A younger man who is not doing too well ... Can you figure it out yourself?
\item \textbf{JOE} Norma, grow up. You're a woman of fifty. There's nothing tragic about being fifty — not unless you try to be twenty-five.
\item \textbf{GILLIS} I don't need any clothes, and I certainly don't want you buying them for --
\item \textbf{NORMA} I'll buy you a boat and we'll sail to Hawaii.
\item \textbf{NORMA} I own three blocks downtown. I have oil in Bakersfield — pumping, pumping, pumping. What's it for but to buy us anything we want.
\item \textbf{NORMA} Just follow me.
\item \textbf{GILLIS} Pull up at the drugstore, will you, Max. (To Norma) I'll get you some.
\end{itemize}


\subsection{Love, Infatuation and Narcissism}
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{NORMA} Salome -- what a woman! What a part! The Princess in love with a Holy man. She dances the Dance.
\item \textbf{GILLIS} They'll love it in Pomona.
\item \textbf{NORMA} They will love it every place. Read it. Read the scene just before she has him killed!
\item \textbf{NORMA} Perfect. Wonderful shoulders. And I love that line.
\item \textbf{NORMA} Is it funny that I'm in love with you?
\item \textbf{NORMA} I'm in love with you. Don't you know that? I've been in love with you all along.
\item \textbf{NORMA} What you're trying to say is that you don't want me to love you. Is that it?
\item \textbf{ARTIE} Wait a minute. This is the woman I love. What's going on? Who was loaded?
\item \textbf{NORMA} To fall in love with you -- that was the idiotic thing.
\item \textbf{BETTY} Of course I love him. I always will. I'm just not in love with him any more.
\item \textbf{JOE} And she was in love with me. Me! She was a fool not to sense that there was something phoney in my set-up.
\item \textbf{NORMA} (In a whimpering monotone) I love you, Joe. I love you, Joe. I love you, Joe. I love you, Joe.
\item \textbf{BETTY} If you love me, Joe.
\item \textbf{NORMA} You're here, Joe ... When did you come home? Where were you? Is it a woman? I know it's a woman ... Who is she? Oh Joe, why can't I ask you? I must know, I must!
\end{itemize}

\section{Character Quote Bank}


\subsection{Joe Gillis}
\subsubsection{Fame}
\begin{itemize}
\item That was last year. This year I'm trying to earn a living.
\item I know your face. You're Norma Desmond. You used to be in pictures. You used to be big.
\item I don't think so. It's your script. It's your show. Good luck.
\item If you get a hundred thousand for it, you buy me a box of chocolate creams. If you get an Oscar, I get the left foot.
\item He must be quite a guy.
\item Come on where? Back to a one-room apartment that I can't pay for? Back to a story that may sell and very possibly will not?
\item And I'd take it in a second, Norma — only it's a little too dressy for sitting behind the copy desk in Dayton, Ohio.
\item Norma, you'd be throwing it away. I don't qualify for the job, not any more.
\item Norma, grow up. You're a woman of fifty. There's nothing tragic about being fifty — not unless you try to be twenty-five.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Reality and Illusion}

\begin{itemize}
\item Oh, you're one of the message kids. Just a story won't do. You'd have turned down \textit{Gone With the Wind}.
\item Forget Romanoff's. It's the car I'm talking about. If I lose my car it's like having my legs cut off.
\item You know, I'm pretty expensive. I get five hundred a week.
\item Why? Her career? She got enough out of it. She's not forgotten. She still gets those fan letters.
\item Yes, I was playing hooky every evening along in there.
\item Oh, that. It's from a friend of mine. A middle-aged lady, very foolish and very generous.
\item Sure you are. And we're not helping her any, feeding her lies and more lies. Getting herself ready for a picture ... What happens when she finds out?
\item That's between you and yourself, Norma.
\item Wake up, Norma. You'd be killing yourself to an empty house. The audience left twenty years ago. Now face it.
\item De Mille didn't have the heart to tell you. None of us has had the heart.
\item Madame is the greatest star of them all... I will take Mr. Gillis' bags.
\end{itemize}

\section{Character Relationships}

\subsection{Joe Gillis and Norma Desmond}
Joe and Norma's relationship is built on dependence, illusion, and emotional imbalance. Norma clings to Joe as a last connection to youth and relevance, while Joe allows himself to be financially supported, though emotionally trapped. Their dynamic is parasitic and destructive, culminating in Joe’s death and Norma’s complete psychological collapse.

\subsubsection*{Key Quotes}
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textit{Joe:} 'Norma, you'd be throwing it away. I don't qualify for the job, not any more.'
    \item \textit{Norma:} 'Is it funny that I'm in love with you?'
    \item \textit{Joe:} 'Wake up, Norma. You’d be killing yourself to an empty house. The audience left twenty years ago.'
    \item \textit{Norma:} 'You're not leaving me!'
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection*{Key Ideas}
\begin{itemize}
    \item Dependency
    \item Reality vs. Illusion
    \item Power imbalance
    \item Gender roles
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Joe Gillis and Betty Schaefer}
Joe and Betty’s relationship symbolises hope, creativity, and authentic emotional connection. While Joe is initially cynical, Betty helps him reconnect with his talent and ideals. However, Joe’s entrapment in Norma’s world ultimately sabotages their bond.

\subsubsection*{Key Quotes}
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textit{Betty:} 'If you love me, Joe.'
    \item \textit{Joe:} 'Three cheers for Betty Schaefer! I will now kiss that nose of yours.'
    \item \textit{Betty:} 'Joe, I can't look at you any more.'
    \item \textit{Joe:} 'Good luck to you, Betty... When you and Artie get back... here’s the pool.'
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection*{Key Ideas}
\begin{itemize}
    \item Authenticity
    \item Missed opportunities
    \item Love vs. obligation
    \item Hope and disappointment
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Norma Desmond and Max Von Mayerling}
Max and Norma share a hauntingly devoted and co-dependent relationship. Once her director and husband, Max now lives as her butler, fuelling her illusions to protect her fragile psyche. His loyalty borders on tragic obsession.

\subsubsection*{Max as a Pygmalion Archetype}
Max can be read as a Pygmalion archetype: like the mythical sculptor who fell in love with the statue he carved, Max created the star 'Norma Desmond' \textemdash{} he discovered her at sixteen, directed her early pictures, and 'made her a star' \textemdash{} and then fell in love with his own creation. Unlike Pygmalion, however, Max cannot bring his creation to life in the present; instead he devotes himself to preserving the illusion he built, writing the fan letters that sustain it. This reading deepens the tragedy of their relationship: Norma's delusion is not merely her own weakness but a construction Max continues to maintain, making him both her most loyal protector and the quiet architect of her downfall.

\subsubsection*{Key Quotes}
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textit{Max:} 'I made her a star. I cannot let her be destroyed.'
    \item \textit{Max:} 'It was I who asked to come back... I was her first husband.'
    \item \textit{Max:} 'The cameras have arrived, Madame.'
    \item \textit{Norma:} 'All right, Mr. deMille, I'm ready for my close-up.'
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection*{Key Ideas}
\begin{itemize}
    \item Loyalty
    \item Delusion
    \item Power reversal
    \item Sacrifice
    \item The Pygmalion archetype \textemdash{} the creator in service of his creation
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Norma Desmond and Cecil B. deMille}
Norma views deMille as the key to her return to fame, though he treats her with nostalgia and pity. Their relationship highlights the **Hollywood power shift** from silent films to sound, and the industry's neglect of ageing stars.

\subsubsection*{Key Quotes}
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textit{Norma:} 'Now Mr. deMille can wait till I'm good and ready.'
    \item \textit{deMille:} 'Thirty million fans have given her the brush. Isn’t that enough?'
    \item \textit{Norma:} 'We’ll be working again, won’t we, Chief?'
    \item \textit{deMille:} 'Tell him to forget about our car. I’ll buy him five old cars if necessary.'
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection*{Key Ideas}
\begin{itemize}
    \item Fame and decay
    \item Nostalgia vs. reality
    \item Hollywood’s cruelty
    \item Gendered ageism
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Joe Gillis and Max Von Mayerling}
Though they barely trust one another, Joe and Max are both caught in Norma’s gravitational pull. Max warns Joe of Norma’s delusions but ultimately enables them, silently observing Joe’s downfall.

\subsubsection*{Key Quotes}
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textit{Max:} 'You must understand, I discovered her when she was eighteen.'
    \item \textit{Max:} 'Madame is the greatest star of them all... I will take Mr. Gillis’ bags.'
    \item \textit{Joe:} 'Oh, that. It’s from a friend of mine. A middle-aged lady, very foolish and very generous.'
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection*{Key Ideas}
\begin{itemize}
    \item Powerlessness
    \item Enabling behaviour
    \item Masculinity
    \item Pity and rivalry
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Joe Gillis and Artie Green}
Joe and Artie are casual friends, connected through Betty. Artie is warm, generous, and unsuspecting—offering Joe a place to stay and unknowingly introducing him to Betty, who will fall in love with Joe. Their relationship represents Hollywood's working-class camaraderie, but it is ultimately undermined by Joe’s betrayal.

\subsubsection*{Key Quotes}
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textit{Artie:} 'Hey Joe, I said you could have my couch, I didn’t say you could have my girl.'
    \item \textit{Artie:} 'Where you been keeping that gorgeous face of yours?'
    \item \textit{Artie:} 'Well what do you know, Joe Gillis.'
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection*{Key Ideas}
\begin{itemize}
    \item Friendship
    \item Jealousy and betrayal
    \item Trust
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Betty Schaefer and Artie Green}
Betty and Artie are engaged, but her growing admiration for Joe shifts the dynamic. Artie symbolises comfort and stability, while Joe represents ambition and excitement. Their relationship quietly dissolves as Betty’s emotional attention shifts.

\subsubsection*{Key Quotes}
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textit{Artie:} 'Wait a minute, this is the woman I love, who was loaded.'
    \item \textit{Betty:} 'Of course I love him. I always will. I'm just not in love with him any more.'
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection*{Key Ideas}
\begin{itemize}
    \item Love vs. stability
    \item Unspoken heartbreak
    \item Emotional conflict
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Joe Gillis and Sheldrake}
Sheldrake represents the industry gatekeepers—pragmatic and dismissive. Joe’s interactions with him underscore how impersonal and transactional Hollywood has become. Their relationship exposes the disillusionment of struggling writers.

\subsubsection*{Key Quotes}
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textit{Sheldrake:} 'Alright Gillis, you’ve got five minutes, what's your story about?'
    \item \textit{Joe:} 'You’d have turned down \textit{Gone With the Wind}.'
    \item \textit{Sheldrake:} 'Put in a few numbers, that’d make a cute musical.'
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection*{Key Ideas}
\begin{itemize}
    \item Hollywood as business
    \item Artistic compromise
    \item Creative frustration
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Norma Desmond and Betty Schaefer}
Although they never interact directly, Norma and Betty represent **opposing ideals of womanhood**. Betty is young, modern, and practical; Norma is older, delusional, and clinging to a past era. Their symbolic rivalry culminates in Norma's jealousy-fuelled murder of Joe.

\subsubsection*{Key Quotes (Implied Tension)}
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textit{Norma (to Joe):} 'Is it a woman? I know it's a woman...'
    \item \textit{Joe:} 'All right, baby. This way out.'
    \item \textit{Betty:} 'If you love me, Joe.'
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection*{Key Ideas}
\begin{itemize}
    \item Rivalry
    \item Gendered insecurity
    \item Age and beauty
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Norma Desmond and Cecil B. deMille}
Norma idolises deMille as her old mentor, believing he will revive her career. deMille pities Norma, indulges her briefly, but ultimately distances himself. Their relationship exemplifies Hollywood’s betrayal of its past stars.

\subsubsection*{Key Quotes}
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textit{Norma:} 'Now Mr. deMille can wait till I'm good and ready.'
    \item \textit{deMille:} 'Thirty million fans have given her the brush. Isn’t that enough?'
    \item \textit{Norma:} 'We'll be working again, won't we, Chief?'
    \item \textit{deMille:} 'A dozen press agents working overtime can do terrible things to the human spirit.'
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection*{Key Ideas}
\begin{itemize}
    \item Nostalgia
    \item Fame and decline
    \item Compassion vs. cruelty
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Max Von Mayerling and Betty Schaefer}
Though they never meet, Max and Betty represent two opposite relationships with Joe: one built on silent loyalty and loss of self (Max), the other on mutual respect and potential for growth (Betty). Max is the ghost of what Joe could become if he fully submits to Norma.

\subsubsection*{Key Quotes (Implied Contrast)}
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textit{Max:} 'I directed all her early pictures. I made her a star.'
    \item \textit{Betty:} 'I want to write. I don’t want to be a reader all my life.'
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection*{Key Ideas}
\begin{itemize}
    \item Self-sacrifice vs. ambition
    \item Emotional entrapment vs. liberation
    \item Generational contrast
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Sheldrake and Betty Schaefer}
Betty and Sheldrake represent opposite ends of the studio power structure. Sheldrake controls opportunities and trivialises creative work, while Betty strives to have a voice as a writer. Their professional relationship reflects the dismissiveness of studio executives toward aspiring creatives.

\subsubsection*{Key Quotes}
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textit{Sheldrake:} 'Make it a girls’ softball team... it’d make a cute musical.'
    \item \textit{Betty:} 'I kind of hoped to get in on this deal. I don’t want to be a reader all my life.'
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection*{Key Ideas}
\begin{itemize}
    \item Industry hierarchy
    \item Gender and creative voice
    \item Power and dismissiveness
\end{itemize}

subsection{Joe Gillis and Cecil B. deMille}
Joe’s view of deMille is shaped by disillusionment; deMille represents the **industry's indifference** toward people like Norma and himself. Their interaction is limited, but deMille’s treatment of Norma is indirectly relayed to Joe—highlighting Hollywood’s tendency to sentimentalise the past while refusing to truly engage with it.

\subsubsection*{Key Quotes}
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textit{deMille:} 'Thirty million fans have given her the brush. Isn’t that enough?'
    \item \textit{Joe (on deMille):} 'He didn’t have the heart to tell you. None of us has had the heart.'
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection*{Key Ideas}
\begin{itemize}
    \item Fame and abandonment
    \item Disillusionment with power
    \item Truth vs. kindness
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Betty Schaefer and Norma Desmond}
Though Betty and Norma never speak directly, their relationship exists as a powerful symbolic contrast—two women representing vastly different eras, values, and roles in Hollywood. Betty is young, ambitious, and grounded in reality; Norma is ageing, delusional, and obsessed with regaining lost fame. They become rivals not only for Joe’s affection but also as emblematic opposites: one looking forward, the other trapped in the past. Norma’s jealousy toward Betty culminates in Joe’s death—making their indirect relationship one of the most consequential in the film.

\subsubsection*{Key Quotes}
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textit{Betty:} 'It’s not your career — it’s mine. I kind of hoped to get in on this deal. I don’t want to be a reader all my life. I want to write.'
    \item \textit{Norma:} 'Is it a woman? I know it's a woman...'
    \item \textit{Norma:} 'You're not leaving me!'
    \item \textit{Joe (to Betty):} 'Good luck to you, Betty... here's the pool.'
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection*{Key Ideas}
\begin{itemize}
    \item Generational conflict
    \item Female ambition vs. faded fame
    \item Jealousy and romantic rivalry
    \item Hope vs. delusion
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{Relationship with Ambition}
Ambition is a desire and determination to achieve success. It is a motivational force that compels individuals to set and pursue goals, often with a willingness to work hard, overcome obstacles, and make sacrifices to attain those goals.\\

\subsubsection{Norma Desmond}
\begin{itemize}
\item Norma’s ambition is rooted in her desire to regain her past Hollywood stardom and in her fear of becoming irrelevant.
\item It is also sustained by her delusional belief in her continued desirability as an actress, despite Hollywood moving on. 
\item Norma’s isolation and the presence of enablers who validate her fantasies further fuel her ambition and detachment from reality.
\item In response to the word comeback: ‘I hate that word. It’s a return, a return to the millions of people who have never forgiven me for deserting the screen.’
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Joe Gillis}
\begin{itemize}
\item Joe’s ambition arises from financial desperation and a desire for Hollywood success.
\item His pursuit of financial security and career advancement drives him into a complex relationship with Norma.
\item ‘The poor dope - he always wanted a pool. Well, in the end, he got himself a pool.’
\end{itemize}


\subsubsection{Betty Schaefer}
\begin{itemize}
\item Betty is driven by her genuine passion for screenwriting and a strong desire to succeed in the film industry.
\item ‘What’s wrong with being on the other side of the cameras? It’s really more fun.’
\end{itemize}
\subsubsection{Max von Mayerling}
\begin{itemize}
\item Max’s ambition is solely devoted to serving and preserving Norma Desmond’s illusion of fame.
\item He is driven by his unwavering dedication to Norma, having been her director and husband during her stardom.
\item ‘Madame is the greatest star of them all.’
\end{itemize}
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\subsection*{Relationship with Delusion}
\subsubsection{Joe’s agent, Morino}
Wilder characterises Joe’s agent Morino as a representative of the cutthroat nature of Hollywood. Morino says he could give Gillis money… but he won’t. Instead, Gillis should use his desperate state as inspiration – it’ll force him to write because he’ll need to do it.

\subsubsection{Betty Schaefer}
Betty is portrayed as a down-to-earth, sincere, and practical character. She represents a departure from the delusional world of Hollywood, focusing on her genuine passion for screenwriting and her desire for authentic, meaningful connections.

\subsubsection{Norma Desmond}
\begin{itemize}
\item ‘All right, Mr. DeMille, I’m ready for my close-up.’
\item In the film’s iconic closing line, Norma's delusional state of mind reaches its peak, marking her descent into madness. While she retained some connection to reality in earlier scenes, this quote signifies the complete erasure of any remaining self-awareness. Norma regresses to her past glory, even mistaking Max for director Cecil DeMille, vividly highlighting the profound transformation of her fantasy world.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Joe Gillis}
\begin{itemize}
\item ‘You don’t yell at a sleepwalker – he may fall and break his neck. That’s it: She was still sleepwalking along the giddy heights of a lost career.’
\item Joe compares Norma to a sleepwalker, suggesting she is unaware of her actions and the reality of her situation, emphasising the delicate balance between preserving her illusions and confronting the truth of her faded career.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Max von Mayerling}
\begin{itemize}
\item ‘That is my job. It has been for a long time. You must understand I discovered her when she was eighteen. I made her a star. I cannot let her be destroyed.’
\item By nourishing her illusions and crafting fan mail, Max sees himself as Norma’s guardian, dedicated to preserving her faded fame. Yet, as he fervently conceals the truth to protect her from despair, his deceptions inadvertently propel her further into madness.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{Relationship with Greed}

    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=1\linewidth]{gtable.png}
    \caption{Who Decieves Who? in \underline{Sunset Boulevard}}
\end{figure}

\section{Scene Summaries and Analyses}
\subsection{Scene 1 : The Corpse (0:00:0 - 0:02:38)}
Summary : Opening Credits; a body is discovered in the pool of a Hollywood Mansion.\\
1. The opening music and the long shot of the road in the film contribute to creating a mood of dramatic, lonely, mystery, tension, apprehension, terrified and shocked.\\
2. When the voice-over explains that the 'poor dope' always wanted a pool, the price 'turned out to be too high', the purpose of this voice-over to refer to the main\\
character Joe Gillis, the outcome of his desires and decision to the dark side of Hollywood. Other answer : The voice meant that the man always wanted a pod. But ironically he ended up dead in it, the price he paid was life.\\
3. Joe Gillis voice-over is a key storytelling tool. It not only leads us through the film's scenes but also gives us a deeper focus into Joe's character, his challenging, his struggling and the tough realities he encounters in the competitive Hollywood environment.' Other answer : We learn that the narrator is a young man who is nobody important, just a movie script-writer.\\

\subsection{Scene 2 : The Corpse (0:02:38 - 0:09:55)}
Summary : The finance company threatens to repossess Joe’s car. Joe tries unsuccessfully
to seek the job and borrow the money from his friend to save his car from the finance
company.\\
1. My first impressions of Joe Gillis are of a character who is disillusioned and
somewhat cynical about Hollywood. He appears to be a struggling screenwriter who
feels overlooked and underappreciated by the industry.\\
2. The entrance gates to Paramount Studios illustrate the attraction and exclusivity of
Hollywood. They represent the gateway to fame, success, and the charming world of
the movie industry. (represent wealth and power)\\
3. Schwab's Pharmacy is a drugstore, serving as a multifaceted symbol of Hollywood's
allure, desire, and the stark contrast between illusion and reality within the film
construction.\\
4. Betty Schaefer's appraisal of Joe's script 'Bases Loaded' has a particular impact on
Joe both personally and professionally and Joe is furious with Betty because the way
she said to Joe, it can affect him to seek his job. (being resentful, uncomfortable,
frustrated and saying she was just a message kid.)\\


\subsection{Scene 3 : A White Elephant of a House (0:09:55 - 0:12:55)}
Summary : Joe is pursued by the finance company who wants to obtain his car, he escapes
by landing himself in the driveway of some big mansion and storing his car into the huge
garage at that mansion.\\
1. The music score during the car chase supplies a powerful tool for conveying Joe's
emotions, as well as amplifying the suspense, drama, and darkly comedic elements
of the film. After he is landing himself in the driveway in a big mansion, the music
slows down which shows Joe’s emotion of peace. In addition, the panicky energy of
the car chase, the surrounding area in the driveway may provide the risk of serious
car accident.\\
2. Joe narrates about the house that ‘a neglected house gets an unhappy look. This
one had it in spades’, it evoked a sense of disquiet and desolation which makes the
audience assume that the people who live in this mansion might be struggling with
personal or emotional challenges.\\
3. Joe's plan to stash the car displays his resourcefulness, street smarts and
cautiousness, all of which are essential aspects of his character as he navigates the
complexities of life in Hollywood.\\

\subsection{Scene 4 : ‘I am big. It’s the picture that got small’ (0:12:55 - 0:17:04)}
Summary : Joe is mistaken for walking into this old huge mansion of his curiosity. He meets
the isolated former silent film star and recognises her as Norma Desmond.\\
1. We first see Norma from a low angle shot looking up at her in the window watching
Joe, which impresses with power, mystery, weakness, and greatness.\\
2. Norma's bedroom is a luxury designed space that serves as a reflection of her past
fame, present circumstances, and complex inner world.\\
3. Wilder productively presents Norma Desmond's belief that she is still ' big ' in
Hollywood through a combination of visual storytelling, dialogue, character
interactions, and narrative development.\\
4. Despite their contrasting personalities and conditions, Joe and Norma share common
themes and experiences related to their desires, struggles, and illusions in the world
of Hollywood.\\

\subsection{Scene 5 : The Salome Script (0:17:04 - 0:23:01)}
1. Norma comments about her comeback, ‘A return to the millions of people who’ve
never forgiven me for destroying the screen’, she means her complicated
relationship with fame, legacy, possessiveness and self-perception.\\
2. After Joe reads through Norma’s script, he views it as outdated, melodramatic, and
unsuitable for modern audiences.\\
3. The close-up on Norma's face when she suggests Joe stay the night reveals her
intricate emotions of loneliness, dependency, desperation and optimism about
returning to stardom again.\\
4. The mise-en-scène in Norma's salon is accurately crafted to reflect her personality,
status, history, mindset and the overall atmosphere of her character's world.\\

\subsection{Scene 6 : An Invitation to Stay (0:23:01 - 0:28:04)}
Summary : Joe spends the night in Norma's garage due to the need to modify Norma’s
script, Max takes Joe’s belongings from his apartment as what Norma desires.\\
1. The burial ceremony for the chimpanzee provides insight into Norma Desmond's life,
highlighting her extravagance, eccentricity, and underlying feelings of loneliness.\\
2. Norma's decision to pay Joe's unpaid rent is driven by a combination of infatuation,
manipulation, fear, and a desire for control.\\
3. Billy Wilder illustrates Norma as the one in control through her artful behaviour,
domineering presence, financial influence and emotional manipulation.\\

\subsection{Scene 7 : The Face of a Star (0:28:04 - 0:31:47)}
Summary : Joe works on Norma’s script. Joe and Norma watch a movie in the living room
together about Norma’s silent movie film in which she is a main actor.\\
1. When Joe attempts to change her script, Norma reacts irritated, terrified and shocked
due to Joe removing some scenes which relate to her acting. In addition, Norma
wants to have her in all scenes of the movie because she believes that her fans
obsess to see her every episode.\\
2. The panning of the photos of Norma in her mansion serves to illustrate her obsession
with her past stardom and her desperate attempt to hold onto her fading fame.\\
3. The irony in Norma showing the 'idiot producers' ' what a star looks like underlines
the themes of delusion, self-confidence, and tragedy. She stopped working at the
Paramount studio and she didn’t have a beautiful face anymore like before.\\
4. Norma's clutching on Joe's arm conveys a beginning of relationship emotions,
including desire, fear, insecurity, control, and longing.\\

\subsection{Scene 8 : Bridge with the Wax Works (0:31:47 - 0:36:10)}
Summary : Joe loses his car because the finance company comes to Norma’s house and
informs Max that they will repossess Joe’s car due to his 3 payments behind. Norma plays a
little bridge game with Norma’s actor friends. She takes Joe to a men's clothing shop to buy
the shirt for Joe as he’s wearing an old suit everyday.\\
1. The 'waxworks” were former silent film stars who, like Norma, have fading their
careers, delusional, and isolated.\\
2. The quote that shows Joe is becoming a kept man is 'He was the kept boy of a now
somewhat lady-like but very rich lady, about fifteen years older than he was.' which
Joe said to reflect his own situation. Well, as long as the lady's paying for it. Why not
take the vicuna.\\

\subsection{Scene 9 : The Husbands’ Bedroom (0:36:10 - 0:39:31)}
Summary : Joe moves into the main house to live in Norma’s ex-husbands room.\\
1. Joe's move into 'the room of the husband' which indicate various possibilities for the
track of their relationship and Joe is increasingly becoming a part of Norma's destiny.\\
2. This scene makes us believe that Norma is living in a fantasy world and not facing
reality based on the fact that she has moments of sadness which in her mansion
doesn’t have locks, sleeping pills, razor blades and shut off the gas in her room.\\
3. The mise en scene in Norma's bedroom is carefully crafted to convey themes of
longing, decay, and fantasy. In addition, the bedroom represents a place that reflects
Norma's emotion. Satin, drapes, bedding, ruffles, bed like a gilded rowboat (covered in
gold), old fashioned, old furniture. Norma’s living in the past. Keep her lavish
lifestyle.\\

\subsection{Scene 10 : New Year’s Eve (0:39:31 - 0:53:50)}
Summary : Norma declares that there are only two of them in this New Year’s eve party and
she confesses to Joe that she fell in love with him. Joe escapes Norma after they have an
argument between each other about their relationship and Joe returns because when he
calls Max to back up his belongings, someone will pick them up, but Max tells him that the
doctor has come to Norma’s mansion because Norma wants to kill herself.\\
1. The gold engraved cigarette case symbolises Norma’s present to Joe for New Year’s
eve and about her emotion of love with Joe.\\
2. New Year’s Eve from Max’s perspective in which he remembers his old love
memories with Norma when they were husband and wife.\\
3. The turning point for Joe is when he returns to the mansion, after Max calls him to tell
about Norma wants to murder herself because Joe rejects her love for him, then Joe
decides to become Norma’s partner from that night.\\
NOTE: Norma slit her wrists.\\

\subsection{Scene 11 : A Kept Man (0:53:50 - 0:58:38)}
Summary : Betty is trying to persuade and encourage Joe to collaborate on a screenplay
with her, Norma sends Max to ask Joe to come back to the car and Betty wants Joe to work
with her in a new story.\\
1. The interactions between Norma and Joe are characterised by power struggles,
complicated emotions and dependency.\\
2. In this scene, we learn about Betty's hopefulness, working hard and she wants to be
a screenwriter rather than read scripture. In addition, she persuades Joe to work with
her in a new movie that she creates by herself.\\
3. When Norma gives money to Joe to buy cigarettes, the emphasis of Joe doesn’t
have enough money to buy cigarettes. Moreover, Joe is dependent on Norma’s
wealth and Norma has the power to control Joe.\\

\subsection{Scene 12 : The Norma Desmond Follies (0:58:38 - 1:01:24)}
Summary : Norma shows a Charlie Chaplin comedy performance to Joe when she suspects
that Joe’s getting bored with her and Norma receives a telephone call from the Paramount
studio.\\
1. Norma Desmond entertains Joe Gillis as Charlie Chaplin because she doesn’t want
Joe to get bored with her, so she tries to show every comedy's famous movie to
make Joe enjoy living with her and she is scared that Joe will leave her one day.\\
2. The contrast between Joe when he first came to Hollywood and now is when Joe first
arrives at Hollywood, he is hopeful and believes that he can find a great job and be a
successful screenwriter. However, until now he becomes disillusioned with Hollywood
and lives a life which is dependent on Norma’s wealth.\\

\subsection{Scene 13 : Parading to Paramount 9 (1:01:24 - 1:04:42)}
Summary : Norma visits Paramount studio after urgent calls from Paramount.\\
1. The emphasis of the difference between past and present in this scene is the
homesickness and romanticism of Hollywood and the harsh realities of ageing and
unimportance faced by its former stars. For example, after 20 years, when Norma
goes back to Paramount Studio the new generation no one knows her except old
people who work with her in that time and also no one in that studio wants her
anymore due to her age and obsession.\\
2. Norma Desmond and Isotta Fraschini share similarities in their representation of
faded their famous, delusions of beauty and they both serve as powerful symbols in
the past.\\

\subsection{Scene 14 : A Meeting with Mr. Demille (1:04:42 - 1:12:48)}
Summary : Norma speaks to Mr. Demille about her script and someone calling her, Joe talks
with Betty.\\
1. The high angle shot of Stage 18 at Paramount Studios provides viewers with a
perspective of the studio that emphasises its greatness and glory. When the camera
is looking down on Norma Desmond it signifies her fading of fame and
powerlessness.\\
2. When Norma says to Demille, ' And remember, darling. I don’t work before 10 in the
morning and never after 4:30 in the afternoon”, she is failing to realise the extent of
her own delusion, unreasonableness and the reality of her situation in the modern
film industry.\\
3. In this scene, we learn that Max is loyal and intelligent because he found out the
reason for all those phone calls from Paramount, they’re not want Norma but they
want Norma’s car and he didn’t tell Norma about it because he is scared that Norma
will be furious and disappointed. Moreover, he is also a former employee who worked
in Paramount studio as a director.\\
4. The close-ups of Joe and Betty talking, allow Wilder to express the depth and
beginning of their relationship, complicated for them to communicate to each other
and the emotional difficulty of their interactions.\\

\subsection{Scene 15 : Joe Sneak Out (1:12:48 - 1:17:56)}
Summary : Norma is going through a merciless series of treatment. Joe meets Betty
Schafer at night to write a screenplay together.\\
1. Norma's efforts to prepare for her 'return' to the screen are characterised by
hopefulness, obsession and she thinks that there are a lot of fans who are waiting for
her.\\
2. Wilder shows the power Norma has over Joe though she doesn’t allow Joe to go
anywhere what he wants, when Joe goes somewhere he needs to tell Norma and
Joe doesn’t have freedom. She said ' You went out last night, didn’t you Joe ?”.\\
3. Joe desires to get free from his dependence on Norma and control over his own life
which is the symbol for it being taken away represents his demise.\\
4. The irony in Joe’s statement 'That’s the trouble with you reader, you know all the
plots.” Joe's statement carries irony in its criticism of Betty's understanding of plots
while at the same time reflecting his own fault as a writer and his final fall within the
imprison of the Hollywood industry.\\

\subsection{Scene 16 : Untitled Love Story (1:17:56 - 1:26:19)}
Summary : Joe and Betty become closer friends. Max reveals to Joe that he was the first
husband of Norma. Norma discovers that Joe and Betty declare they have feelings for each
other.\\
1. Wilder uses the collaborative process of writing a screenplay at night to illustrate the
slow growth of Joe and Betty's relationship, from writers' partners to close friends
and, eventually, romantic interests.\\
2. Joe and Max have a confrontation about Joe going out every night to write a
screenplay with Betty which can make Norma furious and jealous about it. Max, a
former husband who still loves her and protects Norma by writing fan letters to
Norma and lying to Norma about her comeback to the studio. In addition, when
Norma was young, he was one of the directors who made Norma become a famous
silent film star and he didn't want to destroy her.\\
3. Wilder uses camera angles and framing in the last scene between Joe and Betty to
represent their true love and deep connection between each other but they can’t be
together due to a lot of complicated drama. Ultimately, Joe and Betty's relationship
can’t continue anymore after Betty knows the truth about Joe living with Norma
Desmond.\\

\subsection{Scene 17 : Betty’s Exit (1:26:19 - 1:34:35)}
Summary : Norma tells the truth about Joe and her living arrangement to Betty. Betty comes
to Norma’s mansion to find out about it and Joe introduces/leaves/ Norma’s house to Betty.\\
1. Joe's decision to invite Betty Schaefer to Norma Desmond's mansion shows his
growing sense of honesty, responsibility, acceptance of reality and he tells Betty
about his situation that he has a good deal in this house and he doesn’t want to go
to one room apartment which he can’t pay for and even the screenplay which they
write together.\\
2. The techniques that Wilder uses to show a shift in power and build up of tension is
cinematography, lighting, sound design, dialogue, set design, editing and creating a
scene rich with dramatic intensity, emotional depth and low angels light camera
between Joe and Norma which makes him become more powerful on Norma.\\
3. Betty's feelings of curiosity, anxiety, concern for Joe,support him through whatever
challenges they may face and she deals with that by always staying on Joe’s side
whatever happens to him.\\
4. Norma’s actions and feelings in this scene is when she calls Betty to tell her the facts
about Joe and her relationship and their living arrangement because she is
increasingly jealous and scared that Joe will leave her to live with another girl one
day who is younger than her. Norma Desmond says 'men of his sort', she is likely
talking to men who are selfish, cowardly and take advantage of others for their own
benefits .\\
5. It is ironic that Joe turns the lights on in the pool because he wants to tell Betty about
the danger, deception and darkness of his life with Norma in her mansion.

\subsection{Scene 18 : Noone Ever Leaves A Star (1:34:35-1:39:13)}
Summary : Joe tells Norma that he will leave the house and go back to his hometown, she
tells Joe that she will kill herself if he leaves her.\\
1. The close-up on Norma's face as she looks in the mirror represents a max
complication of emotions, including hopeful and satisfied Joe deciding to be
desperate from Betty because he gets a lot of benefits from this house.\\
2. The context of the scene when Joe says to Norma 'The audience left twenty years
ago” is he wants to inform Norma to wake up from the past, self-obsession,
daydreaming and delusion because nowaday no one cares about her anymore.\\
3. Norma persuades and warns Joe not to abandate her, if he goes away from her, she
will shoot him but Joe doesn’t listen to Norma’s saying, then Norma decides to shoot
Joe on the way out the mansion and he falls into the swimming pool where Joe’s
end.\\

\subsection{Scene 19 : ‘All right Mr deMille, I’m ready for my close up’
(1:34:35-1:39:13)}
Summary : The house is surrounded by the policies, the reporter neighbours and passer-by.
Joe’s body is floating in the swimming pool. Norma has a mental shock after she kills Joe.\\
1. The narrative style adds depth to the storytelling, allowing the audience to empathise
with Joe's experiences while also providing important scenes through which to view
the themes of fame, obsession, examining the dark side of Hollywood and
disillusionment within the film.\\
2. Black humour is evident when the voice over says ' Here was an item everybody
could have fun with”, which is referring to Joe’s body floating in the swimming pool
who wishes to go into Hollywood, as a result end up his life in the water.\\
3. Norma won’t come down the stairs until she hears the word 'camera” Illustrate her
deep-emotions of delusions and obsession with her past fame as a silent film star.
Norma has become isolated and disconnected from reality, living in a huge mansion
filled with memories of her fame days in Hollywood.\\
4. Norma's feelings as she walks down the stairs are a mix of pride, hopefulness,
insecurity, loneliness, and delusion because she thinks she has come back to her
place which she waited for 20 years.\\
5. Yes, I do. Wilder conveys a sense of pity, shallowness and compassion for Norma
Doesmond through inviting the audience to empathise with her challenges and
struggles.\\
