\section{Film Techniques}
\subsection{List of Techniques}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.3}
\begin{longtable}{|p{5cm}|p{\dimexpr\linewidth-5cm-2\tabcolsep-2\arrayrulewidth}|}
\caption{Film Techniques and Metalanguage} \label{tab:filmtechmeta} \\
\hline
\rowcolor[gray]{0.9} \textbf{Film technique / metalanguage term} & \textbf{Definition / Effect} \\
\hline
\endfirsthead

\hline
\rowcolor[gray]{0.9} \textbf{Film technique / metalanguage term} & \textbf{Definition / Effect} \\
\hline
\endhead

% your rows
Close-up shot & Tightly frames a person or a subject. Enables the audience to see the emotions of a character or details on a subject. \\
\hline
Extreme close-up & A more intense close-up that might focus on a specific feature. \\
\hline
Mid shot & Usually shows an actor from the waist up. Focus is on the characters but also shows the environment they are in. \\
\hline
Long shot & Used to show a subject from a distance, establishing them within a setting. \\
\hline
Establishing shot & Usually the first shot of a scene to establish the location and environment. \\
\hline
Panning shot & When the camera moves horizontally from its fixed position and mirrors the motion of looking from side to side. \\
\hline
Point of view shot & Mimics what a character in a scene is seeing, putting the audience into their perspective. \\
\hline
Framing & The composition of characters or subjects within a single shot. \\
\hline
Two shot & A medium shot showing two actors in a single frame. \\
\hline
Mise en scène & All elements contained in a single frame (lighting, costuming, positioning, set, props). \\
\hline
Low angle shot & Subject is shot from below eye level to make them appear powerful, dominant, or morally superior. \\
\hline
High angle shot & Subject is filmed above eye level to make them appear vulnerable or weak. \\
\hline
Aerial shot & A shot with the camera positioned directly above a subject. \\
\hline
Over the shoulder shot & A medium close-up shot from behind another character’s shoulder, typically used in conversations. \\
\hline
Diegetic sound & Sound within the world of the film — characters can hear it. \\
\hline
Non-diegetic sound & Sound added in post-production — characters cannot hear it (e.g. soundtrack, narration). \\
\hline
Setting & The environment in which the film is set. Can shift over time. \\
\hline
Motifs & A dominant or recurring prop, image, or symbol (e.g. mirrors, frames, candles, chandeliers). \\
\hline
Zoom in / Zoom out & A change in focal length during a shot to magnify or reduce the subject. \\
\hline
Film noir lighting & Low-key lighting used to emphasise shadows and create strong contrasts. \\
\hline
Three-point lighting & Technique using key light, back light, and fill light to evenly illuminate a subject. \\
\hline

\end{longtable}
\newpage
\subsection{Analysis of Particular Scenes}
\underline{Sunset Boulevard} is perhaps the most famous film about film. A darkly funny yet disturbing noir, it follows washed-up screenwriter Joe Gillis being pulled into the murky world of even-more-washed-up former silent film star Norma Desmond, disingenuously helping with her screenplay. Critical commentary on the film industry is obviously included here, but Billy Wilder’s 1950 film digs deeper to explore the blurred line between fantasy and reality, as well as power, authenticity and self-delusion. Crucially, these themes are often shown in the film’s construction, via the cinematic techniques implemented by Wilder in each scene.\\

This subsection will explore the most important examples of these cinematic techniques. Remember, VCE examiners are on the lookout for students who can offer a close reading of the text they are discussing, giving specific examples of how its creator has constructed it to support their arguments. Just look at the difference between an essay that says:

\begin{quote}
    'Through the final shot of the film, Wilder shows Norma completely succumbing to her fantasy.’ 
\end{quote}

Compared to one that argues:

\begin{quote}
    'Through his utilisation of an increasingly glossy and distorted filter in the ominous final shot, Wilder depicts Norma being completely overtaken by her romanticised fantasy of ‘Old Hollywood’.'
\end{quote}

\subsubsection{Camera Techniques: Shot Types and Angles}
Camera techniques are arguably the primary way that a director will intentionally direct the eye of the audience, directly framing how they view a film. The two most basic ways in which the camera is used for this are through the distance between the subject (what the scene is about) and the camera, or the ‘shot type’ and the ‘camera angle’ at which the subject is being filmed.\\

\begin{figure} [h!]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{ShotType1.jpeg}
    \caption{First Appearance of Norma Desmond}
    \label{fig:enter-label}
\end{figure}

Our first look at Norma Desmond is within the wide shot above, just as Joe Gillis has entered her dishevelled mansion early in the film. As a rule, the introductory shot of a character is always worth closely analysing, as the director typically establishes their characteristics and place within the film’s wider world. \\

Shown above, this distant first look at Norma establishes her distance, both physical and mental, from the world around her. Removing herself from an industry that has long since moved on from her, she is severely out of touch with the reality of the world outside her home. Crucially, as this same shot is from Joe’s perspective, Wilder also foreshadows the more specific character ‘distance’ that will emerge between the two. Here, the audience sees the space Joe will similarly leave between himself and Norma, disingenuously humouring her poor-quality scripts and romantic advances and, therefore, always keeping her ‘at a distance’.

\begin{figure} [h!]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{Shot2.jpeg}
    \caption{The Final Goodbye}
    \label{fig:enter-label}
\end{figure}Another shot conveying crucial information about character relationships is shown when Joe officially ‘loses’ Betty towards the end of the film, refusing to give up his ‘long-term contract’ with Norma. Here, Wilder consciously frames the scene’s subject (Betty) at a distance with a medium shot. Supported by her refusal to make eye contact with Joe and her literal statement that she ‘can't look at [him]’ we again see physical distance between the camera and the subject translating to emotional distance between two characters. The impact of them no longer ‘seeing eye to eye’ is additionally heightened by the clear chemistry they previously demonstrated across the film. \\

\subsubsection{Key Examples of Camera Angles}
\begin{figure} [h!]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{Ang1.jpeg}
    \caption{Opening Title}
    \label{fig:enter-label}
\end{figure}

Just like the introductory shot of a character is worth digging into, the opening shot of a film is also incredibly important to unpack. Sunset Boulevard’s seemingly straightforward opening shot simply includes the film’s title, by showing the real-life Hollywood street. However, notice that we are not seeing a ‘Sunset Boulevard’ street sign (the more obvious choice), but instead a dirty and stained curbside. Further, Wilder shoots this curb from a high angle. Therefore, the film’s opening shot establishes maybe the most central aim of Wilder’s film; offering a critical look at the superficiality and flawed nature of Hollywood. As such, we are literally looking down on the film industry in the first moment of the film, represented by this dirty and unflattering visual symbol of Hollywood. This, therefore, is setting the stage for the satire and critical commentary that will follow. \\

\begin{figure}[h!]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{ang2.jpeg}
    \caption{After Losing Betty}
    \label{fig:enter-label}
\end{figure}
Wilder’s careful use of camera angles is further shown at the end of the film after Betty abandons Joe at the gate of Norma’s mansion. Crucially, this all happened due to the desperate exertion of power by Norma, who called Betty and revealed the details of her relationship with Joe. As such, Wilder shoots Norma at a low angle, as Joe looks up at her haughty gaze. The level of power that Norma has exerted over Joe may seem minimal within the moment, but when we consider what happens next, this shot becomes much more important. On the brink of descending completely into madness and taking Joe’s life, Wilder uses this shot to establish that Joe should be looking up in fear at Norma, and his dismissive and pitiful opinion of her will soon lead to his death.\\


\subsubsection{Mise-en-Scène}
Mise-en-scène is perhaps the most deceptively simple cinematic technique. It involves analysing what appears within a frame and where it has been placed by the director. This includes elements such as the actor’s costumes, the props and the design of the set. Often, mise-en-scène is used to reinforce something we are being told about a character already through the film’s dialogue and acting.  \\

\begin{figure} [h!]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{mes1.jpeg}
    \caption{Joe's Apartment I}
    \label{fig:enter-label}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure} [h!]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{mes2.jpeg}
    \caption{Joe's Apartment II}
    \label{fig:enter-label}
\end{figure}

We can see a key example of characterisation through mise-en-scène early in the film, where the audience’s introduction to Joe Gillis visually communicates his unconcerned and detached attitude, as well as his tendency to settle for something convenient despite its inauthenticity. His being dressed in a bathrobe with the blazing sun outside (and his debt collectors clearly up and doing their jobs) speaks to his slovenliness and uninvested approach to life. The set design within this scene further characterises Joe, with the script directly describing the ‘reproductions of characterless paintings’ that cover his walls. Here, the set arguably provides a visual metaphor for the profit-driven ‘Bases Loaded’ script he is writing at that very moment, later described by Betty as having come ‘from hunger.’ 


\begin{figure} [h!]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{nb1.jpeg}
    \caption{Norma's Bedroom}
    \label{fig:enter-label}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure} [h!]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{nb2.jpeg}
    \caption{Norma's Living Room}
    \label{fig:enter-label}
\end{figure}

Equally, our introduction to the home of Norma Desmond helps establish the key elements of her character. The house is, as Joe describes, ‘crowded with Norma Desmonds’, in the form of countless framed photos of her from her silent film era. These self-portraits constantly looking out onto Norma symbolise the deluded fantasy world she has placed herself in. They both show how this world is based around her still being a youthful and famous actress, and that this delusion is maintained through Norma only communicating inwardly, refusing to face the reality of the outside world.



ZZPDFEMBED ShotAnalysis.pdf ZZEND






\paragraph{Further Mise-en-Scène Analysis}
\subparagraph{Set Design and Locations}
\begin{itemize}
    \item Norma Desmond’s Mansion: The opulent yet decaying mansion is central to the film. It represents Norma’s past glory and her current isolation. The grand, dark interiors are filled with outdated, Gothic furnishings, creating a contrast with the modern Hollywood world outside. This setting emphasises the disconnect betweenN Norma’s delusions and reality.
    \item Joe Gillis’ Apartment: Joe’s modest apartment contrasts sharply with Norma’s mansion. Its simplicity reflects his struggle and the reality of his life as a struggling screenwriter. The clutter and starkness of the apartment underscore Joe's ﬁnancial and emotional instability.
    \item Paramount Studios: The scenes at Paramount Studios, vibrant and bustling,symbolise the thriving, contemporary Hollywood that has left Norma and her era behind. The contrast between the lively studio and Norma’s decaying home highlights the industry's constant evolution and neglect of past stars.
\end{itemize}

\subparagraph{Lighting}
\begin{itemize}
    \item Low-Key Lighting: especially in Norma’s mansion. This creates deep shadows,enhancing the ﬁlm’s noir atmosphere. The darkness of the mansion’s interiors mirrors Norma’s mental state and her descent into madness.
    \item High-Key Lighting: In contrast, scenes set in the modern parts of Hollywood, such as the Paramount Studios, use high-key lighting. This bright and even lighting reﬂects the vibrancy and energy of the current film industry and emphasises the disconnect between the past and present
\end{itemize}

\subparagraph{Costume Design}
\begin{itemize}
    \item Norma Desmond’s Costumes: Norma’s costumes are elaborate and dramatic,reflecting her former star status and her attempt to maintain an image of grandeur.Her wardrobe includes vintage, glamorous outfits that emphasise her clinging to a bygone era. The costumes also highlight her delusions of grandeur and her disconnect from reality.
    \item Joe Gillis’ Costumes: Joe’s wardrobe is more casual and practical, reflecting his status as a struggling screenwriter. His clothing is less ﬂashy, indicating his lower position in the Hollywood hierarchy and his struggle to make a name for himself.
\end{itemize}

\subparagraph{Camera Angles and Framing}
\begin{itemize}
    \item Close-Ups: The ﬁilm frequently uses close-ups, particularly of Norma’s face. These shots reveal her emotional state and add to the film’s psychological intensity. Thec lose-ups emphasise her vulnerability, madness, and the contrast between her internal and external worlds.
    \item Wide Shots: Wide shots of Norma’s mansion often include extensive background details, emphasising the grandeur and the sense of decay. These shots also highlight the mansion’s isolation and Norma’s detachment from the world.
\end{itemize}

\subparagraph{Composition}
\begin{itemize}
    \item Symmetrical and Asymmetrical Composition: The film uses both symmetrical and asymmetrical compositions to reflect character relationships and themes.Symmetrical compositions often highlight Norma’s desire for control and order,while asymmetrical compositions reflect the chaos and imbalance in her life.
    \item Depth of Field: The use of depth of field helps separate characters from their environment, particularly in scenes where Norma’s fantasies clash with reality. This technique underscores the distance between her delusions and the real world.
\end{itemize}



\subsubsection{Symbols in Scenes}
\begin{figure} [h!]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{chimp.jpeg}
    \caption{Chimp}
    \label{fig:enter-label}
\end{figure}
One of the more seemingly inexplicable parts of Wilder's film actually contains one of its most important symbols, with Norma’s pet monkey playing a key foreshadowing role from beyond the grave. The chimp, a pet owned and trained by Norma to amuse her, leaves a vacant role that Joe will gradually fill after having unknowingly interrupted its funeral. From this point in the film, Joe is manipulated, or ‘trained’, by Norma to entertain and provide companionship to her. Naturally, Joe also ends up dead within the bounds of Norma’s estate, with this symbol, therefore, foreshadowing the full trajectory of his character. All of this is directly alluded to through Joe’s description of the ‘mixed-up dream’ he has the night of the funeral, imagining ‘an organ [player]’ and the ‘chimp…dancing for pennies’ that he will soon become.\\

\begin{figure} [h!]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{organ.jpeg}
    \caption{Organ}
    \label{fig:enter-label}
\end{figure}
This naturally brings us to the organ itself, which serves as a physical reminder of the unflattering parts of the new role Joe must play. Included after Joe wakes from his ‘mixed-up dream’, the shot above frames Max’s organ-playing hands as massive and overpowering, as the much-smaller Joe storms in demanding to know why his ‘clothes and things’ were moved to Norma’s house without his say-so. Crucially, Norma then reveals that she ordered this action and that Joe's apartment debts are ‘all taken care of’, hand-waving his attempt at grasping back some control and dignity by proposing it be ‘deduct[ed]...from [his] salary’. This scene reveals the symbolic role the organ plays within Sunset Boulevard, reminding Joe of the shameful and powerless role of the ‘pet monkey’ that he now fills, as well as what he will be ‘dancing’ for.\\

\subsubsection{Allusions}
Finally, we come to allusions, one of the techniques that \underline{Sunset Boulevard} is most famous for. Allusions refer to anytime something from outside the world of the text is referenced, including other texts and real-world people, places, events, etc. Biblical and mythological allusions are commonly found in fiction, but references to something closer to our world can often bring a degree of realism to certain texts, working to strengthen their social commentary. \\

\paragraph{Cinematic Allusions}

\begin{figure} [h!]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{para.jpeg}
    \caption{Paramount Studios}
    \label{fig:enter-label}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure} [h!]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{para 2.jpeg}
    \caption{Sheldrake's Office}
    \label{fig:enter-label}
\end{figure}

Being a film about film, \underline{Sunset Boulevard} naturally contains many allusions to other films. However, Wilder does not shy away from adding an extra level of realism to his references to the film industry. Central to this is the use of the real (and still functional) Paramount Pictures studio to which Joe attempts to sell his clichéd baseball script. Notably, this is the studio that actually released Sunset Boulevard, all of which adds a self-deprecating edge to the satire of the film industry these scenes contain. The scene where the cigar-chomping Paramount executive, Mr Sheldrake, cynically suggests that changing Joe’s film concept to a ‘girls' softball team’ might ‘put in a few numbers’, packs an extra punch due to the use of the real film studio, therefore, showing the effect of this allusion in strengthening the film’s satire.\\

Allusions to specific films are additionally used for humorous purposes and character development. For instance, take Joe’s dry observation that the extravagance of the funeral for Norma’s pet means that he ‘must have been a very important chimp’, perhaps the ‘great-grandson of King Kong’. Here, Joe’s sardonic and witty character is revealed to the audience. Additionally, these kinds of references further place the film firmly in the world of real Hollywood, again working to strengthen the satire it offers of this industry. \\


\paragraph{Literary Allusions}
Similarly, allusions to the world of literature flesh out both the characters and the world of Sunset Boulevard. The most stand-out example of this is the allusion to Charles Dickens’ classic novel Great Expectations. Here, Joe muses that the ‘unhappy look’ of Norma’s house reminds him of ‘Miss Havisham’ from this text. This is a character, who, after being abandoned by her fiance, refuses to change her clothing and lives secluded in a ‘rotting wedding dress’. Havisham directly parallels Norma, being a tragic figure immovably stuck in the past, with Norma's excessive placement of young self-portraits being reminiscent of Havishman’s insistence on keeping her house’s clocks at the exact time she received her letter of marital rejection. Therefore, this comparison to the Dickens character, who engages in a more exaggerated version of Norma’s behaviour, seeks to highlight just how detached Norma is from reality through her attempts to live in the past, implying that what she is doing is just as deluded as refusing to remove a rotting wedding dress. Further, the eventual fate of Miss Havisham within Great Expectations, with her wedding dress catching fire and leaving her as an invalid, foreshadows Norma’s similar descent to invalidity through her madness.

\newpage
\section{Symbols}
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{The Mansion}  
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Symbolises Norma's decaying fame and psychological isolation  
        \item A crumbling structure, 'out of beat with the rest of the world'  
        \item Reflects her detachment from reality and the passage of time  
    \end{itemize}

    \item \textbf{Black-and-White Cinematography}  
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Enhances contrast between Norma's world (dark, stagnant) and Betty's world (bright, full of life)  
        \item Reinforces themes of nostalgia and decay  
    \end{itemize}

    \item \textbf{Spotlights}  
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Symbolise attention, former fame, and Norma’s obsession with being seen  
        \item Used dramatically when Norma sits in deMille’s chair – a ghost-like figure in the light  
    \end{itemize}

    \item \textbf{Gates and Barriers}  
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Represent isolation, exclusion, and entrapment  
        \item Norma uses her home as a prison to keep herself—and Joe—separated from the real world  
    \end{itemize}

    \item \textbf{The Monkey’s Funeral}  
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Symbolises Norma’s lost career and her inability to let go of the past  
        \item Gothic mis-en-scène highlights the absurdity and tragedy of her delusion  
    \end{itemize}

    \item \textbf{Cars}  
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Represent control, freedom, and social mobility  
        \item Joe’s loss of his car mirrors his loss of independence when he becomes trapped in Norma’s world  
    \end{itemize}

    \item \textbf{Close-Ups of Norma’s Face}  
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Reflect her silent film origins and the over-expressiveness required in that era  
        \item Symbolise her inability to adapt and her clinging to outdated forms of identity and performance  
    \end{itemize}

\item \textbf{The Salome Script}  
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Symbolises Norma’s desperate attempt to reclaim her stardom and control her narrative  
        \item Reflects her delusion and obsession with youth, power, and seduction  
        \item A vehicle through which she imagines her triumphant return, though the industry has no real interest in it  
    \end{itemize}

\item \textbf{Paramount Studios}  
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Symbolises the modern, ever-evolving world of Hollywood that has left Norma behind  
        \item Represents both nostalgia and rejection—once her kingdom, now a place where she is merely tolerated  
        \item A cold contrast to the warmth of Norma’s imagined fame  
    \end{itemize}

\item \textbf{Max’s Role as Butler}  
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Max, who was once a respected director and is now reduced to being Norma’s butler,symbolises the destructive nature of Hollywood.
        \item His loyalty to Norma and his willingness to play into her delusions highlight how far people can fall in the industry and how they cling to past identities.
    \end{itemize}

\item \textbf{Max’s Gloves}  
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Symbol of Max’s servitude and control—he handles every aspect of Norma’s life with careful precision  
        \item Suggests a barrier between Max and reality, as he preserves the illusion with delicacy and ritual  
        \item Reinforces his role as both protector and enabler of Norma’s fantasy  
    \end{itemize}

\item \textbf{Norma’s Clothes}  
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Luxurious and extravagant, but styled from a bygone era—symbolising her refusal to move forward  
        \item Represent how Norma costumes herself in a fantasy, masking the reality of her decline  
        \item Reinforce the image of a star who is 'big', but only in her own memory  
    \end{itemize}

\item \textbf{The Pool}  
    \begin{itemize}
        \item The pool, where Joe’s body is found, symbolises the false allure of Hollywood’s glamour.
        \item Initially a symbol of success and luxury, it ultimately becomes a symbol of death and disillusionment.
        \item Joe’s lifeless body floating in the pool illustrates the deadly consequences of chasing hollow dreams.  
    \end{itemize}

\item \textbf{The Film Projector}  
    \begin{itemize}
        \item The film projector represents Norma’s obsession with the past.
        \item As she watches her old films, the projector becomes a symbol of her inability to move on from her glory days, trapping her in a loop of nostalgia and delusion. 
    \end{itemize}

\item \textbf{The Final Scene}  
    \begin{itemize}
        \item The final scene, where Norma believes she is on set filming a movie, symbolises her complete disconnection from reality.
        \item It also serves as a critique of Hollywood, where the lines between reality and illusion often blur, leading to tragic consequences.
    \end{itemize}




    
    
\end{itemize}


\section{Written Exemplars}

\subsection{Sample 1 - High Scoring \underline{Sunset Boulevard} Essay I with Annotations}


ZZEXP Sunset Boulevard Essay I with Annotations ZZEXPEND


Topic:
\textbf{\underline{Sunset Boulevard} reveals the dark side of celebrity and fame. Discuss.}\\

\textit{Introduction}

Exposing the powerlessness of naïve ‘dream[s]’ of fulfilment against the toxicity of celebrity which has ‘seized [their] heart[s]’, (1) Billy Wilder’s quintessentially American (2) film \underline{Sunset Boulevard} (1950) poignantly unveils the dark side of fame that is inextricable from Hollywood’s allure (3). Compounded by the skilful overlaying of a gothic lens, this noir mystery (4) performs as a deep meditation on the brutality of this business, and a sardonic, yet subtly sympathetic, depiction of the victims of its endless cycle of sadism (5). Through his intelligent blend of fiction and meta-cinema (6), Wilder’s iconoclastic opus emerges as a powerful vivisection of the dangerous illusions of stardom.\\

\textit{Annotations:}

(1) Beginning the piece with an embedded quote adds a degree of complexity and immediately highlights to markers that you have an understanding of the text. It also improves the poetic and sophisticated nature of the text, almost as if it is a literature essay, thus separating your piece from other purely analytical responses.

(2) Recognising the context from the outset also showcases one’s understanding of the wider societal issues and themes.

(3) An immediate reference to the topic is necessary. If the topic isn’t explicitly or implicitly addressed throughout the response, it cannot receive a high mark.\\


\textit{Body Paragraphs}

Personifying this complex world as a dystopian nightmare, Wilder satirically contextualises the hidden horrors of Hollywood (1). Immediately referencing a classic trope of film noir, (2) a disturbing amalgamation of fantasy and sobering realism is established through the eerie posthumous narration of this ‘poor dope’, with this non-linear structure exposing this inevitable tragedy from the outset. Heightening this strange sense of unease, (3) the foreboding non-diegetic string music of Franz Waxman’s pertinent score (4) warns the audience of this ensuing horror. Indeed, \underline{Sunset Boulevard} itself can no longer be hopefully associated with fame and luxury; rather, its disconcerting introduction in a gutter with ‘dead leaves’ and ‘scraps of paper’ alludes to its burgeoning corruption, as literally emphasised by the titular finiteness of ‘sunset’. Ominously towering over this decrepit street, the silhouetted palm trees embody personified judges (5) of the ‘distorted’ and ‘blown out’ stories that ‘Hollywood columnists’ weave, as fortified by the intense chiaroscuro lighting (6). Similarly, (7) although swimming pools were an iconic image of glamour, the floating corpse ironically exposes that this perpetual chase of fame is a deadly enterprise. As a poignant reflection of this danger, the toxic relationship between Joe Gillis – our cynical antihero – and Norma Desmond – the stereotypical antagonist of a femme fatale – (8) is inherently doomed. This Gothic synthesis of old and new Hollywood thematically foreshadows their demise, as both are dangerously delusional: one is confined by their obsolescence, the other ensnared in the shallowness of the present. Symbolising the subordination that defines their affair, the loss of Joe’s car is a ‘matter of life and death’ that resolutely deforms him into an item of Norma’s possession, as fame is too often synonymous with prostitution. Subverting the traditional gender stereotypes of their time, Edith Head’s pertinent costumes of shared leopard print – a predominant motif of Norma’s wardrobe, which is also reminiscent of her silent-era glamour – illuminates the oppression that this pursuit of celebrity has manufactured. (9) In a house with no locks, Joe is already irreparably caught in the predatory grip of Norma’s atrophic talons, and thus, by extension, trapped by the noxious allure of Hollywood. Functioning as a provocateur to brazenly ‘bite the hand that feeds him’ (Mayer) (10) Wilder controversially deplores the deceptive façade of fame through his pioneering direction.\\

\textit{Annotations:}

(1) Open with dependent clauses elevates the piece by shifting the pacing. Adverbs are a great opportunity to briefly note tone. Every topic sentence should reference the topic.

(2) Link back to the genre to reflect your wider understanding; most students can only analyse what they see, without considering how this fits into the genre as a whole

(3) It is sophisticated to examine how this makes the audience feel (i.e. unease).

(4) Music is often forgotten in analysis. Films provide opportunities for different types of analysis. Don’t forget the soundtrack/camera angles/acting/outfits.

(5) Develop unique interpretations of symbols. Most students would ignore or neglect the palm trees, simply viewing them as a background item. When you provide unique views and analyses, markers are always impressed.

(6) As mentioned before, having an inventory of metalanguage relating to lighting is greatly sophisticated.

(7) Using linking words like ‘similarly’ underlines to markers that your evidence has been specifically chosen to flow cohesively. Listing dissimilar evidence can weaken the sophistication of your writing

(8) Showing an understanding of character tropes is sophisticated.

(9) Referencing outfits and costume choices can elevate your writing above other pieces, especially when you recognise their purpose as motifs and their significance at the time, such as in relation to gender stereotypes.

(10) The inclusion of one or two external quotes throughout your piece shows a wider contextual understanding and advances your piece to an academic or literary text.\\

Challenging his fervent condemnation of Hollywood’s evil, Wilder manufactures a hopeful alternative that appears to exist untarnished by celebrity (1). Joe and Betty’s relationship functions as a tender antithesis to the toxically phantasmal alliance of Joe and Norma: this mystery is also an ‘Untitled love story’, saving it from merely being a lurid horror (2). Momentarily, Wilder permits Joe to escape his ‘peculiar prison’ of empty fame, returning to the corporeal world of Artie’s New Year’s gathering, an event imbued with a jollity that is crosscut against the solemn falseness of Norma’s decrepit mansion. From her entrance to the film, Betty Schafer embodies the forgotten passion for truth, ‘itching with ambition’ and presenting a virtuous alternative to Hollywood’s amorality. Illuminating her integrity, Schafer’s intelligence protects her from being deterred by the toxicity that is fundamental to this rapacious industry, as revealed when she is repeatedly misnamed by the sexist producer. Establishing a meta critique of the film industry, Wilder’s script sees this ‘sweet kid’ affirming that ‘pictures should say a little something’ rather than capitalising on being ‘glossy’ and slick’. Pertinently, whilst Norma is a victimised by-product of fame’s lethal illusion, Betty is an astute examiner who ‘know[s] all the plots’, acknowledging and embracing the deception at Hollywood’s core: she likes this ‘cardboard’ and ‘hollow’ town ‘better than any street in the world’. Further emphasising her innocence and purity, Betty’s practical attire of simple skirts and sweaters oppose Norma’s characteristic wardrobe of glamour to highlight the dichotomy between these women. Betty refuses to accept that amorality is inextricable from fame; rather, she empowers to write her own story, both literally and figuratively, reminding us that ‘life can be beautiful’. Hence, she is the perfect counterpart to Joe’s cynicism, embodying both his possible redemption as a man and Hollywood’s rehabilitation as an industry. Her ‘cubby hole’ of an office holds more promise and passion than the entire ‘white elephant’ of Norma’s mansion, highlighting the importance of truth. Their parallel storyline offers an escape for Joe, dislocating him from Norma’s persecution and empowering him to pursue his creative aspirations for motives nobler than shallow renown. Indeed, their witty dialogue – ‘Don’t you sometimes hate yourself?’ – and romantic chemistry symbolise this newfound sense of ambition, concurrently contrasting the sombre superficiality of Norma’s exchanges. Unveiling the wavering silver lining of celebrity, their classic Hollywood kiss offers a reprieve of romance. Emerging as perhaps the most admirable character of this satire, Betty’s genuineness empowers the audience to believe in an industry that prioritises creativity and love over dangerous materialism.\\

(1) The structure of your essay is ultimately your decision, but having a paragraph that grapples/disagrees with the topic sentence can add an interesting layer of analysis. It provides an opportunity to explore other paths, characters and ideas, and differentiates your work from other responses.

(2) As mentioned previously, it is important to recognise that the film is not one-dimensional: it can be multiple conflicting genres at once.

Notice how this paragraph is based on comparisons and contrasts (e.g. Artie’s party vs Norma’s mansion, and Betty vs Norma). This is far more effective in conveying understanding and nuance than simply analysing characters and settings individually. It is important to consider their wider role and implications within the film.\\

 

By the catastrophic climax of Wilder’s horrifying creation, it is overwhelmingly transparent that the dark side of fame will ultimately eclipse its idealistic counterpart, as this hope cannot survive unadulterated in the poisonous atmosphere of Hollywood. (1) Although Joe and Betty’s bond strives to pervert this amorality, even their romance eventually succumbs to the deception of this industry, for they cannot ‘get away with it’. Evidencing the moral dilapidation that is endemic to Hollywood, Betty’s chastity is defiled by the contagiousness of Joe’s shallow illusions; although she ignorantly maintains that she has ‘never heard any of this’, her newfound awareness of the toxicity of celebrity is palpable. As ominously revealed in the opening scenes, our protagonist’s flawed ‘long-term contract’ catalyses his spiritual and physical death, for the amoral motives of fame cannot be without consequence. Foreboded by the dramatic orchestral music, William Holden’s (2) purposeful characterisation of Joe’s premortem trance mirrors the enduring ignorance of Hollywood’s victims. Indeed, his final reach to his briefcase is analogous of the inherent desire of these hopefuls to continue to nourish fame’s deceptive image, even when that fatal bullet has been lodged deep in their unexpecting back. Rather than shattering Norma’s agonising delusions, this murder gruesomely precipitates her resolute descent into madness. Deprived of her moment of anagnorisis, as the role of Salome consumes her, Wilder’s use of mirrors as a motif conveys Norma’s dislocation of real and imaginary. Adorned with royal and theatrical music, Wilder’s staging of Norma’s slow-motion descent of her staircase in her bedazzled dress disturbingly typifies an embodiment of Hollywood’s ghastly underbelly erupting to the surface. With each step on her terminal pursuit of fame, this deranged ghost exits her corporeal sphere, her hyperreality finally becoming inescapable, as ‘there’s nothing else’. Surrounded by spectators yet entirely alone, as the camera closes in on her manic face – with her rigid claws and psychotic eyes blaming the culpable audience – she blurs into this phantasmal purgatory, destroying the alleged integrity of this business. As this ‘dream she had clung to so desperately’ finally ‘enfolded her’, Wilder’s writing expresses that celebrity, or the lack thereof, possesses the omnipotence to corrode a famed icon into an insane killer. Ultimately, \underline{Sunset Boulevard} serves as a dire caution to the horrors of Hollywood, as the ‘price’ of this stardom will always be ‘too high’. (3)\\

(1) Ensure that, even if your middle paragraph does provide an alternate view, the final paragraph expresses your overarching conclusion. Complexity and equivocation is important, but so is developing strong opinions.

(2) Referencing actors’ names proves that you have done extensive research.

(3) Referencing actors’ names proves that you have done extensive research.

\subsection{Sample 2 - Example Contention and Body Paragraph}


ZZEXP Sunset Boulevard Contention and Body Paragraph ZZEXPEND



Topic
\textbf{'The only villain in \underline{Sunset Boulevard} is Hollywood.' To what extent do you agree?}


\textit{Contention}

Wilder critiques Hollywood’s self-interest, its obsession with youth and its ruthless pursuit for profit over their writers’ artistic intentions, yet he also acknowledges that succumbing to these demands, can be highly immoral and destructive.\\


\textit{Body Paragraph}

Wilder’s critique of Hollywood’s self-interest is exemplified through the decaying mansion, a visual metaphor for the discarded silent era stars. Norma Desmond, ‘the waxworks’, and Max personify how 1940’s Hollywood has relegated these former stars and directors to what resembles a gothic museum. Norma’s boudoir, draped in heavy fabrics and her sculpturally carved ‘gondola’ style bed, is a visual manifestation of her psychological stagnation, as a boat can’t move without water, just like Norma can’t move on from her former status. This is reinforced by Seitz’s camerawork of Norma who is often framed behind curtains, doors, or within mirrored reflections, signifying the prison like barriers she faces from the current era. Her Isotta-Fraschini, also a symbol of her former status, further reinforces stagnation as the car is immobile and a relic of the past. It is ironic that this old car is the only reason she returns to 1940s Hollywood, after receiving a call from Paramount studio, not from deMille whom Norma believed should have called, but Cole who wants her car as a prop ‘for a Bing Crosby film’, rather than Norma Desmond’s delusional role as Salome to be directed by deMille. Living in her delusion of a ‘return’ as ‘Salome’ in her self-written script, where she will play the lead role of a teenage girl at the age of fifty, Norma is exploited by Joe for some quick cash to pay off his car debt, demonstrating how people in the industry of Hollywood succumb to immoral actions in order to keep their dreams alive. In turn, Norma exploits Joe for his script editing and youthful addition to her life, and as a replacement for her dead chimp pet. The use chiaroscuro lighting demonstrates the interplay of light and darkness that underscores this exploitation that occurs. Thus, Wilder signals how both Hollywood of the 1940s, and former stars like Norma of the1920s, who were led to believe that stars never die because 'that’s what makes them a star,” can lead good people to desperate immoral acts, and severe mental illness.\\

\newpage

\subsection{Sample 3 - High Scoring \underline{Sunset Boulevard} Essay II}


ZZEXP Sunset Boulevard Essay II ZZEXPEND



\textbf{'\underline{Sunset Boulevard} shows that the pursuit of fame is a perilous journey. Discuss.”}\\

Billy Wilder’s 1950 noir film, \underline{Sunset Boulevard}, scrutinises the complexity of the Hollywood machine, and through the lens of the grim, sinister underbelly of the famous and fortunate, shatters the glamorous façade fed to the people. By drawing on the personal aberrations of characters and the delusional artefacts which arise with the pursuit of fame, Wilder comments on the fragile nature of stardom, and creates a sense of distrust between the film industry and the ever-elusive entertainment realm. The film reinforces an atmosphere of entrapment silent film actors as those the wisps of her eminence, a B-list script writer from Ohio who aims to keep his distance from the flashy and aspiring master script writer who falls into the Hollywood cookie cutter. By juxtaposing characters helps Wilder reflect the paradox of human nature and portray the foreboding dangers associated with unattainable dreams in the film industry. Through his characters and film genre, Wilder asserts that notoriety is perilous and far less rewarding that what is believed.\\

\underline{Sunset Boulevard}’s Joe Gillis, played by renowned actor William Holden acts as the perfect noir protagonist; cynical, aggrieved and pursued by a mob of sorts. However, Joe is far from a treacherous, undercover cop, black market infiltrating protagonist that Wilder offers other film offer. Unlike most films of the time, Wilder employs meta elements, having famous directors play directors, and legendary, but ageing silent actors such as Buster Keaton, to play themselves. This allows for him to abstractly comment on the brutality of Hollywood, the moral corruption kept so hidden from the public, and sets a sense of realism when inserting Joe Gillis in scenes with these characters. In order to boldly mimic the public of the personalities of fame, Wilder ties Joe’s story line based on a tragic tale as old as time; Icarus. Icarus is a tale of tragedy, a young boy who tried to escape Crete, grew arrogant, flew too close to the sun, and died in the Icarian Sea. Wilder presents Joe Gillis as Icarus, a tale of peril and hubris. By mirroring Icarus’ tragedy through Joe’s story line, with him escaping the sluggish debt collectors 'when things were tough”, becoming Norma Desmond’s gigolo, growing arrogant and eventually a 'has-been” with his lifeless body floating face down in a pool. Wilder showcases that danger associated with catching a 'break” in Hollywood, with tragedy being the likelier outcome. With '[projects] that turned out to be night”, and the misfortunes associated with Joe’s big break into Hollywood, Wilder comments on the complex, manipulative nature of Hollywood, reinforcing the ghastly horrifying truth about his industry for individuals who aren’t old accomplished Caucasian men.\\

While Billy Schader is by no means at fault for allegorical character, Wilder intentionally leaves her story as two dimensional and unfinished, favouring to emphasis the way that he represents most other women in the entertainment industry who do not succeed in these careers. Because of Betty’s duality in representing the unrepeatable aspect of women in Hollywood, and a child of nepotism, who spent 'ten years” on 'dramatic lessons, dictation and dancing” to ascend to stardom. Wilder portrays the frustration of women who want to become screenwriters, directors, and producers. Wilder, through Betty’s technique of Joe to work with her on his script 'bases loaded”, shares his own frustration on making a break in the perilous industry of Hollywood. He embodies his own frustrations and fears through betty, with many parallels between them including the fear of both Betty and Wilder are also shunned from acting due to their looks. Their connections, modern mannerisms, and Betty’s open and vocalised mind is crafted to portray to the audience of the life that may be had should she choose to proceed without acting and instead to fill her life with purpose. Wilder shows Betty’s line to proceed with a fulfilling life not through acting but also leading a fulfilling life, unlike other individuals in the film.\\

Wilder continues to explore this in the hazardous stardom hierarchy that many an actress is victim to, and which does not reflect the innocence and pleasantry of 1950’s woman. Norma Desmond, played by Gloria Swanson, encapsulates this idea. The narrator sets her up with golden names with silent films with 'just her” [face!]. Through Norma’s status as the 'greatest star” who 'made” millions at 'the lovely age of 17”, Wilder throws us in with Hollywood’s pessimistic, backward relationship in Hollywood, placing women stars far more than a role or a 'vehicle”, he dehumanises and contextualised Norma as a silent film actress who was unable to 'get to the talkies”. His account is laced with '100,000 dollar fan letters” which chases her youth, willing as far to kill and obtain Joe after his 'unused” past self, yet self-deluded as she keeps the paper headlines on clips of camera technologies and lighting, including a scene in which an old crewmate of Norma turns the spotlight onto her, before being told to 'return that to where it belongs”. The spotlight shinning down on Norma, paired with a high angle to make her look small, helps symbolise the beginning of the end. It shows Norma succumbing to years of pressure and pressure that was once enveloped in the warmth of her former years, but now has shattered the Hollywood machine. In the end, Wilder chalks up Norma as Narcissus. He shows a woman damaged by an industry that once enveloped her, a woman so afflicted by vanity, egotism, and self-delusion that she is a shell of her former self. Through a deluded Norma, playing an unlike character, and a dramatic story line, with an open end, Wilder caveats his audience of the precariousness and toxicity of the film industry.\\

Although \underline{Sunset Boulevard} is a film centred in the noir and macabre, highlighting the underworld of Hollywood, the ominous and disturbing truths of the famous and fortunate, Wilder portrays contrasting characters, with hopeful endings for some, and deserving, melancholic endings for others. These disparities allow for wilder to abstractly comment on his industry, portraying the notion that no true member of Hollywood is ever as innocent, and despite the dubious actions of his characters, draws a sense of sympathy for those who exploit others. Above all, Wilder allows for conflicting morals to take centre stage, often prompting his audience to takes sides, to showcase the complexity and multidimensional paradox of human nature. Through tragedy, and familiar tales, Wilder rewards courage, resilience and individuality, while disapproving of those embroidered into the Hollywood clothe, leading them to untimely outcomes, and others to fulfilling lives.\\

\subsection{Sample 4 - High-Scoring \underline{Sunset Boulevard} Essay III}


ZZEXP Sunset Boulevard Essay III ZZEXPEND


\textbf{Topic}\\
How does Sunset Boulevard explore the corrupting effects of Hollywood and the desperation it causes?\\

\textbf{Response}\\
Although a grimly cynical film, Billy Wilder’s \underline{Sunset Boulevard} presents a critical portrayal of the effects selfishness and desperation can have on characters. Through its backstudio narrative, the film explores the corrosive nature of a heartless system that mistreats its subjects, and the desperate means necessary to survive in such an industry. By the end of the film, Wilder begins to envision an alternative but concludes by exposing the entrapping nature of a corrupt system.\\

The Hollywood industry is constructed as a corrupt and heartless environment that acts as a catalyst for characters’ desperation. In the opening shot of the film a stark juxtaposition is formed between the glamour and corruption of Hollywood, the name connoting glamour and wealth, while the placement of the sign in the gutter indicating the opposite. Wilder presents Hollywood as corrupt because it promises fame and fortune but cannot deliver to the vast majority of people. From the outset of the film, the struggling screenwriter Joe experiences the cutthroat nature of Hollywood. SitÝng on his bed in a robe, Joe is ‘grinding out’ scripts in a desperate attempt to recover his career. Wilder highlights the dire nature ofhis situation, and how the promises Hollywood once made to the previously successful script writer are no longer being delivered upon. When debt collectors come to repossess his car, Joe’s perilous attempt to cling on to it serves as a parallel to his instability and financial insecurity. Joe's statement that ‘[he needs] about two hundred and ninety dollars and [he needs] it real quick’ works to develop this idea further, highlighting his desperation due to the importance of his car that is symbolic of success and wealth. Wilder exposes Hollywood’s corruption by exploring the false promises of fame and fortune that are never delivered upon.\\

In order to cope with the mistreatment of Hollywood and the ramifications of rejection,
characters seek to survive through desperate means and selfish behaviours. Prior to his
arrival at Norma’s mansion, Joe has been corrupted by Hollywood, making him utterly
desperate and vulnerable. Being rejected by the industry forces him into desperate and
unethical behaviours. He begins ‘rehashing’ old scripts, abandoning artistic integrity in his
desperation to figure out how to succeed in the industry. Wilder examines the effects of
desperation on relationships when Joe begins to exploit his contacts, using them as ‘assets’.
Gillis' deceptive nature is revealed through his exploitation of others, which is analogous to
Hollywood's commodification of its participants. Joe refers to the fact that Sheldrake had
‘always liked [him]’, displaying his intent to leverage personal relationships for his
advantage. Wilder utilises Norma’s house as a reflection of Norma herself, the place being a
‘white elephant’, and a decrepit shrine to the past. Norma’s construction of this shrine is
her way of dealing with the rejection of an industry that previously gave her validation and
identity. Joe and Norma both create a narrative that suits their own personal interests,
exploiting and manipulating one another. Norma is able to exploit Joe’s vulnerability, his
need for money. She buys Joe’s companionship and loyalty, buying him a ‘vicuna’ coat, and
seeing him as a young man that can make her feel young and attractive again. Joe exploits
Norma’s vanity and instability to get what he believes will be easy money to pay off his
debts and reestablish himself in the industry. Gillis is driven to deceive Norma by the
corrupt Hollywood system that causes his desperation for financial aid. Both characters are led to manipulate the other by their desire for personal gain, acting in self-interest due to
their desperation. This behaviour is analogous to the shallow values of Hollywood, living in
an alternate reality and manipulating each other for their own gain.\\

Whilst Wilder demonstrates an alternative approach to selfishly delusional living, the tragic
ending of the film ultimately reveals that there is no real escape from the corrupt and
Hollywood system for those that are too deeply entrenched. Wilder pictures Betty as a
counterpart to Norma, she is an earlier version of Norma, one that is ‘like all us writers
when we first hit Hollywood’ and has not yet been corrupted by the system. Betty
represents authenticity and artistic integrity, adamant in her belief that ‘film’s should say
something’. She is aware of the hollowness of Hollywood referring to it as ‘all cardboard’.
Wilder presents her as the antithesis to Norma, a beacon of hope that is realistic where
Norma is delusional. Wilder offers hope through Betty, an alternative to living in an
alternate reality. Furthermore, her working relationship with Joe is more collaborative and
transparent. She wants to ‘do it together’ and does not attempt to manipulate and leverage
his talent for her own profit. Wilder contrasts Betty’s behaviour to the other characters, as
she is willing to work with Joe rather than exploit him. However, although her sincerity
connects with Gillis, Wilder’s portrayal of Joe’s subsequent behaviours undermines any
optimism proffered by his relationship with Chafer. While Joe reconciles with reality and
attempts to leave Hollywood, he is ultimately unable to extricate himself from Norma’s
grasp, a representation of Hollywood’s hold over him. For Norma, Joe’s departure would
mean the end of her alternate reality. Joe’s death as he leaves Norma’s entrapment
underscores all characters’ inability to escape the corrupt Hollywood system. Even Joe, the
character that finally realised the effect Hollywood has had on him, is not allowed to escape,
with this realisation instead leading to his eventual demise. In line with the prevailing
cynicism throughout the movie, Joe meets his demise just as he attempts to break free.
Following Joe's death, Norma's descent into madness is complete. She retreats further into
her fantasy world, believing that she is starring in a movie scene rather than facing the grim
reality of her actions. The final words of Norma and of Hollywood itself are ‘no one leaves a
star’. Her last words are a testament to the way the system entraps and manipulates its
participants.\\

\underline{Sunset Boulevard} is a scathing criticism of the cruelty of Hollywood and the corrupting
effects of a mistreating system that causes desperation and selfishness. Relationships based
on manipulation and self-interest in addition to characters exploiting others emphasises the
film’s intention the expose the deceitful nature of Hollywood. Furthermore, while some
hope is generated, the corrupting nature of Hollywood overshadows any positive features,
overall revealing the corrosive effects that selfishness and desperation have on characters
within the industry.\\

\subsection{Sample 5 - High Scoring \underline{Sunset Boulevard} Essay IV}


ZZEXP Sunset Boulevard Essay IV (VCAA 2023 Topic) ZZEXPEND


\begin{quote}
\textit{'Sunset Boulevard shows that the pursuit of fame is a perilous journey.' Discuss. (VCAA Exam 2023)}
\end{quote}

Released towards the end of 1950, Sunset Boulevard, Billy Wilder’s incisive expose of the Hollywood industry and American society more broadly was met with scathing reviews by critics who felt Wilder had maligned the all-American pursuit of fame. Indeed, Wilder criticises in his film how the pursuit of fame often creates vulnerability to the exploitation of powerful figures, and more concerningly, the loss of oneself to discontent and even madness. Despite this, Wilder endorses those in the industry who pursue meaning over fame, and illustrates how such a path can allow one to avoid the perils of the industry. Ultimately, Wilder suggests that while Hollywood is a dangerous place, it can be navigated by those who seek to produce good work.

In the movie, the pursuit of fame leaves characters open to exploitation and even endangers their lives. Wilder reminds his audience that predatory characters wait around every corner to profit off the naivety of ‘the talent’. Joe’s callous agent tells him he should be grateful to be short on money, since “the greatest things were written on an empty stomach.” Despite professing to be Joe’s “friend”, Morino represents the most exploitative and profit-driven parts of deep Hollywood, always lurking in wait for the next “10 percent” cut. Wilder symbolically illustrates the dirtiness of the industry right from the opening of the film, when he depicts the titular street name ‘Sunset Boulevard’ written not atop a street sign, but instead in the gutter. This placement represents the lowlife aspects of Hollywood, with the fallen leaves in particular subtly hinting at the industry’s fallen and dead stars, while the high camera angle looking down on it tellingly indicates Wilder’s disgust towards the industry. The potentially fatal outcomes of chasing fame are reiterated in the next scene when the audience is presented with Joe’s dead body floating face down in a pool, while photographers crowd trying to get a good picture. Speaking from beyond the grave, Joe muses with dry empathy that “the poor dope…always wanted a pool.” As the cameras flash, he adds that he “got himself a pool – only the price turned out to be a little high.” This quote epitomises from the outset the ultimate peril of seeking acclaim: death. Indeed, it was only through his murder that Joe achieved not only the “pool”, but also the success and fame this represents in Hollywood. Joe’s haunting narration keeps the thread of death running through every scene, reminding the audience that a reckless pursuit of fame can have potentially fatal consequences.

Perhaps even more sadly, Wilder disappointedly comments on the ability for the Hollywood industry to produce cynicism, discontent, and even madness. In his search for recognition, to “get [his name] up there”, Joe loses his desire to do anything other than chase cash. Although there is an indication Joe has “some talent”, Joe insists “that was last year; this year [he’s] trying to make a living”. As much as he feels hard-done-by in the industry, Joe here reveals that he himself has chosen to give up writing what is “true” in the name of trying to make money. Ironically, this leads Joe to a cycle of producing work that was either “too original” or “not original enough”, but in any case “it didn’t sell”. Through his cynical quips through the film and the lugubrious, jaded tone of Joe’s voiceover, Wilder conveys how for artists who approach Hollywood with the aim of making it big, only burnout and disappointment await. Likewise, Norma loses herself in the pursuit of public adoration. Despite beginning “as a lovely little girl of 17” full of “courage and wit and heart”, Norma became a “terror to work with” after years of “press agents working overtime” on her. Norma’s inability to separate her stage self from her real self is established early on through the thick makeup accentuating her lips and eyes, reminiscent of the silent film style. This makeup and her exaggerated facial expressions become only more extreme as the film continues, symbolising Norma’s mental decline because of her inability to adapt. The wish for fame and “a return” is a manifestation of Norma’s profound desire for attention, represented by attempts to entertain Joe with the “Norma Desmond Follies”, painfully incongruent with her ageing self. Watching Desmond’s cosmetic procedures and her increasing insanity is like watching a trainwreck in slow motion, and by the end of the film when Norma famously cries she’s “ready for [her] close-up”, the audience is left with a profound sense of horror for how the desire for the adoration of “those wonderful people out there in the dark” can lead actors to totally lose touch with reality. The parallel arcs of Norma and Joe both are symbolic of Wilder’s broader message that a desire for fame leads people to lose their sanity and meaning.

Although Wilder’s depiction of the dangers of chasing fame is unforgiving, the director also suggests that those who seek meaning in Hollywood are able to withstand the pressures those only seeking material reward buckle under. Even when faced with Joe’s cynicism, asking “who wants true [stories]? Who wants moving?” Betty maintains she “[wants] to write” rather than be a reader “all [her] life”. Betty’s ambition is not to achieve fame or wealth, but to develop herself, create a career, and ultimately make movies which “say a little something”. Betty is also able to bounce back from failure. As insistent as Joe is that Betty must “weep for those lost close-ups” after she was rejected as an actress, she responds that the whole ordeal “taught [her] a little sense”. Because she does not seek publicity or wealth, Betty is able to escape the disappointments Joe and Norma can not. The extras at Artie’s party, too, are positive, despite being “writers without a job, composers without a publisher”. When Joe enters their room on New Year’s, they are singing happily. It is not that their experience of Hollywood is different: indeed, they sing about how “Hollywood for [them] ain’t been so good”, and Artie jokingly breaks the fourth wall by commenting he can only afford “three drinks per extra”, a reminder of the financial limitations of Hollywood, particular for those on its lowest rung. However, the extras maintain a positive attitude, using humour to cope, and view this hardship more as a liveable reality of Hollywood rather than as a personal affront. Even DeMille’s extras, despite being old and conventionally unattractive, outlast Norma in Hollywood because they are willing to work in the background, creating the scene, rather than starring in it. Thus, Wilder illustrates that while Hollywood is rife with danger, this peril is avoidable for those who choose to work on movies for the love of it, rather than to achieve money or fame.

In Sunset Boulevard, Wilder pulls back the curtain on the multitude of personalities who seek to work in the movie business. Those who seek fame ultimately lose either their physical lives, or their mental stability. However, those who choose meaning and passion over flimsy materialism are able to survive the perils of Hollywood. Thus, though the original critics may not have seen it, Wilder ultimately leaves his audience with the American Dream: that if you do what you love, one day you might just make it.

\section{Practice Analytical Text Response Topics}

\newpage
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Practice Analytical Text Response Topics}

1. '\underline{Rainbow's End} is a bitter condemnation of colonisation.' Discuss.\\

2. 'A real home is where there are people looking out for each other' How does \underline{Rainbow's End}
explore the importance of home?\\

3. "An empty place for Papa Dear." The men, in Harrison's play are marginalised and have little impact on the play's events. To what extent do you agree?\\

4. The main hardships characters face in the play are caused by gender inequality. To what extent do you agree?\\

5. 'The dream sequences in \underline{Rainbow's End} are the characters' only escape from the depressing
reality of life.' Discuss.\\

6. 'Gladys and Dolly are torn between the world of white Australians and their Indigenous Australian heritage.' Discuss.\\

7. How does Harrison use setting to reflect characters' experiences in \underline{Rainbow's End}?\\

8. “Your mum chops wood! With an axe and all!” How is strength displayed throughout the play?\\

9. ‘Hope is a powerful tool for change.’ To what extent does this apply to \underline{Rainbow’s End}?\\

10. “Que sera sera.” \underline{Rainbow’s End} argues that people should accept their fates and not strive for better futures. To what extent do you agree?\\

11. “I don’t want my mother to be served last in the butcher’s.” How does \underline{Rainbow’s End} explore how individuals can survive and overcome their experiences of discrimination?\\

12. ‘\underline{Rainbow’s End} suggests that family relationships can be destructive.’ To what extent do you agree?\\

13. ‘\underline{Rainbow’s End} tells a tragic tale about the loss of culture and identity.’ To what extent do you agree?\\

14. ‘\underline{Rainbow’s End} is a bitter condemnation of colonisation.’ Discuss.\\

15. \underline{Rainbow’s End} highlights the futility of hoping for a better future in a prejudiced world. To what extent do you agree?\\

16. ‘The dream sequences in \underline{Rainbow’s End} are the characters’ only escape from the depressing reality of life.’ Discuss.\\

17. “A real home is where there are people looking out for each other.” How does \underline{Rainbow’s End} explore the importance of home?\\

18. ‘Characters in \underline{Rainbow’s End} are torn between the world of white Australians and their Indigenous Australian heritage.’ Discuss.\\

19. “I want what any mother, black, white or brindle, wants for her daughter.” Harrison presents mother–daughter relationships as complex. Discuss.\\

20. “An empty place for Papa Dear.” The men in Harrison’s play are marginalised and have little impact on the play’s events. To what extent do you agree?\\

21. The main hardships characters face in the play are caused by gender inequality. To what extent do you agree?\\

22. Ultimately, Harrison presents an uplifting message about individuals’ capacity to overcome adversity. Discuss.\\

23. How does Harrison employ dramatic techniques to highlight the struggle and despair faced by Indigenous Australians?\\

24. How does Harrison utilise setting to demonstrate the hardships that Indigenous Australians encounter?\\

25. To what extent does \underline{Rainbow’s End} show that optimism is justified in times of injustice?\\

26. “We demand the right to control our own destiny.” \underline{Rainbow’s End} suggests that vulnerable people can control their own destinies. Do you agree?\\

27. The characters in \underline{Rainbow’s End} share similar experiences but do not see things the same way. To what extent do you agree?\\

28. In \underline{Rainbow’s End} the women discover the power of having a voice. Discuss.\\

29. Relationships can be a catalyst for personal transformation and empowerment. How does Harrison explore the influence of relationships in her play?\\

30. “Are you saying you’d rather live in a humpy by the river? When I’m promising you the world?” In \underline{Rainbow’s End}, attempts to be helpful are often unsuccessful. Discuss.\\

31. Oppression and other negative human experiences can have lasting impacts on an individual’s life and outlook. How is this idea explored in \underline{Rainbow’s End}?\\

32. How does \underline{Rainbow’s End} explore ideas about the ‘other’?\\

33. Ultimately, Jane Harrison’s play \underline{Rainbow’s End} is a study in what it means to be human. Discuss.\\

34. ‘Cultural connection is the greatest resistance in \underline{Rainbow’s End}.’ Discuss.\\

35. In what ways does Harrison explore how tensions between groups shape individual and collective experiences?\\
