\section{Introduction and Context}

\subsection{Author Background}
Jane Harrison, a descendant of the Muruwari people, is an acclaimed playwright, novelist, and critic. As a child, she “always loved books” but was disheartened by the absence of Aboriginal representation in Australian literature. In writing \underline{Rainbow's End}, Harrison sought to give voice to those experiences that had long been silenced or marginalised. Drawing upon oral histories from Aboriginal Elders, she captures the resilience and humour of families who lived on “The Flats” and later at Rumbalara—the First Nations housing estate in Mooroopna.\\

Harrison’s work strongly condemns the substandard housing and systemic neglect endured by First Nations families during the 1950s. Through her writing, she positions herself as a conduit for truth-telling, giving dramatic expression to both the hardships and triumphs of Aboriginal life. Her aim, as she has stated, is to use fiction as “an important way for First Nations people to have a voice.”\\

\subsubsection{Analysis}
Harrison’s authorial perspective intertwines personal heritage and political conviction. By dramatizing the everyday realities of Aboriginal families, she reclaims history from the margins and rehumanises voices often reduced to statistics or stereotypes. Her compassionate yet incisive storytelling challenges audiences to confront both the past and its lingering presence in modern Australia.\\


\subsection{Historical Context}
The colonisation of Australia, beginning with the arrival of the First Fleet in 1788, unleashed devastating consequences for First Nations peoples. The ensuing Frontier Wars—violent conflicts between Aboriginal people and British settlers—resulted in the massacre and displacement of countless Indigenous communities. Throughout much of the twentieth century, government policy continued to pursue assimilation and cultural eradication.\\

One of the most tragic outcomes of these policies was the forced removal of Aboriginal children from their families. These children, later known as the Stolen Generations, were stripped of their language, names, and cultural identity. Many were placed in institutions or adopted into white families, where they were exploited as unpaid labour and suffered emotional and physical abuse.\\

In \underline{Rainbow's End}, Harrison threads this history through the lives of Nan and Gladys, whose trauma reflects the generational wounds inflicted by colonial intervention. Gladys’s experience of being taken from her family and made to work as a domestic servant mirrors the broader policy of forced assimilation. Her obsession with Dolly’s education becomes a personal act of restitution—an attempt to reclaim what was taken from her by systemic injustice.\\

\subsubsection{Analysis}
By embedding the historical trauma of dispossession within intimate family narratives, Harrison compels her audience to recognise the enduring emotional and social consequences of colonisation. Her portrayal of the Stolen Generations not only condemns past wrongs but also foregrounds the resilience and agency of those who survived them.\\


\subsection{The Flats and Cummeragunja}
\textit{Cummeragunja}, founded in 1888 on Yorta Yorta/Bangerang Country, represented both refuge and resistance for Aboriginal people. Initially a self-sufficient farming community, it was later undermined by the New South Wales Protection Board, which seized control in 1915. Aboriginal workers were paid in meagre rations, and funds generated by their labour were appropriated by the Board. By the 1930s, disease and poverty had decimated the community.\\

In 1939, the residents of Cummeragunja staged a historic protest—the first mass strike by Aboriginal Australians. Many left the mission and settled on “The Flats,” a floodplain near Mooroopna and Shepparton. Despite harsh living conditions, including humpies made from “flattened kero tins” and exposure to extreme weather, the community retained its cultural vitality and kinship ties. By the early 1950s, around 300 people lived there.\\

\subsubsection{Analysis}
The Flats serve as both a literal and symbolic setting in \underline{Rainbow's End}. They embody dispossession yet also community, poverty yet perseverance. Through this setting, Harrison contrasts the instability imposed by colonial structures with the cultural rootedness of Aboriginal families. The Flats become a testament to the capacity of a people to adapt, resist, and endure despite systemic neglect.\\

\section{Analysis of the Historical and Cultural Context in \textit{Rainbow's End}}

\subsection{Overview}
Jane Harrison’s \underline{Rainbow's End} emerges from a landscape shaped by colonisation, segregation, and resilience. Set in 1950s Australia, the play reflects both the harsh realities and quiet triumphs of Aboriginal families living under systemic discrimination. The historical context is indispensable to understanding the characters’ identities, the injustices they face, and the endurance that defines their humanity. Harrison’s work gives voice to the unsung heroes—especially women—who sustained their communities despite persistent marginalisation.\\

\subsection{Acknowledgement and Purpose}
The play’s thematic foundation begins with an acknowledgment of Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander peoples as the traditional custodians of the land. This recognition situates \underline{Rainbow's End} within an ethical framework that honours history and memory. Harrison’s intent is not merely to tell a story but to preserve a cultural legacy and challenge erasure. Commissioned to highlight Aboriginal women’s experiences, the play transforms domestic life into political narrative, demonstrating that resistance often begins within the home.\\

\subsubsection{Analysis}
By embedding acknowledgment into the narrative fabric, Harrison bridges past and present. The act of remembering becomes both art and activism, inviting audiences to reflect on how storytelling can restore dignity to those history overlooked. Her focus on Aboriginal women redefines heroism as endurance and nurturing in the face of dispossession.\\

\subsection{Historical Background: Colonisation and Segregation}
The origins of the play’s conflict trace back to the legacy of colonisation and government policies such as the Protection Acts, which sanctioned segregation and the forced removal of children. Aboriginal families were confined to reserves like Kamarra Ganja, where strict management and limited access to resources entrenched poverty. These lived realities form the backbone of Harrison’s narrative world, revealing how state control sought to diminish autonomy while families fought to preserve it.\\

\subsubsection{Analysis}
Harrison transforms historical record into lived drama. The reserve system becomes a stage for both oppression and resilience. By drawing from documented injustices, she crafts a narrative that demands empathy and accountability. The audience confronts how legislative cruelty translated into generational trauma.\\

\subsection{Life on the Reserves and the Flats}
After the walk-off from Kamarra Ganja, many Aboriginal families relocated to makeshift settlements known as “the flats.” These spaces, though impoverished, became hubs of culture, laughter, and communal strength. The families’ persistence in maintaining cultural traditions symbolises defiance against systemic neglect. In this environment, children learned resilience as a form of inheritance, and hope became both a necessity and an act of protest.\\

\subsubsection{Analysis}
The depiction of life on the flats captures a paradox — material deprivation coexists with emotional and cultural richness. Harrison celebrates community cohesion as a radical response to marginalisation. The domestic setting transforms into a microcosm of resistance where identity is preserved through humour, storytelling, and maternal leadership.\\

\subsection{Government Policy and the Metaphor of the Rainbow}
The government housing schemes of the 1950s, such as those in Rumbalara, promised integration but perpetuated segregation through inadequate living conditions. The metaphor of the rainbow recurs as a powerful symbol throughout the play. To the characters, the “end of the rainbow” represents dreams of equality and acceptance — visions that remain just out of reach. This unattainable horizon mirrors the historical struggle for recognition endured by Aboriginal Australians.\\

\subsubsection{Analysis}
The rainbow encapsulates the duality of hope and despair. It is both a dreamscape and a mirage — a reflection of the distance between aspiration and reality. Harrison employs this image to expose the cruel optimism embedded within promises of assimilation. The characters’ pursuit of the rainbow becomes a metaphor for the broader national pursuit of reconciliation still incomplete.\\

\subsection{The 1950s Social Landscape}
Post-war Australia was marked by conservative values, economic expansion, and racial inequality. For Aboriginal peoples, this period intensified the contradictions between visibility and exclusion. Women, in particular, bore the burden of navigating these intersecting oppressions. Through Gladys, Dolly, and Nan Dear, Harrison portrays how Aboriginal women negotiated identity, motherhood, and dignity within a society determined to silence them.\\

\subsubsection{Analysis}
Harrison’s representation of 1950s Aboriginal womanhood dismantles stereotypes of passivity. Her characters inhabit a world of limited choices yet wield moral and emotional power. Their courage lies not in grand rebellion but in persistence — in cleaning, teaching, loving, and speaking truths that white Australia refused to hear.\\

\subsection{Conclusion}
Understanding the historical and cultural context of \underline{Rainbow's End} enriches every reading of the play. Harrison’s depiction of segregation, displacement, and resilience transforms history into human experience. The struggles of the Banks and Dear families are not isolated but emblematic of a broader narrative of survival. The play stands as both testimony and challenge: to remember, to acknowledge, and to act. In recognising the truths of the past, Harrison invites audiences to see the enduring strength of Aboriginal identity — not as tragedy, but as triumph.\\



\subsection{Structure of the Play}
\underline{Rainbow's End} opens \textit{in medias res}, plunging the audience directly into the aftermath of a flood. This sudden beginning creates an atmosphere of crisis and displacement, immediately reflecting the instability that defines the characters’ world. The play’s structure is unconventional—beginning with a prologue, followed by scenes divided into parts A and B, reflecting fragmentation and lack of control in the characters’ lives.\\

Harrison integrates dream sequences that blur the boundary between fantasy and reality. These imaginative interludes, such as Gladys’s visions of meeting the Queen or Dolly’s aspirations for education, express both hope and futility. The juxtaposition of dreams and deprivation highlights the gap between aspiration and circumstance.\\

Two narrative threads underpin the play: Gladys’s political awakening and Dolly and Errol’s romance. Each culminates in an epiphany—Gladys’s courageous confrontation with the Shire Council and Dolly’s recognition of Errol’s unconscious racial assumptions. The play concludes with two significant symbols: Nan’s dream of Dolly’s wedding and the song “Que Sera, Sera.” The latter, appearing at both the beginning and end, transforms from a cynical reflection of government indifference into a subtle anthem of hope and change.\\

Harrison’s structural choices mirror her thematic concerns. The fragmented narrative echoes the disrupted lives of Aboriginal families, while the interweaving of dreams and realism underscores the endurance of hope amid systemic oppression. The dual climaxes—Gladys’s activism and Dolly’s assertion of independence—affirm the possibility of transformation both personal and political. The cyclical use of “Que Sera, Sera” redefines fatalism as faith: a belief that the future, though uncertain, can still hold renewal.\\

\section{Structure and Themes in \textit{Rainbow's End}}

\subsection{Overview}
Jane Harrison’s \underline{Rainbow's End} is constructed as a two-act play divided into short, interlinked scenes that illuminate the resilience, struggles, and dreams of First Nations families in 1950s Australia. The fragmented structure mirrors the fractured social and political realities of Aboriginal life, where moments of humour and hope coexist with hardship and prejudice. Harrison’s manipulation of stage direction, song, and dialogue builds dramatic tension and emotional intimacy, guiding the audience through a tapestry of generational experience.\\

\subsection{Act Structure and Thematic Design}
Unlike traditional plays divided by chapters, \underline{Rainbow's End} unfolds through acts and scenes that offer glimpses into domestic and emotional moments. This episodic format reflects the uncertainty that governs the Dear and Banks families’ lives. Scenes often end abruptly or transition during moments of crisis, symbolising the instability caused by poverty and discrimination. The two major narrative threads — Gladys’s political awakening and Dolly’s coming-of-age romance — develop in parallel, converging at the climax where both women redefine their strength and identity.\\

\subsubsection{Analysis}
Harrison’s two-act structure underscores her social commentary. The brevity and rhythm of scenes intensify audience empathy while exposing the invisible pressures imposed by systemic inequality. By structuring the play around fragmented lives, Harrison creates a dramatic form that mirrors cultural and emotional dislocation — yet also reveals endurance and connection within the Aboriginal matriarchal family.\\

\subsection{In Media Res and Dramatic Openings}
The play begins \textit{in media res} amid the chaos of a flood, a moment both literal and symbolic. Accompanied by the recurring song “Que Sera, Sera,” the opening sequence immediately juxtaposes hope with helplessness. This song returns at the play’s conclusion, binding the narrative through a motif that evokes the tension between aspiration and acceptance.\\

\subsubsection{Analysis}
Beginning in the middle of action thrusts the audience into the characters’ reality without exposition, reflecting the immediacy of their struggle for survival. The flood motif encapsulates emotional and social inundation, while the musical refrain serves as both comfort and irony — a lullaby masking pain.\\

\subsection{Dream Sequences and Juxtaposition}
Dream sequences punctuate the play, revealing the characters’ inner lives and unfulfilled desires. Gladys’s fantasy of meeting the Queen becomes a recurring image of colonial aspiration — an impossible vision shaped by internalised subservience and social exclusion. These dreams contrast sharply with the family’s reality in a riverside shack, where dignity must be defended daily.\\

\subsubsection{Analysis}
Through dream imagery, Harrison critiques the illusion of social mobility within a racist society. The juxtaposition of fantasy and deprivation exposes the psychological toll of colonisation, while simultaneously affirming the imagination as a site of survival and self-worth.\\

\subsection{Character Growth and Narrative Resolution}
Both major storylines reach their culmination in acts of self-realisation. Dolly challenges her mother’s fixation on external validation, forcing Gladys to confront the limits of her own deference. This confrontation propels Gladys toward activism, culminating in her powerful declaration at the Shire meeting: “We demand proper schooling … The white people too — they need to be educated about us” (p.197). Though the play ends with personal growth, systemic barriers remain unresolved, reflecting the persistence of structural inequality.\\

\subsubsection{Analysis}
The dual transformation of Gladys and Dolly demonstrates Harrison’s belief in intergenerational empowerment. Their evolution symbolises a shift from private endurance to public assertion — a necessary transition for community progress. The incomplete resolution emphasises realism over idealism, reinforcing that the struggle for equality continues beyond the stage.\\

\subsection{Dramatic Intensity and Unseen Forces}
Harrison’s use of short, tightly constructed scenes sustains dramatic tension and emphasises the relentlessness of external pressures. Many of the most significant events occur offstage, their impact conveyed through dialogue and reaction. The small cast heightens intimacy while unseen antagonistic figures — landlords, officials, and employers — personify the faceless nature of institutional discrimination.\\

\subsubsection{Analysis}
By keeping oppression unseen yet omnipresent, Harrison amplifies its psychological weight. The absence of visible villains shifts focus toward resilience, love, and humour as tools of survival. The minimal staging and interwoven dialogue allow audiences to inhabit the emotional pulse of the characters’ world, where silence often speaks louder than confrontation.\\

\subsection{Circular Structure and `Que Sera, Sera'}
The play opens and closes with `Que Sera, Sera', and the framing is more argument than decoration. On its surface the song counsels fatalism -- whatever will be, will be -- yet everything between the two renditions shows the women refusing passivity: Gladys finds her public voice, Dolly chooses her own future, Nan lets new truth revise old fear. The circle closes with an echo rather than a repetition: Gladys's early reassurance ``It'll be all right'' returns in the final scene in Dolly's mouth. Spoken after flood, humiliation and assault, the line is no longer naive comfort but a tested position -- hope as something inherited, examined and deliberately kept. Structure itself carries the play's meaning: what recurs is not circumstance but resolve.\\

\subsection{Conclusion}
The play’s structure functions as both narrative device and political metaphor. Through acts, scenes, dreams, and songs, Harrison crafts a world where fragmented experiences reveal collective truth. The rhythm of the play mirrors the cycles of struggle and renewal within Aboriginal communities, inviting readers and audiences to engage with the ongoing dialogue between past injustices and present resilience. Understanding the form of \underline{Rainbow's End} thus deepens appreciation for its portrayal of identity, endurance, and hope in mid-twentieth-century Australia.\\


\newpage
\section{Audience and Purpose in \textit{Rainbow's End}}

\subsection{Overview}
Jane Harrison’s \underline{Rainbow's End} (2005) was written to educate and engage both Indigenous and non-Indigenous audiences through its portrayal of three generations of Aboriginal women — Nan Dear, Gladys, and Dolly — living in 1950s Victoria. Harrison’s purpose extends beyond recounting hardship; she celebrates resilience, dignity, and familial love amidst oppressive social structures. The play invites audiences to confront the consequences of colonisation and assimilation while recognising the strength of community and cultural continuity.\\

\subsection{Purpose of the Play}
Harrison’s central purpose is to foreground the untold stories of Aboriginal women and their everyday heroism. Through the intimate domestic setting of the Dear and Banks family, she challenges the invisibility imposed upon First Nations peoples by historical narratives. Her play functions as both cultural preservation and political statement — illustrating that Indigenous survival is not defined by victimhood but by persistence and pride.\\

Harrison’s writing embodies the principle that art can both memorialise and mobilise. By humanising historical trauma, she transforms policy and prejudice into personal experience, allowing audiences to empathise rather than merely observe. The play’s tone oscillates between warmth and sorrow, comedy and critique, mirroring the emotional complexity of survival.\\

\subsubsection{Analysis}
Through \underline{Rainbow's End}, Harrison asserts that storytelling is a mode of reclamation. Each scene restores voices historically silenced by government policy and social discrimination. Her purpose is not only to depict resilience but to invite truth-telling — to reposition Aboriginal women as custodians of memory, agency, and identity.\\

\subsection{Intended Audience}
\underline{Rainbow's End} addresses a dual audience. For non-Indigenous viewers, it acts as an awakening — revealing the lived experiences obscured by whitewashed Australian history. For Indigenous audiences, it affirms continuity and pride, validating intergenerational strength and the endurance of culture.\\

By positioning humour and tenderness alongside hardship, Harrison ensures accessibility for classroom and theatre audiences alike. The play encourages educational engagement, prompting critical reflection among students while offering Aboriginal spectators the comfort of recognition. The inclusion of intergenerational female perspectives fosters empathy across age groups and cultural divides.\\

\subsubsection{Analysis}
Harrison’s nuanced approach ensures that the play operates on multiple emotional and intellectual levels. Non-Indigenous audiences are confronted with complicity in structural injustice, while Indigenous audiences are offered representation that transcends trauma. This balance of challenge and affirmation defines the play’s enduring impact within Australian theatre and education.\\

\subsection{Historical Awareness and Cultural Responsibility}
The play’s authenticity stems from Harrison’s meticulous research and personal connection to her Murrawari heritage. Drawing upon oral histories, newspaper archives, and community interviews, she constructs a historically grounded yet emotionally resonant narrative. Her inclusion of real figures, such as Pastor Doug Nichols, situates the fictional world within an authentic cultural framework.\\

This fidelity to truth underscores Harrison’s responsibility as both playwright and cultural custodian. Her depiction of the 1950s assimilation era reflects an ethical commitment to remembrance — to ensuring that audiences understand the policies that fractured families and communities, and the resilience that rebuilt them.\\

\subsubsection{Analysis}
Harrison’s creative purpose merges documentation with dramatisation. She transforms history into a living dialogue between past and present, ensuring that Aboriginal experience is neither mythologised nor forgotten. By embedding historical authenticity within intimate storytelling, she teaches audiences that understanding cultural identity begins with acknowledging historical reality.\\

\subsection{The Role of Representation in Theatre}
For Harrison, representation is activism. The play reclaims space within the Australian canon for Aboriginal voices, particularly those of women. Through humour, vernacular speech, and emotional candour, she dismantles stereotypes and restores humanity to communities too often reduced to statistics. \underline{Rainbow's End} thereby becomes a vessel for truth-telling, reconciliation, and education.\\

\subsubsection{Analysis}
Representation in \underline{Rainbow's End} is not symbolic but lived — an act of cultural assertion. Harrison’s purpose is fulfilled when audiences see Aboriginal women not as passive subjects of history but as authors of their own narratives. The play’s enduring presence in school curricula ensures that these voices continue to educate, inspire, and transform generations of Australians.\\

\subsection{Conclusion}
Harrison’s \underline{Rainbow's End} unites purpose and audience in a dialogue of empathy, education, and recognition. Her portrayal of three Aboriginal women transcends their fictional identities to stand as symbols of cultural endurance. By crafting a story grounded in truth and delivered with compassion, Harrison reshapes the Australian stage into a site of reconciliation — where understanding begins through listening, and hope endures through storytelling.\\



\newpage
\section{Language in \underline{Rainbow's End}}

\subsection{Overview}
Language in Jane Harrison’s \underline{Rainbow's End} is a powerful vehicle for expressing identity, class, and cultural conflict. The characters’ speech patterns reveal their personal histories and social positions, while Harrison’s authorial voice is embedded within their dialogue, reflecting her own commentary on racial inequality, resilience, and generational change. Language, therefore, functions not only as a mode of communication but also as an indicator of power, prejudice, and pride within the 1950s Australian context.\\

\subsection{Nan Dear}
Nan’s language is colloquial, humorous, and often sharply ironic. When she informs Gladys that she “won’t be needing any of them encyclops” (p.136), she dismisses education’s relevance to her family’s daily struggles, revealing both her pragmatic worldview and the limited opportunities afforded to her generation. Her unpolished speech reflects her lower-class status and lack of formal education—an outcome of systemic exclusion faced by Aboriginal Australians.\\

Yet Nan’s language also injects comedy into the play. Her dry humour and literal phrasing create moments of levity that highlight resilience in hardship. When mocking Gladys’s excitement about meeting the Queen, Nan’s irony underscores the absurdity of colonial class structures. Through Nan’s speech, Harrison reveals both the pain and pride of a woman grounded in survival, whose humour becomes an act of quiet rebellion against social inequality.\\

\subsubsection{Analysis}
Nan’s dialect situates her within a marginalised social class while asserting authenticity and self-respect. Her vernacular speech becomes an emblem of identity, opposing assimilationist pressures that sought to erase Aboriginal voice. Harrison uses Nan’s language to celebrate oral tradition and defiant humour, challenging notions that education alone defines intelligence.\\


\subsection{Dolly Banks}
Dolly’s language reflects her education and confidence but also her awareness of the social hierarchies constraining her. She is direct and assertive, particularly when confronting racism. Her rebuke of Errol’s remark about “you people” (p.141) exposes his unconscious bias and the normalisation of racial division. Likewise, her challenge to her mother — “You’re always telling me to stick up for myself, but when do you, eh?” (p.162) — forces Gladys to examine her internalised inferiority.\\

Dolly also code-switches, adopting Indigenous vernacular when she threatens to “kick Ester’s boys up to moon” (p.137), using humour and familiarity to assert belonging within her community. Her fluency, both in educated English and colloquial Aboriginal dialect, embodies her dual identity as a bridge between two worlds. Her closing assertion to Errol — that she would never trade her family or heritage for a “better life” (p.172) — epitomises pride and defiance.\\

\subsubsection{Analysis}
Dolly’s language signifies empowerment through articulation. Harrison crafts her speech to demonstrate that mastery of language is not assimilation but assertion. Dolly’s ability to speak for herself—and against injustice—makes her a vehicle for Harrison’s advocacy of Indigenous self-determination. Her linguistic versatility becomes symbolic of cultural strength rather than concession.\\



\subsection{Errol Fisher}
Errol’s halting and awkward speech reflects both his nervousness and his ignorance. As an encyclopedia salesman who depends on persuasion, his incompetence is ironically comic. When he awkwardly notes that Dolly’s glass is “made out of a can” (p.140), he unwittingly insults her. His language often reveals class and racial privilege cloaked in politeness — exemplified in his use of “you people” (p.140) to generalise Aboriginal Australians.\\

His verbal missteps and tone-deaf expressions highlight his sheltered upbringing and lack of cultural awareness. Over time, however, Errol’s language softens and becomes more considerate, mirroring his emotional growth and respect for Dolly’s world.\\

\subsubsection{Analysis}
Harrison uses Errol’s speech to critique the well-meaning but ignorant paternalism of mid-century white Australia. His progression from patronising to empathetic reflects the broader social journey toward understanding and equality. His linguistic awkwardness thus serves as a tool of both comedy and commentary.\\



\subsection{Gladys Banks}
Gladys’s voice develops from timid dreamer to assertive activist. During the Rodney Shire meeting, her passionate declaration — “We demand proper schooling … The white people too — they need to be educated about us, and our ways” (p.197) — becomes a turning point where her speech echoes Harrison’s own political message.\\

Her earlier language is often infused with fantasy and aspiration: daydreams of the Queen’s attention or Dolly’s graduation soften her struggles. Yet by the play’s end, Gladys has found a political and moral voice that embodies the collective frustrations of Indigenous Australians denied dignity. Her transition from domestic to public speech represents empowerment through language.\\

\subsubsection{Analysis}
Through Gladys, Harrison demonstrates the transformative power of speech as activism. The act of “speaking up” replaces passive dreaming with agency. Gladys’s voice becomes that of her people — articulate, righteous, and unafraid — asserting the necessity for visibility and reform.\\


\subsection{The Royals}
The Queen’s language is formal, elevated, and exclusionary. Her reference to Australia as “the birthplace of the nation” (p.124) erases 65,000 years of Aboriginal civilisation, while her statement about standing “at last among Australians” implicitly refers only to white citizens. This polished rhetoric embodies colonial arrogance.\\

By contrast, the Duke’s language is more spontaneous and unguarded. His offhand question — “Where’s Father?” (p.127) — humanises him but also underscores the royal couple’s detachment from the lived realities of those they visit.\\

\subsubsection{Analysis}
Harrison employs the Royals’ diction as social critique. Their words — ceremonious, detached, and self-congratulatory — expose the gulf between imperial symbolism and the Indigenous suffering it conceals. The Queen’s non-inclusive language mirrors the nation’s non-inclusive identity at mid-century.\\


\subsection{The Radio Announcer}
The radio announcer’s language is superficially cheerful yet profoundly condescending. In describing the royal visit, he mentions a demonstration of “how to throw a boomerang” and marvels that it “really does come back” (p.128). His tone trivialises a complex cultural artefact, reducing Indigenous skill to entertainment for the royal couple.\\

\subsubsection{Analysis}
The announcer’s commentary encapsulates institutional racism disguised as enthusiasm. Harrison’s use of irony highlights how mainstream media perpetuated ignorance, framing Aboriginal culture as novelty rather than heritage. His words parody the way national pride excluded First Nations people.\\


\subsection{Structural and Symbolic Language}
Beyond dialogue, Harrison uses language in her stage directions and symbolism to reinforce meaning. Descriptions of the Dear family’s humpy — “waterlogged” and “mud-spattered” (pp.123, 176) — visually communicate poverty and marginalisation. The title itself, \underline{Rainbow's End}, draws on the myth of a pot of gold, suggesting the illusion of prosperity and the unattainability of a promised better life.\\

The reference to “Somewhere Over the Rainbow” from \textit{The Wizard of Oz} introduces an ironic hopefulness. Its lyrics — “the dreams that you dare to dream really do come true” — echo the family’s optimism but also question the realism of those dreams in a society unwilling to grant equality.\\

\subsubsection{Analysis}
Harrison’s manipulation of both literal and figurative language constructs a layered critique of Australia’s racial landscape. The interplay between hope and futility in her metaphors underscores the central tension of the play — between aspiration and limitation, imagination and reality. Language, whether spoken, written, or symbolic, thus becomes both a weapon and a wound.\\




\newpage

\section{Analysing Stage Directions}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Analysing Stage Directions}

In a play, the author speaks twice: once through the dialogue, and once through everything the audience is shown. Harrison's stage directions -- lighting changes, props, physical business, silences -- are deliberate authorial constructions, which makes them premium evidence in a Section A essay: quoting one instantly signals that you are treating the text as something \textit{made}. The rule, however, is absolute: you are never writing \textit{about} stage directions. You are writing about character, relationships, ideas, concerns and values -- and using stage directions as evidence to prove your interpretation. Identifying a direction and stopping is the mid-band trap; the marks live in what it proves.\\

\subsection{The Evidence Ladder}
Before a stage direction goes anywhere near your essay, climb it through five rungs: What is the direction? What does the audience literally see? What does that reveal? What idea, concern or value does it support? And finally -- what does Harrison want the audience to understand? Consider the difference in these three sentences about the moment Dolly and Errol look at each other ``longingly'': saying ``Harrison uses the stage direction `longingly''' merely identifies; saying it ``shows that Dolly and Errol like each other'' merely explains; but writing that ``Harrison's direction that the pair look at each other `longingly' allows their developing affection to be communicated visually, positioning their relationship as a source of emotional possibility'' analyses -- it moves from choice to meaning to purpose. Reach for precise analytical verbs as you climb: constructs, establishes, reveals, emphasises, reinforces, contrasts, symbolises, positions, foregrounds, intensifies, challenges.\\

\subsection{A Worked Ladder: The Blue Jewels}
Take the direction from Gladys's dream in Act 1, Scene 7: ``A tap appears from nowhere and from it flows blue jewels in an approximation of water.'' What we see: water presented as treasure. What it reveals: the intensity of Gladys's longing for something most of the audience has never once thought about. What idea it supports: dignity, housing and inequality -- the politics of the play made visible in a single image. What Harrison wants understood: that the ordinary becomes precious only when it has been withheld, so the audience is made to feel the family's deprivation rather than merely be told about it. Built into a paragraph, the ladder becomes: argument (Gladys's hopes are shaped by material deprivation), evidence (the ``blue jewels''), analysis (the theatrical image renders running water magical), significance (the depth of her longing for basic security), link (what many audiences regard as ordinary becomes a powerful aspiration for those living without it).\\

\subsection{Directions Worth Knowing Closely}
Some of the play's directions carry entire arguments within them. The Prologue's opening -- ``gloomy outside'', everything ``sodden and mud-splattered'' -- makes the setting perform the family's circumstances before a word is spoken, while the women's calm, practised rebuilding tells the audience this catastrophe is routine: Harrison stages normalised crisis, not singular disaster. The Inspection's ``nervous, anxious energy'' as Nan and Gladys straighten everything in sight physicalises the power the welfare state holds over the household -- fear expressed through housework. At the bank, the manager ``pours a cup for himself, but doesn't offer one to GLADYS'': four seconds of stage business that dramatise an entire social hierarchy without one hostile word. At the ball, ``DOLLY is frozen in shame'' -- stillness as violence, the visual opposite of her ``swirling and floating'' entrance moments earlier; the two directions analysed together map her humiliation more precisely than any dialogue. When ``their humpy disappears'' to the sound of bulldozers, Harrison erases the family home as an act of theatre, and the replacement housing rises ``concrete, small, white and featureless'' -- the adjective ``white'' doing quiet, double work. And in Act 2, Errol finds Gladys ``in a similar scene to the first time he saw her, chopping wood'' -- a structural echo that measures how far the relationship has travelled by holding the setting still. Small kindnesses are staged too: Errol ``notices GLADYS is sitting on a kero tin so he does the same'' -- respect performed as posture, the visual antithesis of the bank manager's tea.\\

\section{Quote Banks}

\begin{itemize}

\section{Theme Analysis}

\subsection{Love and Belonging}
Harrison explores love and belonging in rainbows end, with Nan and Gladys creating a warm, loving home for Dolly. When Errol offers Dolly a “better life” in the city with a “real home,” Dolly is taken aback by his naivety to her culture. Even though the humpy had its downside, Dolly acknowledged that it's the fierce love and loyalty that made living there easier. 

Nan. Sometimes, you have to move on. Leave things behind. Even things you love. (P.187) \\
Dolly. No matter how they come into the word, you still love'em the same. (P.186) \\
Errol. What matters is [her] / not [her] address (p. 149). \\
Nan. Oh, for heaven's sake, Dolly marry that boy, before someone else does. (P.198) \\

\subsection{Leadership}
Leadership is explored through Gladys’ speech where she took Papa Dears place at the petition.
Harrison displays not only the leadership in the family dynamic but also the government taking leadership over the indigenous Australians and believing that they know what is best for them and displaying leadership in a very different way. This is displayed by the Inspector as being the character showing the government in ‘Rainbow’s End’.

Inspector: “but someone must take leadership” \\
Gladys: “I will present the petition” \\
Gladys: “She needs to know the world is bigger than just this.” (pg 131) \\
Gladys: “I’m not an interloper- I belong here –this is my land!” \\
Gladys: “We’ve been humble for too long.” \\
Gladys: “I make the decisions regarding Dolores, thank you.” \\
Nan Dear: “Grab the flour, Glad, and the tea.” \\
Gladys: “Oi, Re the so-called ‘housing problem’, it is only a problem because of us Aboriginals-” \\
Nan Dear: “I don’t care what you think! You and your visiting hours. Your rules. No singalongs after dark. Your spying. You, mister, can go to blazes! I’ll give you ‘one’...” \\
Gladys: “We demand to be heard.” \\

\subsection{Race and Racism}
- The love plot in the book is purposefully between Dolly and Errol, Harrsion aims to defy the otherwise segregated nature of the society the play is set in. \\
- Whiteness is a constant theme throughout the play, symbolising the belief that white is somehow ‘cleaner’. \\
- All white characters in the play besides Errol are a source of authority; The councilmen, bank manager and inspector all represent different amenities needed by Aboriginal people that have been controlled by the white population. \\

Nan Dear: “They forced us to leave. Forced us to leave Cummeragunja. Our home” the repetition of “forced” and the use of the words “our home” suggests that Harison is trying to show that the white people think they know better than the indigenous people in the play. p126. \\
Nan Dear: “and hospitals is where they take our baby’s away” \\
Nan Dear: “A girl from The Flats? I don’t even see the town Aboriginals working in stores.” \\
P127 “the hessian that I couldn’t get through and couldn’t even peek over.” \\
P127... “stop the likes of her seeing our humpies.” \\

P152 “I don’t know how you do it...With river water, no less!” \\
P164 “- sorry, report means -” bank manager assumes she doesn’t know. \\
P140 “You people” - Errol – oblivious \\
P163 “What I'm wondering is how she’d fit in” \\
“and hospitals is where they take our babies away...” (Nan pg129) \\
“and your whites are so white!” (Inspector pg152) \\

\subsection{The Intersection of Class and Race}
- The intersection of race and class in Rainbow’s End is exemplified by the dynamic between the white population and the Indigenous population. This relationship is explored in scenarios such as Dolly’s search for job, in which it is seen as unlikely she will be able to receive employment. This creates the inescapable cycle that is forged when race meets class, that to escape a lower class, it’s required to achieve a job that could be considered higher status to society. Race hinders this progress due to the prejudices and barriers held against Indigenous people. \\
- The inspector's view in regard to the flood, as he seems to have no awareness of the effect this will take upon first nations families, particularly the Dear family. \\
- Gladys having to save every penny to afford the encyclopedias just to invest in the cultural capital. \\
- Profit from proximity to whiteness (town Aboriginals) \\
- Government policies \\

Inspector: “property can be replaced” \\
“I'd kind of like to be a nurse”- Dolly, which she believes to be silly. \\
Dolly- “What tree? It would be nice to have a tree and presents.” Pg 169 \\
Inspector “I’m not familiar with the department stores in this area” – pg. 152 (in reference to Daisch’s the town tip” \\
“Your whites are so white! With river water no less!” Pg 152, the inspector. Subtle reference to white is believed better. \\
“When it was our sunroom curtains. But you know i thought we took them to the tip” (in reference to dolly’s ball gown) pg.166 \\
Gladys- “Not on a picker’s wage. Gawd we’d be living on Johnny cakes” pg. 174 (in reference to when Jungi says that property can be replaced.) \\
“The old, ruined lino” pg. 123 The dears have to make dear with what they can get access to unlike the whites who it is privileged. \\
“Love your dress, Dolly. Love the fabric. My mother quite liked it too. When it was our sunroom curtains. But, you know, I thought we took them to the tips.” Pg 166 Nancy embarrasses dolly about her dress when Dolly doesn’t have access to the same privileges that Nancy does. \\
Bank manager: “But everyone needs savings” when Gladys says she’s never been in a bank, the white population has the privilege of using banks while the aborigines make do with tins to store their money. Pg 164 \\
“Rapport with our customers is important –sorry”. Pg. 164 \\
“Isn’t there any way?” Errol, when Gladys says she no longer needs the encyclopedia; The dears unlike whites would have trouble with paying for the encyclopedias and the metre box. Errol doesn’t understand this and there for asks the quote. \\
“I’d think a hospital would be a more suitable place-“when Dolly goes into labour. Unlike white people Aboriginals babies are taken away at the hospitals and the dears are unlikely to be able to afford to go to the hospital. \\
Gladys: -read and write” Errol starts to say that he’s never met anyone who cannot read or write and Gladys cuts him off. Gladys unlike Errol did not have the privilege of being taught yo read and write. Gladys had been sent to work for a family by a terrible mission manager and then had children and then there was the war, so she never had the opportunity Errol did. \\
“Homemade glue” pg. 123 \\
Errol: “What matters is you, not your address...” \\

\subsection{Connection to Land and the Importance of Home}
- When the floods hit Nan, Dolly and Gladys, their house is destroyed and they are forced to move out of their home, the importance of home comes from Gladys when she is determined to “Fix my own house.” Although Gladys does not have the money to fix her home she is sharing the importance of the connection to the land by not leaving and letting the white population win. Dolly also expresses the importance of home when Errol and Dolly argue about leaving the flats to live in the city, Dolly tells Errol that her world is about family rather than leaving them for what Errol calls a real home in the city. \\

“I'm gunna go back and die there”- pg. 125 nan \\
Gladys: “I’m not an interloper – I belong here – this is my land! \\
“a real home is where there are people looking out for each other”- pg. 172 dolly \\
Nan Dear: “Least here we do things our way.” \\
The Rumbalara housing is “white and featureless” and “Anything but loveable.” (Stage directions) \\
Radio: “the most vigorous attempt yet to solve Aboriginal housing” \\
Gladys: ‘[I’m going] to fix my own house.” \\
Errol: “It’s like your family’s from another country or something” \\
Dolly: “Our life isn’t perfect, but like Nan says, it’s ours.” \\
Gladys: “People is what matters.” \\
Gladys: “Mrs Windsor, would you live at Rumbalara? Then why is it good enough for us?” \\

\subsection{Types of Knowledge and Ways of Knowing}
Much of the family’s energy is expended on searching for access to the institutions of the white majority. Gladys never had time for learning, to busy to earn a living to have time to make up for the education that she was denied when she was removed from her home. Gladys has knowledge that far eclipses that of the ‘encyclops boy’. Harrison addresses the sparce of the colonial model of education and the aboriginal knowledge and ways of learning have faced since colonisation. \\



\newpage

\section{Character Analysis}

\subsection{Dolly Banks}
Dolly is the teenage daughter of Gladys and
granddaughter of Nan Dear. Throughout the play, she serves as
a receptacle for her mother’s dreams and her grandmother’s
fears. Gladys fantasies often about what a better life might
look like, but she doesn’t believe that such a life is possible for
her. Instead, she dreams on Dolly’s behalf, and she tries to
encourage Dolly to pursue goals that seem impossible, like
getting a job at a bank that does not want to hire Aboriginal
employees. Nan Dear, meanwhile, views Dolly as vulnerable to
the same discrimination and violence that Nan Dear and
Gladys have experienced. She is especially wary when Dolly
begins seeing Errol, a White boy. Dolly’s relationship with Errol
is complicated by Errol’s unconscious biases, and she
temporarily breaks things off with him when he is unable to put
aside his assumption that White middle-class life is superior to
the more community-based life Dolly wants. Her relationship with Errol also enrages Dolly’s cousin Leon, who sexually
assaults her and leaves her pregnant. Despite the violent origin
of the pregnancy, Dolly loves her baby and names it after Papa
Dear, cementing baby Regina’s status as a beloved member of
the family included in the traditions and legacy it carries.\\

\subsubsection{Analysis}
Dolly Banks is a crucial character in Jane Harrison's play "Rainbow's End," capturing the 
experiences of a young Aboriginal woman during the 1950s, a time rife with changes and challenges 
for her community. The daughter of Gladys Banks and the granddaughter of Nan Dear, Dolly is a 
character who embodies the bridge between the prior generation's traditional views and the new 
generation's aspirations.\\

Throughout the play, Dolly asserts her desires and individuality in a society that would typically 
dismiss them. In one scene, she interacts with Errol, a character symbolising the broader society's 
views, displaying her self-assured yet personable character. When Errol calls her "Miss Banks," she 
promptly corrects him, saying, "Dolly. We’re not too fussed about fancy titles," indicating both 
her lack of pretense. Errol complements her name, but once he learns her full name is Dolores, 
Dolly momentarily moves away, reflecting a subtle consciousness of boundaries and self-respect as 
she navigates her interactions with a non-Aboriginal man.\\

Dolly's aspirations and accomplishments are a point of pride for her mother, Gladys, who notes that 
Dolly is the first in the family to complete her Leaving Certificate, a testament to her 
intelligence and drive. The circumstances in which this achievement is presented, however, bring to 
light the stark realities of racial prejudices they confront. Gladys holds a photo of Dolly, which 
prompts the bank manager to acknowledge Dolly's physical appearance but does not immediately 
understand the reason for Gladys's visit. The scene showcases

Dolly through her mother's eyes—as capable and promising— and reveals the obstacles that she would 
face in achieving her potential in a society that does not readily afford opportunities to 
Aboriginal people.\\

Dolly's character arc within the play reveals the nuanced complexities of identity, as she must 
balance the expectations of her cultural heritage with her own personal ambitions in a society that 
often sees her and her community as lesser. Her actions and decisions throughout the play 
demonstrate the younger generation's determination to both honour their heritage and pursue a 
future they define for themselves.
Through Dolly, Harrison presents themes of cultural identity,
generational change, and the possibilities that lie ahead for
Aboriginal women in a changing world.



Dolly's most decisive act is a refusal. When Errol offers her a life in Melbourne -- a flat, a Kelvinator, the department stores -- he imagines himself as a rescuer, but the offer quietly asks her to give up her family, her community and her Country in exchange for comfort on his terms. Her reply, ``Your world. And you're just assuming that your world is better\ldots This is my place. I'm staying right here with my mum and my nan,'' overturns the assumption at the heart of assimilation: that a white life is self-evidently a better life. In choosing the humpy over the flat, Dolly redefines ``a real home'' as belonging rather than comfort, and asserts a self-determination that the play treats as strength, not sacrifice.\\

Even her scavenging is complicated. At Daish's Paddock tip she adopts a mock-genteel accent -- ``Why yars, I'm after new linoleum'' -- performing the department-store shopper she is denied the chance to be. The joke is self-aware rather than deluded, and the linoleum she carries home genuinely improves the humpy. Harrison lets imagination and dignity work together: the fantasy does not replace real action, it fuels it.\\


\subsection{Gladys Banks}
Gladys is Nan Dear’s middle-aged daughter
and Dolly’s mother. As a child, Gladys was taken away from her
family by the government and put to work as a maid for a White
family. As such, she never learned to read, and the only way she
could make a life for herself was to follow in her mother’s
footsteps as a manual labourer. Despite the hardship she has
faced, Gladys is relentlessly optimistic. She frequently enters
into fantasy sequences where the world is just, her family is
happy, and Dolly has endless possibility before her. Even
outside those fantasy sequences, Gladys believes that Dolly
can live a better life than the generations of women before her.
She is steadfast in this belief, even when Dolly herself doubts it.
Gladys’s optimism proves a vital tool for her eventual activism.
After Dolly accuses Gladys of projecting her own dreams onto
Dolly, Gladys commits herself to working toward making the
world a better place for the sake of herself and her family. She
confronts the city council on the radio about Aboriginal
housing, and she takes over Papa Dear’s speech at a public
presentation of a petition when he fails to arrive on time.
Gladys’s optimism lets her imagine and believe in a better
future, which in turn empowers her to fight for that future.\\

\subsubsection{Analysis}
Gladys Banks represents the aspirations and challenges of an
Aboriginal woman in 1950s Australia. This multi-dimensional
character is caught between her cultural heritage and the
desire for progress and recognition within the broader, often
unfriendly, society.\\

As the daughter of Nan Dear and mother to Dolly, Gladys
mediates between the old ways and new possibilities that her
daughter may have. A scene in the play that captures her
ambitions and protective instincts as a mother is her
confrontation with the bank manager where she presents her
daughter Dolly for a teller's position. Proudly asserting
"She's just
completed her Leaving Certificate—the first in the family—with
real good grades… 'N' top of her class in algebra,"
Gladys is met
not with recognition but indifference, with the bank manager
pouring himself tea but pointedly ignoring her. The scene
captures the societal barriers that Gladys and her daughter
face, as well as Gladys's determination to push against them.\\

Gladys's agency is further highlighted in her firm stance on her
daughter's
autonomy
and social life. Despite Nan Dear's
apprehensions, Gladys declares decisively,
"I’ll make the
decisions regarding Dolores thank you. She’s going to the ball.
And Errol Fisher is walking her home."
This reveals her resolve
and the assertion of her role as a parent, as well as the
modernising viewpoint she holds compared to Nan Dear, who is
more rooted in traditionalist concerns. Moreover, Gladys's
readiness to confront and challenge Nan Dear's objections
demonstrates her strong will as she endeavours to protect her
children's rights to forge their own paths.\\

Gladys's complexity extends into her views on housing and
respect. When discussing the Queen's visit and the humiliation of having their poor living conditions concealed behind hessian curtains, Gladys expresses upset not just over the cosmetic cover-ups but the deeper issue of decent housing and respect for her community. This highlights her awareness of the broader socio-political context and her aspiration for improved conditions for her people. Yet, she also values the autonomy they possess in their current living situation, preferring the freedom they enjoy at the shanty on the river to the restrictions experienced in more controlled environments like Cummeragunja, an Aboriginal settlement she and Nan Dear remember with claustrophobia.\\

Gladys Banks embodies the transition between tradition and progress, acting as both a nurturer and a pioneer. Her interactions with others showcase her resolve to advance her family's status despite the systemic challenges imposed by aw orld that often disregards their aspirations and dignity.



Gladys's quiet arc from shame to voice binds the whole play together. Her illiteracy -- a legacy of the mission schooling she was denied -- is first a secret managed with jam tins and pride, then something she confronts directly, learning to read so that no institution can again decide things about her family over her head. The arc completes at the Melbourne meeting: when Papa Dear fails to arrive, Gladys steps up unplanned, begins haltingly with the petition's borrowed words, then abandons them for her own -- ``Why humbly? We've been humble too long\ldots we demand to be heard.'' The movement from reading someone else's sentence to speaking her own is Harrison's clearest image of empowerment through voice.\\

Tellingly, the crowd's tribute -- that she is ``Papa Dear's daughter all right'' -- hands her courage back to a patriarch, showing how even a triumphant woman is read through the men around her. And when her earlier interjection at the council is carried into the humpy by radio, Harrison literalises the point: a woman may be shut out of the room, but her voice can travel beyond it.\\

\subsection{Nan Dear}
Nan Dear is the matriarch of the family at the
centre of the play. She is Gladys’s mother and Dolly’s
grandmother, and she takes her duty of care to them seriously.
Nan Dear is a bastion of tradition, teaching Dolly about her
family history and aspects of their culture that she doesn’t
learn at school. She has suffered under institutions of racism
and ideologies of White supremacy, especially at the Aboriginal
reserve in Cummeragunja, where Nan Dear was abused and
her daughter Gladys was taken away from her. As such, Nan
Dear has little faith that the world can improve, and she doesn’t
understand Gladys and Dolly’s attempts to improve it. She
especially fears for Dolly when Dolly begins a romantic
relationship with Errol, a White boy with the same last name as
a White man who raped Nan Dear years ago. Nan Dear works
to separate Dolly and Errol, but at the end of the play she
realises that Errol is a considerate and respectful young man.
When she finds out for certain that Errol is not related to her
attacker, she gives him her blessing and encourages Dolly to
marry him, demonstrating that even someone as traditional as
Nan Dear can grow and change.

\subsubsection{Analysis}
Born by the Murray River as her birth certificate states, Nan Dear
is steeped in her Aboriginal heritage and connections to her
traditional lands. Her identity is closely tied to the Murray River
and the land from which she was
displaced
, specifically from
Cummeragunja, marking a bitter and forcible removal from her
home. This
dislocation
is a source of deep-seated resentment
and shapes her worldview. Despite the trials she has faced, Nan
Dear is characterised by a deep sense of duty and
pragmatism
.
She is concerned with everyday survival and the practical needs
of her family—working to put food on the table and keeping her
family safe.\\

As the matriarch, Nan Dear's relationships with her daughter,Gladys, and granddaughter, Dolly, are a mix of affection and stern guidance. She is quick to offer wisdom to Dolly, such as reminding her of the importance of knowing family connections to avoid unwitting relationships with close relatives. Nan Dear's knowledge of family history is implied to be extensive, though she may feign ignorance when it suits her, suggesting a complicated relationship with the past.\\

Her cultural beliefs surface subtly through her reluctance to embrace the visiting monarchy, mocking the idea of needing white gloves for a royal visit and declaring, "She's not my queen". This resistance to the monarch's visit and the detachment she expresses towards Gladys' interest in royalty reveals an underlying rejection of colonial authority and ap reference for the Aboriginal ways of life.\\

Nan Dear's resilience is not just a personal trait but serves as symbolic of her community's endurance. Statements like "Born[by the Murray River] and by crikey I'm gunna go back and die there" demonstrate her strong will to return to her roots despite the adversity faced. This return, for her, is associated with embracing the simplicity and beauty of her origins, like having a feed of swan eggs before she passes, signifying a connection to her birthplace. Her experience of displacement is steeped in historical injustices, as evidenced by her bitter recollection of being forced to leave Cummeragunja. This comment on forced relocation exposes the reality of Aboriginal displacement and the loss of home and culture.\\

These observations offer a critical perspective on the consequences of colonial policies for Aboriginal people.Nan Dear is a fiercely independent individual whose character lends gravity and authenticity to the narrative of "Rainbow's End." Her dialogue is delivered with an undercurrent of knowing defiance, revealing a woman shaped by adversity yet undiminished in spirit. She represents a generation of Aborigina lpeople who, despite systematic disenfranchisement,maintained their dignity and cultural identity. Through her words and actions, Nan Dear confronts the audience with the realities of her community's history, while also displaying an indomitable resolve to withstand and challenge these realities.\\


Two late revelations deepen Nan beyond the stern matriarch. The first is comic in form and serious in meaning: her faked coughing fit to keep Dolly from the dance, exposed by the stage direction that she ``remembers to cough again''. The manipulation is real, but so is the love underneath it -- her obstruction of Errol is later revealed to be grounded not in simple prejudice but in her own experience of violence at the hands of a white man, which recasts her control as embodied fear. Harrison compounds the irony cruelly: the assailant's surname was Fisher, yet Errol's family, originally ``Vischer'', is no relation. The secret that might have spared Dolly was kept in shame across a generation, and the play's judgement falls less on Nan than on the silence itself -- a silence colonial violence taught families to keep.\\

The second revelation is wordless. Publicly, Nan scorns the encyclopedias -- ``Ask him what it says about the Aborigines, eh?'' -- but once Gladys is out of the room, the stage directions show her taking down the latest volume to read alone. The gesture complicates any neat opposition between Western and Indigenous knowledge: curiosity is universal; what colonialism rationed was access, not desire.\\


\subsection{Errol Fisher}
Errol Fisher is a young White encyclopedia
salesman. He encounters Nan Dear, Gladys, and Dolly by
accident, after taking a wrong turn on his route selling
encyclopedias. Errol falls in love with Dolly and comes to
admire her family. Gladys demonstrates a strength that Errol
has never seen in his own mother, who is subservient to Errol’s father. The affection that Nan Dear and Gladys show Dolly is
also alien to Errol, who has grown up with a strict and unfeeling
father. Though he earnestly loves Dolly, he offends her with his
ignorance of her culture and his assumption that his way of life
must be superior to hers. He assumes she would be eager to
leave her family behind to be his wife in the city, and he
struggles to understand her insistence that she must remain
with her loved ones. Ultimately, though, Errol comes to respect
what Dolly wants for her future, and he promises to live any
kind of life she wants as long as they can do so together.\\

\subsubsection{Analysis}
Fisher
Errol Fisher emerges in Jane Harrison's "Rainbow's End" as a significant, though less central, 
character, who embodies the intersection of the Aboriginal community's life and the broader 
Australian society during the 1950s. Portrayed initially as somewhat awkward and uncertain, he is 
introduced to the audience as he nearly falls off his bike upon encountering Dolly, signaling an 
immediate spark of attraction that complicates his interactions with the family.\\

This initial interaction with Dolly sets the stage for Errol's character development. The exchange 
with Gladys further illustrates his social position and perspective. When he tentatively approaches 
Gladys as she is chopping wood, mistaking her for a man, he immediately demonstrates cultural 
ignorance and nervousness. Apologising and stating his name, "Sorry er ma’am. My name is Errol 
Fisher," he provides a contrast to the women's more assertive presence. This contrast is emblematic 
of the interplay between white Australians and Aboriginal people at the time—a dynamic of presumed 
authority and misunderstanding from the former, contrasted with an under-recognised competence and 
resilience from the latter.\\

Errol's intentions are primarily associated with business—selling encyclopedias as revealed in his 
conversation with Gladys—yet this transactional engagement serves as a backdrop to more complex 
intercultural exchanges. Gladys and Errol mention the encyclopedias simultaneously, indicating 
their previous interactions and the economic realities pressing on Gladys, who states, "We… I… 
won’t be needing them any more. All the shillings go into the meter box now," highlighting not only 
financial struggles but also the prioritisation of practical
necessities over educational luxuries.\\

His interactions with Dolly in the play are coloured with romantic interest, albeit fraught with 
the implications of such a relationship in a racially divided society. He compliments her name and 
attempts to bridge their cultural gap, however awkwardly. Since Errol also represents other 
characters in the play, his role is fundamental in bringing out the perspectives and reactions of 
Nan Dear, Gladys, and Dolly and propelling their narratives forward. Errol's presence and character 
serve as a mirror that reflects and refracts the complexities of identity and interpersonal 
relationships within the stratified, mid-20th
century Australia Australian society.



\subsection{Leon (Cousin)}
Leon, often referred to as simply “Cousin,” is
Dolly’s cousin. He is aggressive and often drunk, and he
disapproves of Dolly’s relationship with Errol. As a twisted
form of punishment for that relationship, Leon rapes Dolly,
resulting in her pregnancy. Leon’s assault of Dolly speaks to the
reality of gendered violence within marginalised communities.\\

\subsection{Papa Dear}
Papa Dear is Nan Dear’s husband, Gladys’s father,
and Dolly’s grandfather. Though he is not Gladys’s biological
father, since Gladys was conceived from Nan Dear’s rape by a
White man, Nan Dear considers him Gladys’s father. Papa Dear
is an activist for Aboriginal rights, and his work takes him all
over the country, which means he is rarely there for his family.
He never appears in person in the play; he is only mentioned in
dialogue and imagined in fantasy sequences. His absence
leaves Nan Dear, Gladys, and Dolly alone to create an
exclusively feminine space in their home.\\

\subsubsection{Analysis}
Dear
Papa Dear, as depicted in Jane Harrison's "Rainbow's End," is a paternal figure whose presence is 
felt throughout the narrative despite being physically absent for much of the play. His character 
is crafted through the recollections and expectations of the other characters, particularly Nan 
Dear and Gladys. Papa Dear's role is largely communicated through anecdotes and his influence on 
the family's structure and values.\\

Mentioned by Nan Dear as "busy doing good work. God’s work and hard work," Papa Dear is portrayed 
as a figure devoted to the betterment of the Aboriginal community. His dedication to activism and 
community work is further highlighted when Gladys recalls that he is "in Western Australia. Touring 
the communities there," and has been featured "in the newspaper and all," suggesting his actions 
and efforts are recognised and appreciated beyond their immediate surroundings.\\


Papa Dear's dream visitations reinforce his endearing place within the family, despite his absence. 
In an imagined scene, he is seen wearing "an old-fashioned hat and coat," hinting at a sense of 
nostalgia and timelessness associated with him. His brief entrance to kiss Gladys on the top of the 
head showcases a symbolic and loving connection with his family, despite his lack of direct 
intervention in their current struggles.\\

The character of Papa Dear is particularly important to Dolly during a vulnerable moment when she 
reflects on her family tree, considering naming her unborn child Reg or Regina, "after Papa Dear." 
This indicates an enduring legacy and respect for him within the family, especially for his 
commitment to their people.\\

While Papa Dear does not have a speaking role or much direct action, his presence resonates with 
the family and the audience, capturing the spirit of a generation of Aboriginal individuals who 
strived to improve conditions for their communities. Through the memories shared and the respect 
expressed by the characters, Papa Dear stands as a symbol of resilience, hope, and the ongoing 
struggle for activism and recognition within the
Aboriginal community of the time.



\subsection{The Inspector}
The Inspector is a White man employed by the
Australian government to inspect the living conditions of
Aboriginal Australians, which includes the family’s humpy. As a
representative of the Aboriginal Protection Board, the
Inspector regulates and controls the lives of Aboriginal
Australians, and that power extends to taking Aboriginal
children away from their family. The Board took Gladys away
from her family when she was a child, and she and Nan Dear
are terrified that the Inspector will take Dolly away from them.\\

\subsubsection{Analysis}
In the play "Rainbow's End," the inspector is a minor but telling character that presents a 
snapshot of the authority and surveillance that Aboriginal people endured in 1950s Australia. He 
appears in a scene where Nan and Gladys are inside the humpy, anxiously straightening everything, 
which sets a tone of tension and unease. The inspector serves as a symbol of the government 
oversight and control that loomed over the lives of the characters.\\

Paying a visit to evaluate their living conditions, the inspector enters with a superficially 
pleasant demeanor that thinly veils the oppressive power dynamics at play. He greets the efforts of 
Nan and Gladys with an offhand compliment, "I say crocheted pillow shams. Such beautiful work!" 
which Gladys attributes to her mother's handiwork. His interaction is polite yet distant, making 
notes while seemingly ignoring the weight of his presence and authority in the humpy.\\

As the scene unfolds, it becomes clear that his visit is not a casual or friendly one, but an 
inspection that could have severe implications for the family. The anxiety Nan and Gladys display 
is indicative of their vulnerability to the whims of the inspector and, by extension, the colonial 
power structure. When the inspector inquires after a Mr. Banks, his question underscores the 
traditional views of family structure and the expectation of a male head of household, hinting at 
patriarchal values as well.\\

The inspector's questions and his mode of taking notes encapsulate the intrusion of government into 
personal spaces and lives. This scrutiny implies potential consequences if living conditions or 
behaviors do not meet certain standards, though these criteria are not made explicit. This scene 
heightens the awareness of the audience to the systematic and normalised regulations that govern 
the lives of Aboriginal characters in the play, bringing the broader context of racial disparity 
and control into focus through the actions and attitudes of the inspector.



The Inspector's language deserves close reading, because Harrison gives him the play's most chilling sentence and delivers it in the mildest of tones: ``The Aborigine needs to be absorbed into the community. But how can he be absorbed until he learns to live like us?'' The verb ``absorbed'' does the ideological work -- assimilation imagined as dissolution, help defined as the disappearance of a people. His compliment ``Your whites are so white!'' measures human worth in laundry, and his marvelling ``I don't know how you do it'' converts the women's obvious skill into a miracle, which is its own condescension. He is no snarling villain; he is a courteous functionary, and the courtesy is the point -- Harrison locates racism not in individual malice but in the machinery a polite man can operate with a clear conscience.\\

The visit also fractures the family in front of him: asked about Dolly's future, Nan, Gladys and Dolly give three different answers -- the laundry, a bookkeeper, the hospital -- a small dramatic irony that maps three different accommodations with a world that limits them.\\

\subsection{Mr. Coody}
Mr. Coody is the rent collector at Rumbalara. He
imposes strict and invasive rules on his tenants, which includes
threatening to evict Dolly and her family due to Dolly’s
pregnancy. His intrusion into the women’s lives reflects that
although Rumbalara is an Aboriginal housing district, it is not a
safe haven or a stable place of community because it is run by
White men.\\

\subsection{Bank Manager}
The Bank Manager works at the bank where
Gladys wants Dolly to find a job. He resists the idea of hiring an
Aboriginal worker, making excuses as to why Dolly would not
be qualified for the job, but Gladys finally stands up to him and
he offers Dolly an opportunity to apply.\\

\subsubsection{Analysis}
Harrison uses the The Bank Manager to showcase the systemic barriers faced by the Aboriginal 
community in terms of employment and societal inclusion. His interaction with Gladys Banks in her 
attempt to secure a job for her daughter, Dolly, is telling of the racially prejudiced attitudes of 
the time. Gladys, with determination, engages the Bank Manager and puts forward Dolly's 
qualifications for a teller's position: "But she’s just completed her Leaving Certificate—the first 
in the family." Despite the qualifications presented, the Bank Manager dismisses the notion, 
indicating a lack of openness to hiring an Aboriginal person.\\

The Bank Manager focuses on Dolly's appearance rather than her capabilities when he says, "Yes. 
Very pretty face," after examining a photograph of her. This superficial assessment underscores the 
racial objectification and segregation of Aboriginal people. His concern regarding how Dolly would 
"fit in" at the bank delineates the invisible boundaries placed by a society reluctant to accept 
Aboriginal individuals into professional roles. "In a job such as this reliability is important," 
he states, questioning how Dolly would commute to work, despite evidence of her punctuality and 
capability presented by Gladys.\\

The role of the Bank Manager, while not overly complex, provides a window into both the 
institutionalised racism of the period and the socioeconomic status of Aboriginal families. His 
patronising attitude when offering a tin money bank to Gladys, likening it to "jam tins," reflects 
a condescension towards her and her family's means of saving money, which is deeply ingrained in 
the Bank Manager’s and wider society’s view of Aboriginal people.\\

The character subtly represents the gatekeeping that denies Aboriginal people equal opportunities, 
serving as a juxtaposition to Gladys’s and Dolly's aspirations for advancement. Through  this 
interaction, Harrison critiques the wider Australian society's views on race and the illusory concept of a fair, meritocratic
system, thereby reinforcing the themes of racism and
discrimination that are central to the play's message.\\



The bank scene achieves its discrimination almost entirely through manners. The stage business does the work: the manager pours tea for himself and offers Gladys none -- in an era when sharing tea ritualised social equality, the omission quietly declares that no equality is on offer. His words stay cordial while his meaning does not: Dolly's excellent results are praised, then dissolved into doubts about how she would ``fit in''. And when Gladys cannot write, his smoothing courtesy -- ``Fountain pens are a little tricky, aren't they?'' -- performs kindness while reclassifying the results of educational exclusion as a small mechanical mishap. Harrison's point is exact: this is discrimination without a single hostile word, prejudice wearing the costume of politeness, which is precisely what makes it so difficult to fight.\\

\subsection{Ester}
Ester is a neighbour of the family who represents the
most vulnerable members of the Aboriginal community. She is regularly abused by her White husband, and her sons are taken
away by the Inspector.\\

\subsection{Regina}
Regina is Dolly’s infant daughter. Her birth marks the
beginning of the fourth generation of women in the family.\\



\newpage

\newpage

\section{Character and Relationships}

\subsection{Dolly Banks}
\textbf{Key Quote:} ``And as far as what you’re offering … no thank you. This is my place. I’m staying right here with my mum and my nan.'' (to Errol, p.172)\\

Dolly’s non-reaction to her ruined magazines indicates a practised stoicism in the face of misfortune. She is studious, completing her Leaving Certificate with all ‘As and Bs’ and coming ‘top of her class in algebra’ (p.163). Remarkably ‘pretty’, she is also ‘clever’, ‘kind’, ‘modest’, and ‘straightforward’ (p.190). Dolly sometimes allows herself to dream, like Gladys winning \textit{Pick-A-Box} (p.130) or winning the Miss Mooroopna-Shepparton title at the ball (p.166). She dreams fancifully of becoming an actress or model, yet practically of a career in nursing (p.142).\\

She is unhappily aware that she might become a fruit picker like her mother and grandmother (p.141). Dolly’s exposure to racial discrimination — with ‘stones’ thrown at her and ‘snide remarks’ (p.162) overheard in the street — makes her pessimistic about her future. She has a close and loving relationship with Nan, whose overprotectiveness can sometimes be tedious. Yet their closeness and trust alleviate Dolly’s distress after she becomes pregnant, when Nan confides that Gladys was conceived in similarly traumatic circumstances. The sharing of this dark ‘secret’ brings them both comfort.\\

\subsubsection{Analysis}
Dolly Banks embodies the tensions between youthful hope and social limitation. Harrison portrays her as the bridge between aspiration and resignation — a young woman educated enough to imagine a different future but aware of the barriers imposed by race and class. Her determination to remain with her family, rather than abandon them for the city life Errol proposes, symbolises her rejection of assimilation and her embrace of identity. Dolly’s relationship with Nan and Gladys represents the generational continuum of Aboriginal womanhood: endurance, wisdom, and resistance to erasure.\\

\subsection{Nan Dear}
\textbf{Key Quote:} ``Ask him what it says in that there encyclops about the Aboriginies, eh?'' (to Gladys, p.135)\\

Nan Dear is often the cynical voice of experience, reminding Gladys that she is a lowly bean picker unworthy of the Queen’s notice (p.124). Unafraid to speak out, she angrily confronts the Rent Collector for criticising their supposed ‘flouting’ of rules, throwing eggs at him (pp.185–6). Capable and energetic, she works as a fruit picker for the canning factory, tackles physical labour, and can make a stew out of nettles when money is short.\\

She is a loving and protective grandmother who knows the danger of being ‘[taken] advantage of’ by ‘a whitefella’ (p.187). Set in her ways, she mistrusts outsiders — seeing Bob Dyer as ‘one of them flash Yanks’ (p.131) and regarding Errol as a ‘snake-oil salesman’ (p.136). Initially doubtful, she later recognises Errol’s goodness and advises Dolly to marry him ‘before someone else does’ (p.198). Nan also clashes with Gladys over Dolly’s upbringing, criticising her monarchist sympathies and sceptical of education’s value. Yet when she hears Gladys speak up on the radio, she dances with pride around the room.\\

\subsubsection{Analysis}
Nan Dear represents endurance, tradition, and the persistence of Aboriginal matriarchal strength. Her scepticism toward authority and colonial structures masks deep compassion and wisdom. Through her character, Harrison celebrates the resilience of older generations who, despite trauma and displacement, preserve dignity and family unity. Nan’s humour, practicality, and protectiveness make her both a guardian of the past and an emblem of cultural survival.\\

\subsection{Gladys Banks}
\textbf{Key Quote:} ``We demand proper schooling. And not just for us. The white people too – they need to be educated about us, and our ways.'' (p.197)\\

Initially a fervent royalist, Gladys goes to extraordinary lengths to glimpse the Queen, only to be blocked by hessian screens hiding Indigenous homes from sight (p.127).
