\section{Sample Creating Texts Piece (19/20)}


ZZEXP Creating Texts: 'The Path Less Taken' (to reflect / express) ZZEXPEND


A high-scoring (19/20) Section B piece written to reflect and express, responding to the title below.
\begin{quote}
\textit{Title: 'The Path Less Taken'}
\end{quote}

I was at a family dinner over Easter earlier in the year, and one of my aunts asked me a seemingly-innocuous question that made me quite uncomfortable. Usually, I am an upbeat, bubbly person, and most would tell you that I have high confidence. Unfortunately, the opposite is true: every day I question the choices I have made and the impact they have had on my relationships.

When I entered high school, I was faced with a rather uncomfortable choice: I would have to pick a school sport to play during winter, or join a band. The extracurricular lady peered at me over her bifocals, frowning, eyebrows drawn together and raised like little anthills. The only band that had any space was the orchestra, and they needed a piccolo player.

Now, anyone who is acquainted with woodwind instruments would know that the piccolo is a tiny, shrieking version of the flute, and that it should be played only by an experienced flautist with excellent tone. But for a reason unknown both to my parents and myself, I, standing in my oversized blazer with my dress almost at my ankles, boldly offered my services to the school band. The lady in charge of extracurriculars frowned a little harder, and I could almost imagine the tiny ants running in and out of those hills. Then she sat back, sighed, and put my name down, one eyebrow still raised.

Obviously, I was terrible. However, I started practising more and more. I took a certain tormented delight in knowing that while other students were tucking into dinner, or doing a little homework, I was running up and down the same five notes with deliberate focus. As painful as my practice was for all within earshot, the improvement it allowed me in the orchestra gave me surging motivation.

Sometimes in life, you are captured by an idea which is inexplicable to those who haven't felt it. The sense of mastery I felt playing the piccolo made all other subjects at school pale in comparison. What was maths, with all its useless formulae repeated over and over, when music was a space with infinite room for improvement? Where every day's practice contributed to notable growth, however small? Music was not just a hobby for me, but a pursuit which gave me the deepest sense of meaning. Over time, I came to love the piccolo more than anything else.

Unfortunately, as the years went by, my parents, who had initially been in favour of my playing an instrument, slowly started to prioritise other things. I should have been studying more maths, more science, writing more essays. I knew that they expected me to study like my friends, and to aim for medicine so I could become a doctor. As much as I tried, my enthusiasm for those subjects never matched what I felt for music. I practised more, and my grades fell further. I tried to hide reports from my parents (with little success). Finally, when I entered year 12, I committed a cardinal sin: I listed a music degree as my first university preference.

The decision had a negative impact on my parents. Our relationship turned to more fighting, and then stony, awkward dinners. Parents often have high expectations for their children. Life is short. You only buy books for a new highschool year six times with your child, and they only get the ages of 18-22 to do undergrad with people their own age. For parents, not only does time with children fly by, but in many cases the having of children cut their own professional lives short. I remember my mother constantly reminding me that perhaps she would have been so much further ahead in her workplace if not for the family, as much as she loved us. I think for her in particular, the lives of my siblings and myself were vicarious extensions of her own, through which she could live out different "maybe" paths.

You can understand, then, why I would have been so perturbed by my aunt's question: "And what will you be doing at the end of the year?"

For my part, I could never see why my parents were so upset. My three older siblings all left secondary education and diligently commenced medical school. I, on the other hand, could only see one thing: I had one life to live, and I'd rather do anything than spend it dissecting bodies and examining skin conditions. Once you realise that life is short, you come to terms with the fact that you may as well spend it doing things you enjoy. How sad it would be to die doing something you hated out of a feeling of social obligation.

And yet, this is what so many of us do. Many of us end up trapped in degrees, then internships, then grad roles, then middle-management jobs chosen for us by our high-schooler selves, with extremely little experience to guide the decision. Most people enter this life because it is the socially acceptable thing to do, and also because it is extremely difficult to find direction. Artists, inventors, and scientists have to bet against conventional wisdom to pursue their innate dreams. However, conventional wisdom is usually right (which is why we call it wisdom), and so if you don't have a good reason to ignore it, you probably shouldn't. Perhaps, given how much I know my parents care about me, I do understand their worry after all.

So when my aunt asked me this question, the mood in the room dropped somewhat. I thought carefully about my response. Should I lie, tell her that I'll be doing medicine too? Should I say I was prevented by my poor grades? It was almost true. But then I felt the weight of the truth settle upon me, and it was heavy and liberating all at once. I was different. Who cared? This was my life. "So, then? Will you be following your good older siblings?"

"You know, aunty," I twirled some spaghetti around my fork. "I've decided to walk the path less taken."

\newpage

\section{Sample Creating Texts Piece: 'Finding My Way' (10/10)}


ZZEXP Creating Texts: 'Finding My Way' (full marks) ZZEXPEND


A full-mark Personal Journeys piece responding to the title 'Finding My Way'.
\begin{quote}
\textit{Stimuli used: a pair of feet on asphalt with painted arrows pointing in many directions; 'The caterpillar transforms without thought. Its journey to a beautiful butterfly, inevitable. Why must my own metamorphosis be shaped by so many choices?'}
\end{quote}

I remember the day that I got off the plane so vividly. I remember the sun that day, a cruel god beating down upon me from its court of cloudless blue. It scorched my skin and left no prisoners. It was nothing like the benevolent sun over my homeland of Calcutta, where the gentle rays of morning would shine over the backs of a hundred thousand people, wrapped up in their idiosyncratic lifestyles since twilight.

But there was no one here. There was just me, my father, and the red earth that stretched out before me in every direction, desolate and uninviting. My father, a self-titled opportunist, yearning to abandon the stagnancy that is the defining characteristic of the Indian middle class, saw Laxmi, the Hindu goddess of prosperity, hidden in every crack of the drought-stricken land.

I always thought my father was a little bit of an oddball. His brain was rattled around by the many, many wars it had been exposed to. In the same spirit, his nervous yet paradoxically poised energy set our house alive. It would wake up with the soft, repetitive chants of his Sanskrit prayers in the morning and fall asleep to his many lectures about the ideal governmental system come nightfall. It smelled like incense, asafoetida, and cumin, instead of the burnt smoke of the great Australian outback outside our porch door. It was the only piece of home in a strange new country. I cherished it with all my heart.

He was quick to find his way. In three years' time, he was listening to The Beatles whilst fortifying himself with double helpings of Milo and Bengali egg fry, perusing an assortment of Hindi newspapers he got internationally delivered to us. The blokes at his sheep shearing job liked to alternate between calling him African and Indigenous Australian, slurs for both inclusive. He paid no heed, jovially punctuating his latest tales of workplace discrimination with, "Their stones and sticks can only rattle my bones! In Calcutta, we used gulel when we really wanted to hurt someone." I didn't have the heart to quip about his idiomatic misquoting. His spirit was indomitable. Nothing could faze him.

I, on the other hand, could not extricate myself from the diminishing gazes of my peers. Every remark on the failure of my foreign tongue to mold itself to the valleys and troughs of a new language, every question underpinned by perfunctory distaste, every snigger swirled in my head each night, consuming the liminal space between waking and sleeping. The movies I watched, the songs I liked, the food I ate, all underwent a strict metamorphosis like the caterpillar confined within the fecund dark of a chrysalis. Slowly, one choice at a time, aligning more and more with the Australian lifestyle. Slowly, one choice at a time, departing from my roots.

That was, until the diagnosis. "Hepatitis B," the doctor called it, the only bloke in town willing to look at a 'coloured' patient. "From infected needles, or maybe passed down in the womb from his mother." My father, try as he might, could not pronounce the name of the illness eating his liver alive. He jokingly called it a gift from our motherland. Over the next few months, I watched him disintegrate into a shell of himself. He could barely open his eyes most days, and when he could, he would talk. He would rasp his smooth tenor into a small tape recorder into the early hours of the morning, flitting from English to Hindi to Bengali as the disease continued its unending assault into his being.

When they complained to me, he laid his papery, wizened hand, which once, in a bygone era, would emanate vitality so potent it was palpable in his air, upon my shoulder and looked at me in the eye with a desperate so intense I felt it in my very soul. "You must find your way, beta. You must tell my story before my time is up."

When he passed, I lost the only person rooting for me, both on this side of the Indian Ocean and the one where I came from. I was lost without him. I felt stagnant, stuck. Our house suddenly felt as desolate as the golden land on my very first day. Over time, I have found my way. I honour his memory by telling his extraordinary story to anyone that will listen. I continue his tradition by hanging his portrait above my mantel and pestering my eldest daughter to hang a garland of fresh marigolds underneath it every morning, just as my father did with his father's portrait, and how his father would have done with his father before him.

Because of him, I have spent a lifetime working as a social worker, aiding those fragmented because of migration, granting me the insurmountable pleasure of listening to a thousand stories. Many of them are like my father and I's, each marked with their own anecdotes and idiosyncrasies. People, struggling, trying, grappling with making sense of a new world. I see the glint of my father's smile in the face of a stranger coming to Australia to make a better life for themselves and their families. I hear his voice in their adversities. It feels like home.

\subsubsection{What made this a 10/10}
Every scene threads the stimuli through fresh sensory imagery, recurring motifs, and a quiet, honest voice. That layering is what lifts a Section B piece from competent to top band.

\newpage

\section{Sample Creating Texts Piece: 'This is Me' (10/10)}


ZZEXP Creating Texts: 'This is Me' (full marks) ZZEXPEND


A full-mark Personal Journeys piece responding to the title 'This is Me'.
\begin{quote}
\textit{Stimuli used: 'Life isn't about finding yourself. Life is about creating yourself.'; 'My story is ready. There's enough to share. As I have made plenty. Come, sit at my table. Listen and learn. And you will see me.'}
\end{quote}

"Good evening customers. Your next service to depart from Platform 2 will be the four-fifty-six Flinders Street running express from Newport to Footscray, Footscray to North Melbourne, and then stopping all stations to Flinders Street. Always remember to touch on your Myki at the start of your journey." The train announcer's voice is a worn chime, filling the stark station like a frayed tapestry in an abandoned household. The station smells like it always does, redolent with the acrid tang of piss, sweat, and smoke. There is something self-righteous about the smell of a train station; none are exempt from its slightly carcinogenic, slightly metallic embrace.

A low mechanical groan swells beneath his feet as the train approaches, its headlights cutting through the dimness of the already-darkening winter sky with a tired reluctance. The doors slide open with a hydraulic sigh. He steps inside. The 4:56 pm train from Newport Station on Wednesday, the 20th of August, the 2025th year of the Common Era is almost deserted.

He looks down at his hands, admiring the soft webbing connecting the valleys between his fingers. His calluses are peeling, revealing the angry red of new skin underneath. It distantly reminds him of the animal documentaries he used to watch as a kid. He recalls his grandfather teaching him about the rattlesnakes as they appeared on their family's old LCD; how their cyclical shedding of skin added a new segment to their rattles. Each interlocked and keratinised bit of memory ultimately conjoined to create their characteristic rattles, allowing them to alert nearby friends or warn foe when shook. He feels a bit like that rattlesnake now, shedding another layer of him, who he was (is?), as his sole ward against something bigger than him.

He straightens, instinctively brushing his hands against the worn fabric of his jeans, as though he can rid himself of that thin, flaking skin, of the evidence that he is still stuck in the process of becoming something else.

The carriage is quieter than he expects. Subdued, like a room where the conversation has only just ended and the last spoken consonant lingers. A man in a hi-vis vest dozes with his head tilted back, mouth slightly ajar and a tenuous string of drool tracing its way down the contours of his jaw. Two teenagers sit across from one another, sharing a set of earbuds, their heads bowed together in conspiratorial symmetry. The girl in green does not look up. He takes a seat by the window.

As the train lurches forward, the station recedes into abstraction. The steel beams dissolve into streaks of grey, then into nothing at all. He watches his reflection instead. It hovers faintly against the glass, superimposed upon the passing city: a face that feels both intimately his and faintly unfamiliar. The train gathers speed.

But at the city, the carriage doors part and the world rushes in. The platform is dense with motion. He takes note of the sudden layering of language, the multiplicity of accents folding over another like tenuous silk organza of his mother's sarees. Someone coughs sharply; someone else argues into a phone; a group of schoolboys with reckless affection. The air smells of coffee, of baked bread, and of the faint tang of iron from the rails.

He lingers. He feels it again, that distance. Not exclusion, really, but a kind of peripheral existence. As though he is still watching the city through the glass when in reality, he's out in the cold, the gale lashing unforgivingly like a whip against his face. He looks down at his hands, at the peeling skin, scrutinising the rawness beneath. Shedding, again. Becoming, again.

He steps off the train and follows the crowd instinctively, letting the current of its movement take him toward Queen Victoria Market. It is not the quiet barter of daytime commerce that he expected. Tonight, the market radiates luminous against the winter chill, illuminated by strings of fairy lights and the glow of fire pits. The scents are the first to hit him: the rich spices from the steaming laksa, sweet smoke from grilled takoyaki, and the inviting swirl of mulled wine and spiced Milo in ceramic mugs held tight between gloved hands.

Luxuriating in the sensory overload, he moves deeper into the market. He spots Hungarian chimney cakes next to Malaysian satay, Latin American empanadas beside Turkish gozleme; the diversity of flavour becoming a rare map of the world in itself, condensed into the congested borders of one crowded laneway. At a food stall selling steaming xiaolongbao, an older man smiles at him and nods, the gesture small but genuine. The delicate crows feet of his smile remind him of his grandfather, the passionate twinkle in his eye as he taught the young boy before him, so many moons ago.

Suddenly trapped in the fossilised resin of memory, he remembers his grandfather's parting words to him, "Life isn't just about finding yourself, beta. You must remember to listen to the stories around you." He nods back to the older man, a little bit awkwardly, but finally laden with the understanding that had eluded him prior.

He realises, watching the ebb and flow of the throng, locals and newcomers, laughter and music, old and young, all engaged in the shared act of eating, moving, and breathing as one under the winter light, that belonging is not a singular destination. It is a mosaic of interactions, layered and overlapping, like the rattles on the snake's tail. Distinct segments forming something whole, something enduring.

He leaves the market with nothing in his hands, but a warmth settled somewhere in his throat. A loosening, however slight, of the tight, invisible vice of unfamiliarity.

The next evening, he boards the 4:56 again. The carriage is fuller now, or perhaps he is simply noticing more. A conversation drifts across the aisle, punctuated by laughter. As the train comes to a grinding halt, someone shifts to make space without being asked. He catches his reflection in the window. It is still there, sullen and superimposed against the speeding city. However, the edges have softened. The distance, though not demolished, has narrowed into something traversable. This is me, he thinks.

"Now arriving at Flinders Street Station..." The train announcer's voice is a worn chime and the station smells like it always does, like piss and sweat and smoke, but this time, he does not flinch. He listens instead, as the city moves with him, no longer a current he moves against, but something he is, slowly, learning how to inhabit.

\subsubsection{What made this a 10/10}
Every scene threads the stimuli through fresh sensory imagery, recurring motifs, and a quiet, honest voice. That layering is what lifts a Section B piece from competent to top band.

\newpage

\part{Analysing Argument}

\clearpage
\thispagestyle{empty}
\hfill
\clearpage

\section{Essay Structure}

\subsection{Introduction}

\subsubsection{What you are trying to achieve}
The purpose of an introduction is to 'introduce' both the irrefutable facts about the text you will be analysing, and your interpretation of the text. The best way to do this is to connect the text's plain details with your early analysis of what the contention, tone and intended audience.

\subsubsection{Include}
\begin{itemize}
    \item Context and contention
    \item Details (writer, publication, date and text type)
    \item Major persuasive devices
    \item Tone (consistant or shifting)
    \item Intended audience and how you know this
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Revise}
A common mistake students make is that they turn their introductions into lists of everything they need to include. Remember to express yourself clearly, professionally and with style. This is the introduction to your analysiss and sets your assesor's expectations for the rest of the piece.

\subsubsection{Sentence structure}
The first part of your introduction should focus on what issue is being discussed and why it is important. This will help you to create an opening to demonstrate a strong understanding of the article, for example:
\begin{quote}
    The current trend of dangerous Tik-Tok challenges has once again sparked debate over whether we should be restricting access to the wildly popular app. 
\end{quote}

If we break it down it becomes:
\begin{itemize}
    \item The current trend - \textbf{Phrase of recency}
    \item of dangerous Tik-tok challenges - \textbf{Incident}
    \item has once again sparked - \textbf{Verb phrase}
    \item debate - \textbf{Issue}
\end{itemize}

Here are some sample sentence builders:

\begin{longtable}{|p{2.5cm}|p{3cm}|p{2.25cm}|}
\caption{Framing Contemporary Issues in Writing: Phrases of Recency, Cause, and Contention} \label{tab:recencyphrases} \\
\hline
\textbf{Phrases of Recency} & \textbf{Verb Phrases} & \textbf{Issues} \\
\hline
\endfirsthead

\hline
\textbf{Phrases of Recency} & \textbf{Verb Phrases} & \textbf{Issues} \\
\hline
\endhead

\hline
\endfoot

The latest...         & ...has once again raised... & issue \\
The present...        & ...has sparked...           & debate \\
The recent...         & ...has led to...            & argument \\
The earlier...        & ...has motivated...         & discussions \\
The current...        & ...has caused...            & disputes \\
Once again...         & ...has generated...         & altercation \\
Recently...           & ...has engendered...        & discourse \\
Lately...             & ...has provoked...          & consultation \\
Over the past year... & ...has propagated...        & dialogue \\
\hline

\end{longtable}

\subsubsection{Key Information Phrase}
In this section we should include key information that is usually found in the background information section of the text, including:
\begin{itemize}
    \item Author's name
    \item Connection of author to text
    \item The form of the piece, the date and where it was published
    \item How this links back to the issue you identified in your first sentence
\end{itemize}


For example, this sentence can look like:
\begin{quote}
    Dr Marko Shellius, CEO of Psychiatric Safe Australia, has written a submission to the parliamentary inquiry into social media, in particular addressing Tik-Tok’s burgeoning influence over Australian teens. 
\end{quote}

If we break it down, it looks like this:
\begin{longtable}{|p{2cm}|p{2.5cm}|p{2cm}|p{2.5cm}|}
\caption{Authorial Context and Publication Framing for Argument Analysis} \label{tab:authorcontext} \\
\hline
\textbf{Author} & \textbf{Connection of Author to the Issue} & \textbf{Form and Publisher} & \textbf{Link Back to the Issue} \\
\hline
\endfirsthead

\hline
\textbf{Author} & \textbf{Connection of Author to the Issue} & \textbf{Form and Publisher} & \textbf{Link Back to the Issue} \\
\hline
\endhead

\hline
\endfoot

Dr Marko Shellius & CEO of Psychiatric Safe Australia & Has written a submission to the parliamentary inquiry on social media & In particular addressing TikTok’s burgeoning influence over Australian teens \\
\hline

\end{longtable}


This order can also be moved around as well:

\begin{longtable}{|p{2.75cm}|p{2cm}|p{2.75cm}|p{2.75cm}|}
\caption{Form, Author, and Contextual Framing of the Issue} \label{tab:formauthorcontext} \\
\hline
\textbf{Form and Publisher} & \textbf{Author} & \textbf{Connection of Author to the Issue} & \textbf{Link Back to the Issue} \\
\hline
\endfirsthead

\hline
\textbf{Form and Publisher} & \textbf{Author} & \textbf{Connection of Author to the Issue} & \textbf{Link Back to the Issue} \\
\hline
\endhead

\hline
\endfoot

In a written submission to the parliamentary inquiry into social media, & Dr Marko Shellius & CEO of Psychiatric Safe Australia & Addresses TikTok’s burgeoning influence over Australian teens \\
\hline

\end{longtable}

Here are some sentence starters to help build these sentences:
\begin{longtable}{|p{2.5cm}|p{3cm}|p{2.5cm}|}
\caption{Author Connection, Form of Publication, and Issue Framing Phrases} \label{tab:authorformissue} \\
\hline
\textbf{Connection of Author to Issue} & \textbf{Form and Location} & \textbf{Link to Issue} \\
\hline
\endfirsthead

\hline
\textbf{Connection of Author to Issue} & \textbf{Form and Location} & \textbf{Link to Issue} \\
\hline
\endhead

\hline
\endfoot

Chief … of…                & has written in/on…            & in response to… \\
Leader of…                 & has spoken at/in…             & addressing… \\
Instigator…                & a blog published…             & about… \\
a writer…                  & an opinion piece published…   & concerning… \\
an opponent of…            & a… newsletter                 & regarding… \\
a supporter of…            & a speech presented to…        & encircling… \\
a member of…               & a debate in front of…         & surrounding… \\
a key…                     & published a…                  & acknowledging… \\
an important…              & created a…                    & responding to… \\
one of the…                &                               & confessing… \\
\hline

\end{longtable}


\subsubsection{Tones}
Analysing argument involves understanding how authors use language to persuade their audience. This includes identifying the author's tone, which refers to the overall feeling or attitude conveyed in the text. Recognising shifts in tone is crucial as it often accompanies changes in argument strategies. Authors use tone to appeal to the reader's emotions, establish credibility, and guide the reader's interpretation of the argument.\\

This is the perfect place for you to show the breadth of your vocabulary. Choose a specific word that relates to the article. Always avoid using the terms 'Positive', 'Negative' and 'Neutral'.''

\begin{longtable}{|p{2cm}|p{2cm}|p{2cm}|p{2cm}|p{2cm}|}
\caption{Positive Tone Vocabulary Bank} \label{tab:positivetonewords} \\
\hline
\hline
\endfirsthead

\hline
\hline
\endhead

\hline
\endfoot

 &  & Promising & Romantic & Humourous \\

\hline

 &  & Uneasy & Quarrelsome \\
 &  & Vulnerable & Sarcastic \\

 &  &  & Vexed \\

\subsubsection{Audience}
The audience refers to anyone who is viewing, reading or listening to a text.\\

When writing an analysing argument essay, it isn’t enough to just say your audience is ‘everyone’ or ‘all Australians’. You need to refine your idea of who you are writing for!\\

Three questions to ask yourself
\begin{itemize}
    \item What is the author writing about?
    \item Who would be interested in this topic?
    \item Is there more than one audience who might be interested in this?
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure} [h!]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{image.png}
    \caption{Refining Audience}
    \label{fig:enter-label}
\end{figure}

\textit{The key take-away is that you need to be as specific as possible when stating the intended audience.}


\newpage
\subsection{Body Paragraphs}
\subsubsection{What you are trying to achieve}
Each body paragraph should explore, in sequence, the major arguments that the author uses to support their contention. Remember you are being assessed on your ability to analyse the arguments the author uses and how the language they have chosen supports these.

\subsubsection{Step 1: What?}
Outline what the author is arguing in your own words. Make a comment on structure; if this is the first or final argument then what is the reason the author introduced this at this time.
\subsubsection{Step 2: How?}
Begin by identifying the persuasive language features that the author uses to substantiate their argument, include a quote illustrating your point (but no more than 5 words). But this is not enough, then you need to analyse the effectiveness of this approach and discuss how it positions the audience. If there is an image associated with this argument, you should talk about it here - treat it like a persuasive device. Depending on the argument, you may want to talk about how a group of persuasive devices work together.\\

\textit{Note: Try to avoid repetition by constantly stating 'The author uses X which makes the audience feel Y.} This can come across tacky and quite robotic. To see examples of how this can be done in a successful way, look at the three high-scoring essays below.\\

\subsubsection{Step 3: Why?}
Finally, you need to infer why the author chose this approach, what is their persuasive intent? How would it appeal to their particular audience? How does it impact the tone of the article? How does this support their contention?
\subsubsection{Step 4: Examine (Optional)}
Does the tone change for this argument? Does the structure change? How does this position the audience?

\newpage

\subsection{Conclusion}
\subsubsection{What you are trying to achieve}
In your conclusion, you are exploring both how the text concludes and wrapping up your analysis at the same time. It is an opportunity to discuss the text as a whole, and emphasises your analytical skills.

\subsubsection{Include}
\begin{itemize}
    \item Comment on the structure of the article, including whether there is a consistent or changing tone of writing.
    \item Focus on the last paragraph of the article, evaluating how it supports the writer's contention.
    \item Evaluate whether the article developed more trust in the readers and explain why.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Note}
Some students find they don't have time for a conclusion. This isn't ideal, but it isn't the end of the world, as most of this information should have been explicit throughout. If you are running short on time, a sentence summarising how each argument leads to the  contention is sufficient.
\newpage


\subsection{Complex Sentence and Paragraph Building}

When analysing persuasive texts, it’s not enough to simply spot techniques—you need to explain what is happening, why it’s there, and how it affects the audience.\\

Students often start with short, seperate sentences:\\
\textit{'The writer uses emotive language. This makes the audience feel sympathy'}\\

A stronger version combines ideas into one complex sentence:\\
\textit{‘Describing the victims as “cruelly abandoned” evokes a deep sense of sympathy in the audience, prompting them to see the government as neglectful and morally failing in its duty of care.’}\\

\subsubsection{Techniques for Stronger Sentences}
\begin{itemize}
\item Subordinating conjunctions – because, although, while, since, if
\item Embedded quotes – weave quotes into the sentence naturally
\item Relative clauses – who, which, that
\item Appositives – a technique aimed at…
\end{itemize}

By applying these sentence-building tools, your analysis will become more precise, connected, and persuasive—mirroring the sophistication expected in high-level responses.\\

To help you identify and discuss these elements in a clear, structured way, we will now look at a step-by-step framework that breaks persuasive writing into its key components.\\


\subsection{W.A.S.T.E.: F.A.T.!}
This acronym is designed to assist you in crafting complex and sophisticated sentences that strengthen your analytical writing. In higher-scoring essays, writers deliberately weave these elements into their work to add depth, clarity, and precision. While it is not necessary to include every element in every individual sentence, you should aim to integrate all of them across the body of your essay. This means that, by the end of each body paragraph, the reader should be able to identify how you have employed these elements to develop your argument, enhance your expression, and engage the audience effectively. Consistent and purposeful use of these techniques will not only improve sentence complexity but also demonstrate a mature and well-structured writing style.\\

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{W}ords – Focus on the most persuasive words or phrases of the piece.
    \item \textbf{A}rgument – What is the supporting argument of each section?
    \item \textbf{S}tructure – Where is this located in the piece and why?
    \item \textbf{T}one – How does the author apply tone to be more persuasive in each section?
    \item \textbf{E}ffect upon the audience – How does it position the audience to:
    \begin{itemize}
        \item \textbf{F}eel?
        \item \textbf{A}ct?
        \item \textbf{T}hink?
    \end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{W.A.S.T.E.: F.A.T. Medium Example}


ZZEXP W.A.S.T.E.: F.A.T. Medium Scoring Example ZZEXPEND


\textit{The editorial uses emotionally charged words such as “climate catastrophe”, “tipping point”, and “irreversible damage” to stress the seriousness of the issue. The supporting argument is that urgent government action is necessary to prevent catastrophic environmental collapse. These phrases appear in the introduction so that the reader is immediately confronted with the scale of the problem. The author adopts an alarmist and urgent tone to heighten concern and prompt immediate attention. This positions the audience to feel fear and anxiety about the future, to act by pressuring politicians or signing petitions, and to think that if society does not act now, it will be too late.}

\subsubsection{W.A.S.T.E.: F.A.T. High Example}


ZZEXP W.A.S.T.E.: F.A.T. High Scoring Example ZZEXPEND


\textit{The editorial uses emotive phrases such as “climate catastrophe”, “tipping point”, and “irreversible damage” in its introduction to immediately confront the reader with the scale of the problem. By presenting the argument that urgent government action is needed to prevent environmental collapse, the writer establishes the issue as both pressing and unavoidable. Through an alarmist and urgent tone, the piece positions the audience to feel fear, act by pressuring politicians or signing petitions, and think that immediate intervention is essential.}

\subsection{Fill-in-the-blanks Analysing Argument Template for an Introduction}

Your introduction should discuss the writer’s contention and intention, the specific audience, the
form, and the publication details, as well as the context of the issue. You might like to make a
comment about the tone of the piece in the introduction.\\

Here is a sample introduction structure:\\
The issue of ….. (issue) was addressed in a ….. (text type) for ….. (publication) on ….. (date). …..
(Author) contends, in a ….. (tonal word) and at times ….. (tonal word) voice that ….. (contention).
The piece is predominantly ….. (words about overall structure or style) in style, and seeks to
position its ….. (audience) to accept ….. (intention).\\

Another sample introduction structure could look like this:\\
Following ….. (context), debate resurfaced regarding ….. (issue). In a ….. (text type) for …..
(publication) on ….. (date), ….. (author) argues in a ….. (tonal word) and ….. (tonal word) fashion
that ….. (contention), and seeks to ….. (intention). The piece, targeted at ….. (audience), ….. .\\

For example:\\
In the wake of the recent sentencing of murderer John Smith, Sarah Farah’s letter to the editor of
The Daily Telegraph (9 June 2014), raises the issue of perceived injustice in the treatment of
Australian citizens. In an aggressive tone, she contends that the penalty imposed on this man was
too harsh and that such punishment represents a form of anti Australian discrimination. Thus, she
hopes to prompt the Australian Government to take action to protect its citizens.\\



\section{Analysing Written Language}
\subsection{Terms and Strategies}

\subsubsection{Specific Word Choice (word connotation) }
Writers choose particular words to provoke an emotional reaction in the reader or emphasise an idea.\\

For example, if writing about abortion, the author may choose;
"I read with disgust that the government is to ...
kill, slay, slaughter, murder, butcher, exterminate, destroy, sacrifice, execute ...
Emotive language is language that is not logical or rational - it reflects and expresses an emotional or subjective reaction. Such words help create mood. Many words have connotations or associations that the writer uses with deliberate intent to create a desired effect in the reader. Words may have positive or negative connotations. They may evoke intense imagery. You will be expected to discuss some targeted words and their relative connotations in your language analysis piece.\\


\subsubsection{Particular language structure and features}
Inclusive language (we, our) may be used to give the audience a sense of being part of a wider
community of thought/action.\\

Modifiers and qualifiers are words used to denote a level of impact. There is a difference
between a writer choosing to use 'some' instead of 'all' or 'most' instead of 'many'. Phrases
such as 'very good' or 'basically good' also suggest different meaning to the reader. Students
analysing language need to consider why specific lexical strings have been chosen by the writer
as a form of persuasion.\\

\subsubsection{Euphemisms}
Euphemisms are words sometimes used to soften a more accurate but seemingly more offensive
word or phrase. For example, euphemisms for 'die' include; passed away, lose one's life, kick
the bucket, croak. A company may say they are 'downsizing' instead offiring workers from the
job.

\subsubsection{Hypothetical Scenarios}
Hypothetical scenarios are used to prompt the reader to imagine possible outcomes, often beginning with phrases like \textit{“Imagine if...”}. These speculative examples draw attention to the consequences of actions or inactions, even if they are not real or likely. Writers use this technique to provoke reflection and emotional investment from the reader.\\

For example: \textit{“Imagine if the world was ruled by tyrants who dominated our every thought daily...”}\\

\subsubsection{Repetition}
Repetition involves deliberately repeating words or phrases for emphasis and to make an idea more memorable.\\

\textbf{Anaphora:} repetition at the start of successive clauses.\\
\textit{e.g. School is distracting. School is boring. School sucks.}\\
\textbf{Epistrophe:} repetition at the end of clauses.\\
\textit{e.g. Follow the rules. Obey the rules. These are the rules.}\\

\subsubsection{Puns}
Puns are humorous wordplays based on similar sounds or multiple meanings. They are often used to lighten tone or ridicule opposing views. Some puns also carry double meanings, especially in persuasive or satirical writing.\\

\textit{e.g. “Time flies like an arrow; fruit flies like a banana.”}\\

\subsubsection{Irony}
Irony occurs when the intended meaning of a statement contrasts with its literal meaning. Writers often use irony to highlight absurdities or criticise ideas indirectly.\\

\textit{e.g. “Lovely day,” said during a storm.}\\

\subsubsection{Sarcasm}
Sarcasm is a biting, mocking form of expression usually intended to hurt, ridicule, or convey contempt. It often overlaps with irony but has a more cutting tone.\\

\textit{e.g. “Nice face. Shame about the rest.”}\\

\subsubsection{Exaggeration / Hyperbole}
This technique involves overstating a point for dramatic effect. It can be used to emphasise importance or provoke an emotional response.\\

\textit{e.g. “I’d rather cut off my arm than go back to work under those conditions!”}\\

\subsubsection{Humour}
Humour engages the reader by entertaining them. It can also make controversial ideas more palatable or draw attention to flaws in opposing arguments.\\

It is often used as a non-threatening way to critique.\\

\subsubsection{Satire}
Satire combines humour and criticism to ridicule individuals, institutions, or ideas. It often exposes hypocrisy or absurdity, making serious points in a humorous way.

\textit{e.g. A TV show like \textit{Frontline} that uses mock-journalism to critique the media.}\\

\subsubsection{Oxymoron}
An oxymoron combines two contradictory terms to highlight contrast or provoke thought. It is often used to emphasise complexity or irony.\\

\textit{e.g. deafening silence, wise fool, bitter sweet}\\

\subsubsection{Assonance}
Assonance is the repetition of vowel sounds within words or phrases to create rhythm, mood, or emphasis. Often used poetically or in slogans.\\

\textit{e.g. “cool, calm and collected”}\\

\subsubsection{Simile}
A simile compares one thing with another using the words \textit{‘like’} or \textit{‘as’}. It helps readers understand unfamiliar concepts by likening them to familiar ones, often to create vivid imagery.\\

\textit{e.g. My bedroom is like a pigsty. She is as skinny as a rake.}\\

\subsubsection{Metaphor}
A metaphor is an implied comparison that states one thing is another, thereby transferring qualities from one to the other. Metaphors can make writing more persuasive by enhancing imagery and emotional appeal.\\

\textit{e.g. I stepped into my pigsty, shut the door and climbed into bed. He is a pillar of strength.}\\

\subsubsection{Rhetorical Questions}
These are questions posed for effect rather than to elicit an answer. They imply that the answer is self-evident, guiding the audience to agree with the writer’s point.\\

\textit{e.g. Do you want to see your wife and children out on the streets in rags? Of course you don’t!}\\

\subsubsection{Scapegoats}
A scapegoat is a person or group blamed for something they are not truly responsible for. This technique simplifies complex issues and focuses the reader’s anger on a convenient target.\\

\textit{e.g. All our crime and unemployment problems are because of the government.}\\

\subsubsection{Assumptions}
Assumptions involve treating beliefs or opinions as facts without proof. These are often subtle and can influence readers by appealing to what is generally believed to be true.\\

\textit{e.g. He must be smart because he is on the computer every day.}\\

\subsubsection{Presumptions}
Presumptions occur when statements are accepted as truths without critical examination. They appeal to common beliefs or cultural norms.\\

\textit{e.g. If it’s natural, it’s good for you.}\\

\subsubsection{Analogy}
An analogy compares two things that are alike in some respects to clarify a point or persuade readers that what applies to one must apply to the other.\\

\textit{e.g. If birds can fly because they have wings, then maybe if we develop mechanical wings we can fly too.}\\
\textit{e.g. The human brain is like a computer.}\\

\subsubsection{Expert Opinion}
This technique involves citing someone perceived to have authority or expertise on a topic. It lends credibility to an argument and can be persuasive even if the “expert” is unnamed.\\

\textit{e.g. In the thirty-five years I was Head Surgeon at...}\\

\subsubsection{Case Studies and Anecdotes}
Case studies and anecdotes are used to personalise or humanise an argument. Anecdotes are usually emotive and personal; case studies more formal and factual. Both aim to build credibility and emotional appeal.\\

\textit{e.g. Pool fencing should be mandatory because my neighbour’s child drowned in an unfenced pool.}\\

\subsubsection{Empty Truths}
Also known as tautologies, these are statements that repeat the same idea unnecessarily, often sounding meaningful but lacking substance.\\

\textit{e.g. Boys will be boys. Enough is enough. Business is business.}\\

\subsubsection{Cause and Effect}
This technique suggests that one event directly causes another. It is persuasive because it offers logical explanation, though causality may not always be valid.\\

\textit{e.g. Peter crashed his car. Peter died.}\\

\subsubsection{Ends and Means / Means to an End}
This approach justifies an action based on the outcome, even if the action itself is questionable.\\
\textit{e.g. A criminal donates to a playground; the donation is good, but the source of funds is illegal.}\\

\subsubsection{Consequence of a Fact}
This presents a pragmatic response to an existing situation by arguing that something should be done simply because a condition exists.\\

\textit{e.g. Nuclear power exists, so we might as well use it.}\\

\subsubsection{Judging an Action by its Consequences}
This technique justifies or condemns an action based on its results. It focuses on outcomes rather than intent.\\

\textit{e.g. Stealing from the rich to give to the poor is acceptable because the poor are hungry.}\\

\subsubsection{Sweeping Statements / Generalisations}
These make broad claims that apply to a group without considering individual differences. Generalisations can sound convincing but often oversimplify.\\

\textit{e.g. All teenagers are irresponsible.}\\

\subsubsection{Cliché}
Clichés are overused phrases that offer familiar shortcuts to meaning. Though persuasive due to their recognisability, they can also weaken writing if not used purposefully.\\

\textit{e.g. What goes around comes around.}\\

\subsubsection{Co-existence}
This assumes an automatic relationship between ideas or traits, such as assuming someone is good simply because they attend church.\\

\textit{e.g. She goes to the local church, so she must be a good person.}\\

\subsubsection{Guilt by Association}
This technique links people, ideas or groups with undesirable individuals or arguments in order to discredit them.\\

\textit{e.g. Premier Kennett has behaved like Hitler since coming to power.}\\

\subsubsection{Questioning Motive}
This involves undermining an argument by questioning the motives of the speaker, diverting attention away from the argument itself.\\

\textit{e.g. The Health Minister’s decision to close the hospital is just an excuse to build a monument to himself.}\\

\subsubsection{Figures and Statistics}
Numbers and data are used to give the illusion of objectivity and reliability. However, the reader must critically assess source, sample size, relevance, and interpretation.\\

\textit{e.g. 300 people died on our roads last year...}\\

\subsubsection{Discredit Another Argument}
This technique aims to dismantle opposing viewpoints by exposing flaws or inconsistencies in them. It draws attention away from the original argument and weakens the opposing side’s credibility.\\

\subsubsection{Attack}
This technique involves criticising or mocking opposing arguments or individuals. It is designed to weaken the opposition's credibility and make their position less persuasive. Writers may use name-calling, ridicule, or other personal attacks to influence the audience’s perception.\\
One form of attack is labelling the opposition in an attempt to belittle them.

\subsubsection{Flattery and Praise}
This technique involves listing individuals or groups who agree with the writer’s position in a positive light. By using terms that convey admiration or respect, the writer bolsters the authority of their own argument.\\

\textit{e.g. “Leading scientists support this research.”}

\subsubsection{Use of Punctuation and Printing Style}
Writers use punctuation and formatting deliberately to add emphasis, create subtext, or manipulate tone and flow.\\
Common strategies include:
\begin{itemize}
  \item Inverted commas – suggest doubt or irony, even when quoting.
  \item Exclamation marks – show emotional emphasis or urgency.
  \item Dashes – indicate pauses, hesitation or parenthetical ideas.
  \item Long sentences – may signal sophistication or rambling.
  \item Short sentences – create punch, pace and immediacy.
  \item Italics, CAPITALISATION, \textbf{bold}, \underline{underlining} – all used for emphasis.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Vocabulary Use}
The choice of vocabulary significantly affects tone and credibility. Writers use sophisticated or precise word choice to sound more authoritative or to target specific reader emotions. Students should be aware of:
\begin{itemize}
  \item Emotive connotations (e.g. \textit{slaughter} vs \textit{kill})
  \item Word strength and subtlety (e.g. \textit{sporadic} vs \textit{occasional})
  \item Replacing vague or weak terms with more accurate or academic language.
\end{itemize}
Improving vocabulary can enhance clarity and persuasiveness. For example:
\begin{itemize}
  \item Replace “straight away” with “immediately”
  \item Replace “in the next paragraph” with “in the following section”
  \item Replace “always” with “consistently”
\end{itemize}

\newpage
\subsection{Table of Common Persuasive Techniques}

\begin{center}
\textbf{\LARGE Persuasive Techniques Reference Table}
\end{center}

\begin{longtable}{|p{2cm}|p{3.25cm}|p{3.25cm}|}
\hline
\textbf{Persuasive Technique} & \textbf{Definition} & \textbf{How It Persuades} \\
\hline
\endfirsthead

\hline
\textbf{Persuasive Technique} & \textbf{Definition} & \textbf{How It Persuades} \\
\hline
\endhead

Adjective & Describing words usually connected to a noun (naming word). \newline e.g. \textit{The bright red house} (bright and red = adjectives; house = noun) & Adds emphasis. \\
\hline
Alliteration & Repeating and playing upon the same letter. \newline e.g. \textit{A back-breaking job...} & Adds emphasis and reinforces meaning, especially where an idea is repeated. Often used to create a highly emotive image. \\
\hline
Anecdote & A short account or story of an entertaining or interesting incident. \newline e.g. \textit{In my experience...} & Usually makes reader sympathetic and receptive to the point; can set up a character, then position reader to accept or reject that individual character. \\
\hline
Associations & Words that create meanings beyond the literal meaning of the word. \newline e.g. \textit{attractive, alluring, pleasing, charming} & Suggests or influences reader response in certain ways, often subconsciously. Implied meanings need to be identified. \\
\hline
Attacks/praise & The writer attacks or praises an opponent or idea. This is really an assertion of the writer's viewpoint. \newline e.g. \textit{denigrating or humiliating the person, discrediting opponents as unreliable, dishonest, suggesting unsatisfactory associations} & Often effective in placing the reader in a position of agreement with the writer/speaker through forceful language and insistent claims, even without evidence. \\
\hline
Bias & One-sidedness in presentation of view/opinion. \newline e.g. \textit{Eminem’s tour promoter obviously sees no danger in what Eminem says.} & Can subjectively influence the reader by intentionally only presenting one side of the argument. \\
\hline
Clichés & Worn out, overused expressions. \newline e.g. \textit{Fit as a fiddle, Turn over a new leaf, Pushing the envelope} & Are familiar, often colloquial, so can offer a shortcut to convey meaning. \\
\hline
Colourful words and descriptive language & Words that are heightened, vivid, lively, and full of interest. \newline e.g. \textit{Hot = blistering, sultry, muggy, suffocating...} & Produce a picture and/or induce an emotion. Engage reader by gaining attention and often put a new slant on familiar events and issues. \\
\hline
Contention & The main line of argument in a text. A statement of the main idea being argued or debated. Focuses on WHAT is being argued. & Pinpoints the issue and the writer’s point of view. Helps the reader identify the issue and evaluate the techniques used to persuade them to agree. \\
\hline
Emotional Appeals & Play on people's emotions such as fears, insecurities, hopes, desires, and values. \newline Can target: \newline • A sense of justice or injustice \newline • Family values \newline • Tradition or custom \newline • Patriotism \newline • Moral values & Frequently appeal to our values and attitudes, sometimes subtly. Can manipulate reader to take notice of issues by triggering emotional responses. \\
\hline
Emotive Language & The deliberate use of strong emotive words to play on reader’s feelings. \newline e.g. \textit{sleazy, slimy, vicious, disgusting, outrageous} & Evokes strong emotional response to coerce or force agreement. Used to stimulate strong emotional reactions and manipulate responses. \\
\hline
Loaded Words (Labelling) & Words that are loaded with associations. \newline e.g. \textit{capitalist, communist, salesman} & Shorthand way of discrediting or praising by suggesting associations. Exploits emotional baggage of such words to sway opinion. \\
\hline
Repetition & Repeated words, phrases, sentence patterns, ideas. & Gives emphasis and prominence to a point or idea; reinforces and makes reader remember the point. \\
\hline
Rhetorical Questions & Questions that have the answer embedded in them; often use irony. & Manipulate the reader to agree because the answer is assumed. Disagreement would mean dismissing a point that commands agreement. \\
\hline
Tone & Refers to the voice of the writer; the writer's attitude to the subject and reader. Tone can be emotive. \newline e.g. \textit{angry, sympathetic, sarcastic} & Reflects writer's attitude, which can influence the reader to agree or reject. Tone can signal a new direction or perspective in the writing. \\
\hline

\end{longtable}

\section{Analysing Visual Language}
Many students find the analysis of images an accessible and enjoyable part of Year 12 English. It is important, however, to prepare for a variety of visual elements that may appear on your Unit 3 SAC task on analysing argument, as well as on Section C of the end-of-year examination. Below are some elements to keep an eye out for.

\subsection{Context and Position}
In order to fully understand the meaning of the visual elements in a text, you need to understand the context. Be sure to read any background information accompanying the task so that you know what the main issue is. Both the background information and the written material will help give you a sense of the visual’s purpose.\\

Remember that the focus of Area of Study 2 in the Unit 3 SAC and in Section C of the exam is on the analysis of argument. Noticing where and why a visual element is placed in the written piece can help you to tackle the idea of how the argument is structured.\\

\begin{itemize}
\item Is the visual at the start of the article to elicit empathy or shock?
\item Is it in the middle to reinforce a particular piece of evidence or reason?
\item Is it at the end to consolidate the argument as a whole?
\item Is it a frame or border to the written piece that contributes to the overall approach and tone?
\item Alternatively, if the visual is a completely separate text from the written piece, does it present the same or a different point of view on the issue?
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Ask yourself:} where is the visual positioned and why does it matter?

\subsection{Colour and Design Elements}
Although the task material for Section C of the exam will be printed in black and white, your Unit 3 SAC might well contain colour images. If this is the case, you can certainly explore the mood that is created by the use of colour, as well as any relevant connotations of the colour scheme. In a black-and-white image it is possible to discuss degrees of shading and how these affect meaning.\\

It is important to remember that visual language can pertain to design elements in the text you are analysing. For example, in the task material for Section C of the VCAA sample exam the speaker’s first slide has illustrations incorporated into the year ‘2010’. The embedded images of fish, a flamingo, waves, a tree, and an adult and child holding hands help to promote the speaker’s arguments about the beauty of biodiversity and humanity’s central involvement in its protection.\\

Also be alert to the design elements of internet text types such as web pages and blogs. You can, for example, discuss the use of icons, how they position the viewer and the symbolism embedded in them.\\

\textbf{Ask yourself:} how do design elements, colour and/or shading of the visual contribute to its meaning?

\begin{figure} [h!]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=1\linewidth]{Screenshot 2025-07-24 105039.png}
    \caption{\textit{Insight Example Image Analysis}}
    \label{fig:enter-label}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Cartoons}
Cartoons often accompany an opinion piece; they can also be stand-alone texts offering a viewpoint on a current issue – especially one that involves politicians or high-profile figures. To understand a cartoon it can be important to have an understanding of the issues circulating in the media in any given week. As I write, there are two quite different cartoons in The Age and The Australian that contain an image of a whiteboard, among other elements. To make sense of both cartoons, a reader would need to know that earlier in the week security guards in Parliament House used a whiteboard to shield a politician from photographers and journalists. Without this knowledge it would be difficult, if not impossible, to interpret the cartoons. Try to stay informed about current affairs as you practise analysing media texts, so that you can grasp their full meanings.\\

Cartoons tend to exaggerate the features of those they represent and use minimal – often quite pithy – text. Be mindful of where this text is: does it appear in a speech bubble or balloon and therefore represent a person’s thoughts or speech? Or is the text elsewhere – on a sign, say, or framed as a caption – providing additional context or comment?\\

The tone of a cartoon is an important feature to note. Political cartoons in particular are often sarcastic and position the reader to be critical of politicians and/or their views and policies.\\

\textbf{Ask yourself:} how does the cartoon make me feel? Your response will give you a guide to the tone of this visual.

\subsection{Photographs}
Photographs can provide a wealth of material for analysis. Look for a range of elements, including:

\begin{itemize}
\item whether the shot is low-angle (looking up at the subject), high-angle (looking down on the subject) or straight-on – for example, a low-angle shot of a child might give the impression that they have been able to overcome adversity and achieve success, whereas a high-angle shot might suggest that a person is vulnerable or remorseful
\item whether the subject is in the foreground (making them very prominent) or background (emphasising their context)
\item whether elements of the photograph are blurred or in soft focus
\item whether a filter (e.g. warm or cool) is used
\item where the horizon is placed in a landscape shot (e.g. far away can signal isolation or opportunity)
\item what is actually in the frame (mise en scène) and what might have been left out
\item whether a text overlay has been used and how that frames your reading of the photograph.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Ask yourself:}What would the impact be if I changed an element of the photograph (e.g. camera angle or focus)? Answering this question will give you better insight into what the photograph is intending to achieve.


\subsection{Charts and Diagrams}
Charts and diagrams containing numerical information can be used to present evidence. Although you are not in a position to question the data, you can explore how the information and its visual presentation are used to strengthen and develop an argument. It is good to watch for:
\begin{itemize}
\item what the visual elements suggest and how they connect to each other
\item whether a background image can be seen beneath a written overlay (e.g. an image of a gun beneath statistics about the number of deaths caused by shooting)
\item whether data is attributed to an authority (such as the Australian Bureau of Statistics).
Sometimes charts and diagrams can look dry and intimidating.
\end{itemize}
\textbf{Ask yourself:}how does the information relate to the written text or texts? Does it support or contradict a written text and, if so, how?

\subsection{Concluding Remarks}
For the Exam, it is imperative that you compare the written and visual material supplied. It is also wise to compare the written and visual texts in the examination. Comparing written and visual material in terms of the arguments they present and the ways in which they position the audience will give your analysis more depth and cohesion, and demonstrate a stronger understanding of each.\\
\begin{figure}[h!]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=1\linewidth]{Screenshot 2025-07-23 161319.png}
    \caption{\textit{Section C image from 2024 VCAA English Exam}}
    \label{fig:enter-label}
\end{figure}
